|x^{i}_{n}-x^{j}_{n}|\to\infty
. Thus we have shown that indeed
\inf_{\beta\in[-\alpha/(2k),\alpha/(2k)]}\inf_{\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}}\mathscr{Q}^{\beta}_{k}u_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{x})>0.

We now claim that there exists
\delta>0
so that for all
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in I^{k}
and
\beta\in[-\alpha/(2k),\alpha/(2k)]
one has that

(2.11)

v_{\mathbf{x}}^{\beta}(\mathbf{y})>\delta\mathbf{1}_{(-\delta,\delta)}\big(y_{i}-y_{j}-(x_{i}-x_{j})\big)-C|\beta|^{4p}-C|\beta|^{2p-1}\mathrm{F_{decay}}\big(\min_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}|y_{i^{\prime}}-y_{j^{\prime}}|\big).

To prove this, note that

\displaystyle|\mathscr{Q}^{\beta}_{k}u_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{y})-\mathscr{Q}^{\beta}_{k}u_{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{y})|

\displaystyle=\bigg|\int_{I^{k}}|a_{i}-a_{j}-(x_{i}-x_{j})|\boldsymbol{q}^{(k)}_{\beta}(\mathbf{y},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{a})-\int_{I^{k}}|a_{i}-a_{j}-(y_{i}-y_{j})|\boldsymbol{q}^{(k)}_{\beta}(\mathbf{y},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{a})\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{I^{k}}\big||a_{i}-a_{j}-(x_{i}-x_{j})|-|a_{i}-a_{j}-(y_{i}-y_{j})|\big|\boldsymbol{q}^{(k)}_{\beta}(\mathbf{y},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{a})

\displaystyle\leq\int_{I^{k}}|x_{i}-x_{j}-(y_{i}-y_{j})|\boldsymbol{q}^{(k)}_{\beta}(\mathbf{y},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{a})=|x_{i}-x_{j}-(y_{i}-y_{j})|.

Recall that
c:=\inf_{\beta\in[-\alpha/(2k),\alpha/(2k)]}\inf_{\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}}\mathscr{Q}^{\beta}_{k}u_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{x})
has already been shown to be strictly positive. Thus for all
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in I^{k}
we have
\mathscr{Q}^{\beta}_{k}u_{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{y})\geq c
, and from the above bound it then follows that
\mathscr{Q}^{\beta}_{k}u_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{y})>2c/3
if
|x_{i}-x_{j}-(y_{i}-y_{j})|<c/3.
Thus we see that
v_{\mathbf{x}}^{\beta}(\mathbf{y})=\mathscr{Q}^{\beta}_{k}u_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{y})-|x_{i}-x_{j}-(y_{i}-y_{j})|>c/3
if
|x_{i}-x_{j}-(y_{i}-y_{j})|<c/3
. Together with Lemma
2.15
, this implies (
2.11
) with
\delta:=c/3.

Step 2.
In this step we are going to find a useful family of martingales and derive a discrete “Tanaka’s formula,” see (
2.13
) below. That formula will be instrumental in the later analysis. We will also derive bounds related to these martingales, specifically (
2.12
), (
2.14
), and (
2.15
) below.

If
I=c\mathbb{Z}
we will henceforth assume without loss of generality that
\delta=c
in (
2.11
), otherwise it may be a smaller value. Let
\mathbf{e}_{i}
denote the standard basis of
\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Equation (
2.11
) easily implies that for all
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},...,x_{k})\in I^{k}
one has

(2.12)

F(|x_{i}-x_{j}|)\leq\inf_{\beta\in[-\alpha/(2k),\alpha/(2k)]}\delta^{-1}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}F(\delta|m|)\big[v^{\beta}_{\delta m(\mathbf{e}_{i}-\mathbf{e}_{j})}(\mathbf{x})+C|\beta|^{4p}+C|\beta|^{2p-1}\mathrm{F_{decay}}\big(\min_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}|x_{i^{\prime}}-x_{j^{\prime}}|\big)\big].

For every
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in I^{k}
, note that
u_{\mathbf{x}}(R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r})-\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}v_{\mathbf{x}}^{\beta}(R^{i}_{s}-R^{j}_{s})
is a
\mathbf{P}^{(\beta,k)}_{\mathbf{y}}
-martingale by Corollary
2.10
. Defining
f_{F},g_{\beta,F}:I^{k}\to\mathbb{R}
by

f_{F}:=\delta^{-1}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}F(\delta|m|)u_{\delta m(\mathbf{e}_{i}-\mathbf{e}_{j})},\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;g_{\beta,F}:=\delta^{-1}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}F(\delta|m|)v^{\beta}_{\delta m(\mathbf{e}_{i}-\mathbf{e}_{j})},

we see that the process

(2.13)

\mathcal{M}_{\beta,F}(r):=-(f_{F}(R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r})-f_{F}(\mathbf{x}))+\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}g_{\beta,F}(R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r})

is a
\mathbf{P}^{(\beta,k)}_{\mathbf{y}}
-martingale (for all
\mathbf{y}\in I^{k})
with
\mathcal{M}_{\beta,F}(0)=0
. Note that
f
is globally Lipchitz, in fact

(2.14)

\displaystyle|f_{F}(\mathbf{x})-f_{F}(\mathbf{y})|

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\delta^{-1}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}F(\delta|m|)\bigg)|x_{i}-x_{j}-(y_{i}-y_{j})|\leq C|x_{i}-x_{j}-(y_{i}-y_{j})|,

where we absorbed all constants in the last bound. We also claim that
g_{\beta,F}
is a globally bounded function.Indeed, Lemma
2.15
gives that
\Gamma:=\sup_{\beta\in[-\alpha/(2k),\alpha/(2k)]}\sup_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in I^{k}}v^{\beta}_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{y})<\infty
, thus we see that

(2.15)

\sup_{\beta\in[-\alpha/(2k),\alpha/(2k)]}\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}}|g_{\beta,F}(\mathbf{x})|\leq\bigg(\delta^{-1}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}F(\delta|m|)\bigg)\cdot\Gamma<\infty.

From here and (
2.14
) one sees that the increments of the martingales
\mathcal{M}_{\beta,F}
have
q^{th}
moments bounded uniformly over
\beta\in[-\alpha/(2k),\alpha/(2k)]
and all times
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, thus by Lemma
2.17
one obtains that

(2.16)

\sup_{\beta\in[-\alpha/(2k),\alpha/(2k)]}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}[|\mathcal{M}_{\beta,F}(r)|^{q}]^{1/q}\leq r^{1/2},

where
C
does not depend on
q
.

Step 3.
In this step we will show an
a priori
bound that the left side of (
2.10
) can actually be bounded above by some multiple of the left side of (
2.9
) plus
C(r-r^{\prime})^{1/2}+C|\beta|^{4p}r,
which will effectively reduce the problem to showing the first bound. This bound is (
2.17
) below.

In (
2.12
), note that
\mathrm{F_{decay}}\big(\min_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}|x_{i^{\prime}}-x_{j^{\prime}}|\big)\leq\sum_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}\mathrm{F_{decay}}(|x_{i^{\prime}}-x_{j^{\prime}}|)
. Furthermore, recall from the beginning of the proof our assumption (without loss of generality) that
F\geq\mathrm{F_{decay}}
, thus
\mathrm{F_{decay}}(|x_{i^{\prime}}-x_{j^{\prime}}|)\leq F(|x_{i^{\prime}}-x_{j^{\prime}}|)
. Thus from (
2.12
) we have the preliminary bound

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}[V^{ij}(F;r)^{q}]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}g_{\beta,F}(R^{i}_{s}-R^{j}_{s})\bigg)^{q}\bigg]^{1/q}+C|\beta|^{4p}r+C|\beta|^{2p-1}\sum_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}[V^{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}(F;r)^{q}]^{1/q}.

Using (
2.13
), (
2.14
), (
2.15
), and (
2.16
), we furthermore have

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}g_{\beta,F}(R^{i}_{s}-R^{j}_{s})\bigg)^{q}\bigg]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}[|\mathcal{M}_{\beta,F}(r)|^{q}]^{1/q}+\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}[|f_{F}(R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r})-f_{F}(\mathbf{x})|^{q}]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq Cr^{1/2}+C\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}[|R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}-(x_{i}-x_{j})|^{q}]^{1/q}.

Combining the last two expressions and summing over all indices
(i,j)
with
1\leq i<j\leq k
, then moving all instances of
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}[V^{ij}(F;r)^{q}]^{1/q}
to the left side of the inequality, yields

\displaystyle\big(1-C|\beta|^{2p-1}\tfrac{1}{2}k(k-1)\big)

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}[V^{ij}(F;r)^{q}]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq Cr^{1/2}+C|\beta|^{4p}r+C\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}[|R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}-(x_{i}-x_{j})|^{q}]^{1/q}.

Now choose
\beta_{0}
small enough so that
C\beta_{0}^{2p-1}\tfrac{1}{2}k(k-1)\leq\frac{1}{2}.
Then after absorbing all constants into some larger one, the last expression yields for
|\beta|\leq\beta_{0}
that

(2.17)

\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}[V^{ij}(F;r)^{q}]^{1/q}\leq Cr^{1/2}+C|\beta|^{4p}r+C\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}[|R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}-(x_{i}-x_{j})|^{q}]^{1/q}.

This bound will be a key step in proving the theorem shortly.

Step 4.
In this step we will obtain a bound that is in a sense converse to (
2.17
). Recall from (
2.7
) that

(2.18)

\bigg|\int_{I^{k}}\big(y_{i}-y_{j}-(x_{i}-x_{j})\big)\boldsymbol{q}^{(k)}_{\beta}(\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})\bigg|\leq C|\beta|^{2p-1}\mathrm{F_{decay}}\big(\min_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}|x_{i^{\prime}}-x_{j^{\prime}}|\big)+|\beta|^{4p}

uniformly over
\beta\in[-\alpha/(2k),\alpha/(2k)]
and
\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}.
Let
\pi_{i}:I^{k}\to I
be the coordinate function
\mathbf{x}\mapsto x_{i}
, and define the process

M^{i,j,\beta}_{r}:=R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}-(x_{i}-x_{j})-\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\big((\mathscr{Q}^{\beta}_{k}-\mathrm{Id})(\pi_{i}-\pi_{j})\big)(\mathbf{R}_{s}).

By Corollary
2.10
, this is a
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}
-martingale for all
\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}
, which should
not
be confused with the Tanaka martingales appearing in (
2.13
), as they are unrelated. Now we can use Lemma
2.17
, because (
2.18
) and Lemma
2.9
show that the increments of this martingale have uniformly bounded
q^{th}
moments. Thus we see that
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}[|M^{i,j,\beta}_{r}|^{q}]^{1/q}\leq Cr^{1/2},
where
C
does not depend on integers
r\geq 0
. Thus we find that

(2.19)

\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}[|R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}-(x_{i}-x_{j})|^{q}]^{1/q}\leq Cr^{1/2}+\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\big((\mathscr{Q}^{\beta}_{k}-\mathrm{Id})(\pi_{i}-\pi_{j})\big)(\mathbf{R}_{s})\bigg)^{q}\bigg]^{1/q}.

Equation (
2.18
) precisely says
|(\mathscr{Q}^{\beta}_{k}-\mathrm{Id})(\pi_{i}-\pi_{j})\big)(\mathbf{R}_{s})|\leq C|\beta|^{2p-1}\mathrm{F_{decay}}\big(\min_{1\leq i<j\leq k}|R^{i}_{s}-R^{j}_{s}|\big)+|\beta|^{4p},
which can be further bounded above by
C|\beta|^{2p-1}\sum_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}\mathrm{F_{decay}}\big(|R^{i^{\prime}}_{s}-R^{j^{\prime}}_{s}|\big)+|\beta|^{4p}.
Using the definition (
2.6
) of the processes
V^{ij}
, this precisely means that

\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\big|\big(\mathscr{Q}^{\beta}_{k}-\mathrm{Id})(\pi_{i}-\pi_{j})\big)(\mathbf{R}_{s})\big|\leq Cr|\beta|^{4p}+C|\beta|^{2p-1}\sum_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}V^{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}(\mathrm{F_{decay}};r).

Plugging that bound into (
2.19
), and recalling
F\geq\mathrm{F_{decay}}
, we obtain

(2.20)

\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}[|R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}-(x_{i}-x_{j})|^{q}]^{1/q}\leq Cr^{1/2}+C|\beta|^{4p}r+C|\beta|^{2p-1}\sum_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}[V^{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}(F,r)^{q}]^{1/q}.

uniformly over
\beta\in[-\alpha/(2k),\alpha/(2k)]
and
\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}.

Step 5.
In this step, we will establish the theorem. Apply (
2.20
) and then (
2.17
) in that order, and one finds that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}[|R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}-(x_{i}-x_{j})|^{q}]^{1/q}\leq Cr^{1/2}+C|\beta|^{4p}r+C|\beta|^{2p-1}\sum_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}[|R^{i^{\prime}}_{r}-R^{j^{\prime}}_{r}-(x_{i}-x_{j})|^{q}]^{1/q}.

Now sum the left side over all indices
(i,j)
with
1\leq i<j\leq k
, and move all instances of
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}[|R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}-(x_{i}-x_{j})|^{q}]^{1/q}
to the left side of the expression, and we obtain

\big(1-\tfrac{1}{2}k(k-1)C|\beta|^{2p-1}\big)\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}[|R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}-(x_{i}-x_{j})|^{q}]^{1/q}\leq Cr^{1/2}+C|\beta|^{4p}r.

Now make
\beta_{0}>0
smaller (if needed) so that
C\cdot\beta_{0}^{2p-1}\cdot\frac{1}{2}k(k-1)\leq 1/2
. Then for
|\beta|\leq\beta_{0}
one obtains from the previous expression (after absorbing constants on the right side into some larger constant) that

\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}[|R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}-(x_{i}-x_{j})|^{q}]^{1/q}\leq Cr^{1/2}+C|\beta|^{4p}r.

This proves (
2.9
), and then (
2.10
) follows immediately from (
2.9
) and (
2.17
).
∎

Remark 2.19
.

Note that (
2.15
) and (
2.14
) actually imply a stronger bound. Specifically if
F:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
is decreasing and satisfies
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}F(k)<\infty
, then uniformly over all
0\leq f\leq F
with
f
decreasing, and uniformly over
r\geq 0
and
\beta\in[-\beta_{0},\beta_{0}]
, one has that

(2.21)

\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}}\;\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}\big[V^{ij}(f;r)^{q}\big]^{1/q}\leq C\bigg(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}f(k)\bigg)\big(r^{1/2}+|\beta|^{4p}r\big).

The proof of this can be copied almost verbatim from Step 2 above, noting that for decreasing
f
one has
\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}f(\delta|m|)\leq 2\delta^{-1}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}f(m)
.

Corollary 2.20
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
T>0
, and
q\geq 1
, and any decreasing function
F:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
with
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}F(k)<\infty
. Consider any sequence
\beta_{N}
in
[-\alpha/(2k),\alpha/(2k)]
such that
|\beta_{N}|\leq MN^{-\frac{1}{4p}}
for some
M>0
. There exists some
C=C(T,k,q,F,M)>0
such that for all
N\geq 1
and
0\leq s\leq t\leq T
with
s,t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
one has

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta_{N},k)}\big[\big|N^{-1/2}\big(R^{i}_{Nt}-R^{j}_{Nt}-(R^{i}_{Ns}-R^{j}_{Ns})\big)\big|^{q}\big]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq C\sqrt{t-s},

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}\big[\big|N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big(V^{ij}(F;Nt)-V^{ij}(F;Ns)\big)\big|^{q}\big]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq C\sqrt{t-s}.

Proof.

This is immediate from (
2.9
) and (
2.10
). The extra term of the form
C|\beta|^{4p}(r-r^{\prime})
on the right side of (
2.9
) and (
2.10
) will contribute
C|t-s|
which can be bounded above by
C_{T}|t-s|^{1/2}
on the bounded time interval
[0,T].

∎

Corollary 2.21
.

Fix arbitrary
\lambda,t>0
and any decreasing function
F:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
with
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}F(k)<\infty
. Consider any sequence
\beta_{N}
in
[-\alpha/(2k),\alpha/(2k)]
such that
|\beta_{N}|\leq MN^{-\frac{1}{4p}}
for some
M>0
. Then

\sup_{N\geq 1}\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta_{N},k)}[e^{\lambda N^{-1/2}V^{ij}(F;Nt)}]<\infty.

Proof.

Note that (
2.10
) easily implies the bound that

(2.22)

\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta_{N},k)}[V^{ij}(F,r)]\leq Cr^{1/2},

uniformly over
N\geq 1
and
r\leq Nt.
Since
V^{ij}
is an additive functional, one may iteratively apply the Markov property to automatically obtain higher moment bounds just from this first moment bound, specifically we will show that

(2.23)

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta_{N},k)}[V^{ij}(F;r)^{m}]\leq C^{m}\sqrt{m!}\cdot r^{m/2},

where
C
is independent of
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
r\leq Nt
. If we can prove (
2.23
), the claim will follow immediately simply by Taylor expanding the exponential, and setting
r=Nt
.

The proof of (
2.23
) follows from a straightforward induction on
m
. The
m=1
case is simply (
2.22
). For the inductive step, let us define
\Omega_{m}
to be the optimal constant so that the left side of (
2.23
) is bounded above by
\Omega_{m}r^{m/2}
uniformly over all
r\geq 0
. We then have

\displaystyle h_{m}(r;\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{s_{1},s_{2},...,s_{m}=0}^{r-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{m}F(|R^{i}_{s_{j}}-R^{j}_{s_{j}}|)\bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq s_{1},s_{2},...,s_{m}<r\\
\mathrm{no\;repeated\;index}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{m}F(|R^{i}_{s_{j}}-R^{j}_{s_{j}}|)\bigg]+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq s_{1},s_{2},...,s_{m}<r\\
\mathrm{repeated\;index}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{m}F(|R^{i}_{s_{j}}-R^{j}_{s_{j}}|)\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq m!\cdot\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}\bigg[\sum_{0\leq s_{1}<...<s_{m}<r}\prod_{j=1}^{m}F(|R^{i}_{s_{j}}-R^{j}_{s_{j}}|)\bigg]+\|F\|_{\infty}h_{m-1}(r;\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=m!\cdot\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}\bigg[\sum_{s_{1}=0}^{r-1}F(|R^{i}_{s_{1}}-R^{j}_{s_{1}}|)\cdot\frac{1}{(m-1)!}h_{m-1}(r-s_{1};\mathbf{R}_{s_{1}})]\bigg]+\|F\|_{\infty}h_{m-1}(r;\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle\leq m\Omega_{m-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}\bigg[\sum_{s_{1}=0}^{r-1}F(|R^{i}_{s_{1}}-R^{j}_{s_{1}}|)\cdot(r-s_{1})^{(m-1)/2}\bigg]+\|F\|_{\infty}h_{m-1}(r;\mathbf{x})

We used the Markov property in the fourth line. Now for the first term on the right side, use summation by parts and we obtain that

\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}F(|R^{i}_{s}-R^{j}_{s}|)\cdot(r-s)^{(m-1)/2}=\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\big[(r-s)^{(m-1)/2}-(r-1-s)^{(m-1)/2}\big]\cdot\bigg(\sum_{t=0}^{s-1}F(|R^{i}_{t}-R^{j}_{t}|)\bigg).

Now note that
(r-s)^{(m-1)/2}-(r-1-s)^{(m-1)/2}
can be bounded above by
C(\frac{m-1}{2})(r-s)^{(m-3)/2}
, then apply the expectation
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}
over the last expression, then use the
m=1
case once again, and we see that the expectation is bounded above by
C(\frac{m-1}{2})\cdot\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}(r-s)^{(m-3)/2}s^{1/2}
which is equal to
r^{m/2}
multiplied by a Riemann sum approximation for
\int_{0}^{1}(1-u)^{(m-3)/2}u^{1/2}du
. Notice that the latter is
O(m^{-3/2})
as
m\to\infty.
This whole discussion yields

\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}\bigg[\sum_{s_{1}=0}^{r-1}F(|R^{i}_{s_{1}}-R^{j}_{s_{1}}|)\cdot(r-s_{1})^{(m-1)/2}\bigg]\leq Cm^{-1/2}r^{m/2}

for some absolute constant
C
.

As for the second term
\|F\|_{\infty}h_{m-1}(r;\mathbf{x})
, we can absorb
\|F\|_{\infty}
into some absolute constant
C
since
F
is just a fixed function. By the inductive hypothesis,
\|F\|_{\infty}h_{m-1}(r;\mathbf{x})
may be bounded above by
C\Omega_{m-1}r^{(m-1)/2}\leq C\Omega_{m-1}r^{m/2}.

The entire above discussion yields the relation
\Omega_{m}\leq Cm^{1/2}\Omega_{m-1}
for some absolute constant
C
independent of
m\in\mathbb{N},
which easily implies (
2.23
).
∎

The next corollary will allow us to control exponential terms of the form appearing in Lemma
2.6
, and will be useful in Section
4
.

Corollary 2.22
.

Let
\mathbf{R}=(\mathbf{R}_{r})_{r\geq 0}
denote the canonical process on
(I^{2k})^{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
. For
\mathbf{x}\in I^{2k}
,
\beta\in[-\alpha/(2k),\alpha/(2k)]
, and
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
define

\mathcal{H}^{2k}(\beta,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{R},r):=\exp\bigg(\sum_{s=1}^{r}\bigg\{\log\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(0,2k)}\big[e^{\beta\sum_{j=1}^{2k}(R^{j}_{s}-R^{j}_{s-1})}\big|\mathcal{F}_{s-1}\big]\;-2k\log M(\beta)\bigg\}\bigg).

Then for all
q\geq 1
and
t\geq 0
we have the bound

\sup_{N\geq 1}\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in I^{2k}}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}},2k)}\big[\sup_{r\leq Nt}\mathcal{H}^{2k}(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{R},r)^{q}\big]<\infty.

Furthermore, let
T>0
. There exists
C>0
such that uniformly over
N\geq 1
,
\mathbf{x}\in I^{2k},
and
s,t\in[0,T]\cap(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})
we have that

\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}},2k)}\big[|\mathcal{H}^{2k}(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{R},Nt)-\mathcal{H}^{2k}(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{R},Ns)|^{q}\big]^{1/q}\leq C\sqrt{t-s}.

Proof.

Let us use the bound

\displaystyle\sup_{r\leq Nt}\mathcal{H}^{2k}(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{R},r)^{q}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\bigg(q\sum_{s=1}^{Nt}\bigg|\log\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(0,2k)}\big[e^{N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}\sum_{j=1}^{2k}(R^{j}_{s}-R^{j}_{s-1})}\big|\mathcal{F}_{s-1}\big]\;-2k\log M(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}})\bigg|\bigg)

\displaystyle=\exp\bigg(q\sum_{s=1}^{Nt}\bigg|\log\int_{I^{k}}e^{N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}(y_{j}-R^{j}_{s-1})}\boldsymbol{p}^{(k)}(\mathbf{R}_{s-1},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})\;-2k\log M(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}})\bigg|\bigg),

where we applied the Markov property in the second line. Using Proposition
2.12
with
m=0
and all
\beta_{i}=N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}
will show that the expression inside the absolute value can be bounded above by

(2.24)

CN^{-1/2}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}\mathrm{F_{decay}}\big(|R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}|\big)+CN^{-1-\frac{1}{4p}},

where
C
is a large constant independent of
N
. An application of Corollary
2.21
will then immediately prove the first bound. The second bound can then be proved by first using the elementary bound
|e^{u}-1|\leq|u|e^{|u|}
, then applying Cauchy-Schwarz, and then applying the first bound in conjunction with Corollary
2.20
(noting the termwise bound (
2.24
) of each summand).
∎

3.
Convergence theorems for the tilted processes

In this section, we will prove various convergence theorems for the tilted Markov processes introduced in Definition
2.8
. These convergence results (specifically Theorem
3.11
below) will build upon the estimates of the previous section, and they will be crucial to proving Theorem
1.4
in later sections. Due to many additional subtleties not present in the previous works
[
DDP23b
,
DDP23a
]
, we will deviate completely from the methods of those papers and derive a new method of studying the tilted measures which leverages the Markov property of the tilted processes proved in Proposition
2.7
(whereas those papers did not use the Markov property).

Proposition 3.1
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
T>0
and
1\leq i<j\leq k
. Recall the measure
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}
as given in Definition
2.8
. Consider any pair of sequences
\beta_{N}\in[-\alpha/(2k),\alpha/(2k)]
and
\mathbf{x}_{N}\in I^{k}
, such that
N^{\frac{1}{4p}}\beta_{N}
remains bounded and
N^{-1/2}\mathbf{x}_{N}\to\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
as
N\to\infty
. Then the rescaled and linearly interpolated processes
\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{i}_{Nt}-R^{j}_{Nt})\big)_{t\in(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})\cap[0,T]}
, viewed as random variables under
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(\beta_{N},k)},
converge in law as
N\to\infty
to a Brownian motion of rate 2 started from
x_{i}-x_{j}
where
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},...,x_{k})
. Convergence occurs with respect to the topology of
C[0,T]
.

We remark that a Brownian motion of rate 2 is defined as a process distributed as
\sqrt{2}\cdot B
for a standard Brownian motion
B
.

Proof.

The tightness of this family of processes has already been established in Corollary
2.20
. Thus we need to identify the limit points. For this we will use Levy’s criterion.

Let
\pi_{i}:I^{k}\to I
be the coordinate function
\mathbf{x}\mapsto x_{i}
, and define the
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta_{N},k)}
-martingales

M^{i,j,\beta}_{r}:=R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}-(x_{i}-x_{j})-\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\big((\mathscr{Q}^{\beta}_{k}-\mathrm{Id})(\pi_{i}-\pi_{j})\big)(\mathbf{R}_{s}).

Recall from (
2.7
) that one has

\displaystyle\sup_{r\leq Nt}|M^{i,j,\beta}_{r}-(R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}-(x_{i}-x_{j}))\big|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s=0}^{Nt}\big|\big((\mathscr{Q}^{\beta}_{k}-\mathrm{Id})(\pi_{i}-\pi_{j})\big)(\mathbf{R}_{s})\big|

\displaystyle\leq C|\beta|^{2p-1}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt}\mathrm{F_{decay}}\big(\min_{1\leq i<j\leq k}|R^{i}_{s}-R^{j}_{s}|\big)+|\beta|^{4p},

\displaystyle\leq C|\beta|^{2p-1}\sum_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt}\mathrm{F_{decay}}\big(|R^{i^{\prime}}_{s}-R^{j^{\prime}}_{s}|\big)+|\beta|^{4p}.

Specialize to the case where
\beta=\beta_{N}
with
|\beta_{N}|\leq CN^{-\frac{1}{4p}}
, and where and
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}_{N}
, then multiply both sides by
N^{-1/2}.
Then using the result of Corollary
2.20
, one finds that the processes

\big(M^{i,j,\beta_{N}}_{Nt}-(R^{i}_{Nt}-R^{j}_{Nt}-(x_{i}^{N}-x_{j}^{N}))\big)_{t\in[0,T]}

converge in probability to 0 in the topology of
C[0,T]
, under the measures
\mathbf{P}^{(\beta_{N},k)}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}.
In fact one has for all
q\geq 1
that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbf{E}^{(\beta_{N},k)}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}\bigg[\sup_{r\leq Nt}|M^{i,j,\beta}_{r}-(R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}-(x_{i}-x_{j}))\big|^{q}\bigg]=0.

Consequently it suffices to show that the family of processes
\big(M^{i,j,\beta_{N}}_{Nt}\big)_{t\in[0,T]}
converge in law to a standard Brownian motion in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
started from the origin, viewed under the measures
\mathbf{P}^{(\beta_{N},k)}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}.
Since martingality is preserved by limit points as long as one has uniform integrability, it is clear from the
q^{th}
moment bounds in the last expression and from Corollary
2.20
that any limit point in
C[0,T]
of the family
\big(M^{i,j,\beta_{N}}_{Nt}\big)_{t\in[0,T]}
must be a martingale under the canonical filtration of
C[0,T]
. Thus we only need to show that the quadratic variation is
2t
.

Define the process

Y_{r}^{i,j,\beta}:=(M_{r}^{i,j,\beta})^{2}-\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}[(M_{s+1}^{i,j,\beta}-M_{s}^{i,j,\beta})^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]

which is also a
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}
martingale. Notice just from the definition of these martingales that

\displaystyle\bigg|2r-\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}[(M_{s+1}^{i,j,\beta}-M_{s}^{i,j,\beta})^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\bigg|2r-\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}\big[\big(R^{i}_{s+1}-R^{j}_{s+1}-(R^{i}_{s}-R^{j}_{s})\big)^{2}\big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\big]\bigg|

\displaystyle\;\;\;+2\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}\bigg[\big|R^{i}_{s+1}-R^{j}_{s+1}-(R^{i}_{s}-R^{j}_{s})\big|\cdot\big|\big((\mathscr{Q}^{\beta}_{k}-\mathrm{Id})(\pi_{i}-\pi_{j})\big)(\mathbf{R}_{s})\big|\bigg|\mathcal{F}_{s}\bigg]

\displaystyle\;\;\;+\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}\bigg[\big|\big((\mathscr{Q}^{\beta}_{k}-\mathrm{Id})(\pi_{i}-\pi_{j})\big)(\mathbf{R}_{s})\big|^{2}\bigg|\mathcal{F}_{s}\bigg]

We claim that all three terms on the right side can be bounded above by
C|\beta|r+C\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\mathrm{F_{decay}}\big(\min_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}|R_{s}^{i^{\prime}}-R_{s}^{j^{\prime}}|\big),
where the constant
C
is uniform over
r\geq 0
and
\beta\in[-\alpha/(2k),\alpha/(2k)].
Indeed, the first term on the right side is given by the absolute value of
\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}g_{\beta}(\mathbf{R}_{s})|
, where the function
g:I^{k}\to\mathbb{R}
is given by

g_{\beta}(\mathbf{x}):=2-\int_{I^{k}}(a_{i}-a_{j}-(x_{i}-x_{j}))^{2}\boldsymbol{q}^{(k)}_{\beta}(x,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{a}).

We expand the square as
(a_{i}-x_{i})^{2}+(a_{j}-x_{j})^{2}-2(a_{i}-x_{i})(a_{j}-x_{j})
, and from Corollary
2.13
one obtains that

|g_{\beta}(\mathbf{x})|\leq|2-2(\mathpzc F(\beta)^{2}-\mathpzc G(\beta))|+C\mathrm{F_{decay}}\big(\min_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}|x_{i^{\prime}}-x_{j^{\prime}}|\big)+C|\beta|^{4p-1}\leq C|\beta|+C\mathrm{F_{decay}}\big(\min_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}|x_{i^{\prime}}-x_{j^{\prime}}|\big).

For the second term on the right hand side, note that
\big|\big((\mathscr{Q}^{\beta}_{k}-\mathrm{Id})(\pi_{i}-\pi_{j})\big)(\mathbf{R}_{s})
is actually
\mathcal{F}_{s}
-measurable, thus it can be pulled out of the conditional expectation. By Lemma
2.9
, the remaining part
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}\bigg[\big|R^{i}_{s+1}-R^{j}_{s+1}-(R^{i}_{s}-R^{j}_{s})\big|\bigg|\mathcal{F}_{s}\bigg]
is deterministically bounded uniformly over
\beta\in[-\alpha/(2k),\alpha/(2k)]
and
\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}
, while the term
\big|\big((\mathscr{Q}^{\beta}_{k}-\mathrm{Id})(\pi_{i}-\pi_{j})\big)(\mathbf{R}_{s})\big|
itself can be bounded from above using (
2.7
), obtaining an upper bound of
C|\beta|^{4p}+C\mathrm{F_{decay}}\big(\min_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}|R_{s}^{i^{\prime}}-R_{s}^{j^{\prime}}|\big).
The third term can also be bounded from above by can be bounded from above using (
2.7
), obtaining the same upper bound of
C|\beta|^{4p}+C\mathrm{F_{decay}}\big(\min_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}|R_{s}^{i^{\prime}}-R_{s}^{j^{\prime}}|\big)
.

Summarizing and specializing to the case where
r=Nt
and
\beta=\beta_{N}
, we see that

\bigg|2t-N^{-1}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta_{N},k)}[(M_{s+1}^{i,j,\beta}-M_{s}^{i,j,\beta})^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]\bigg|\leq CN^{-1}\cdot(Nt|\beta_{N}|)+N^{-1}\sum_{r=0}^{Nt}\mathrm{F_{decay}}\big(\min_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}|R^{i^{\prime}}_{r}-R^{j^{\prime}}_{r}|\big).

The latter tends to
0
in probability under
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(\beta_{N},k)}
in the topology of
C[0,T]
. Indeed from Corollary
2.20
and the elementary bound
e^{-\min_{\ell}u_{\ell}}\leq\sum_{\ell}e^{-u_{\ell}}
we can immediately deduce that

N^{-1}\sum_{r=0}^{Nt}\mathrm{F_{decay}}\big(\min_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}|R^{i^{\prime}}_{r}-R^{j^{\prime}}_{r}|\big)\leq N^{-1}\sum_{r=0}^{Nt}\sum_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}\mathrm{F_{decay}}\big(|R^{i^{\prime}}_{r}-R^{j^{\prime}}_{r}|\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle N\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}0

in probability in the topology of
C[0,T]
under
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(\beta_{N},k)}
. This is because the multiplying factor
N^{-1}
here is converging to 0 faster than the fluctuation exponent
N^{-1/2}
appearing in Corollary
2.20
.

We claim that
(N^{-1}Y^{i,j,\beta_{N}}_{Nt})_{t\geq 0}
is a tight family of processes in
C[0,T]
as
N\to\infty
. Indeed, one easily verifies that
\big(N^{-1}(M^{j,\beta_{N}}_{Nt})^{2}\big)_{t\geq 0}
is tight and satisfies the same
L^{q}
estimates as the processes
\big(N^{-1/2}M^{j,\beta_{N}}_{Nt}\big)_{t\geq 0}
on any compact time interval
t\in[0,T]
, simply because it is the square of a process satisfying such bounds as shown in Steps 2 and 3. On the other hand, by Minkowski’s inequality, we also have that

\displaystyle\bigg\|N^{-1}\sum_{r=Ns}^{Nt}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(\beta_{N},k)}[(M^{i,j,\beta_{N}}_{r+1}-M^{i,j,\beta_{N}}_{r})^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]\bigg\|_{L^{q}(\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(\beta_{N},k)})}

\displaystyle\leq N^{-1}\sum_{r=Ns}^{Nt}\big\|M^{i,j,\beta_{N}}_{r+1}-M^{i,j,\beta_{N}}_{r}\big\|^{1/2}_{L^{2q}(\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(\beta_{N},k)})}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\sup_{N\geq 1}\mathcal{S}_{2q}(M^{i,j,\beta_{N}};\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(\beta_{N},k)})^{1/2}\bigg)|t-s|.

Summarizing these bounds, we have that for each
q\geq 1
we have
E_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(\beta_{N},k)}\big[\big(N^{-1}|Y^{j,\beta_{N}}_{Nt}-Y^{i,j,\beta_{N}}_{Ns}|\big)^{q}\big]^{1/q}\leq C|t-s|^{1/2}
for all
s,t
in a compact interval, thus completing the proof that
(N^{-1}Y^{j,\beta_{N}}_{Nt})_{t\geq 0}
is a tight family of processes in
C[0,T]
as
N\to\infty
.

Thus if we take a joint limit point
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
as
N\to\infty
of the processes
\big(N^{-1/2}M^{i,j,\beta_{N}}_{Nt},N^{-1}Y^{i,j,\beta_{N}}_{Nt}\big)_{t\geq 0}
, then it must hold that
\mathcal{Y}_{t}=\mathcal{X}_{t}^{2}-2t
and moreover both processes are continuous martingales, since martingality is preserved by limit points as long as uniform integrability holds. By Levy’s criterion, we conclude that
\mathcal{X}
must be a Brownian motion of rate 2 starting at
x_{i}-x_{j}
since the starting values of
N^{-1/2}X_{Nt}
converge to
\boldsymbol{x}
, i.e.,
N^{-1/2}\mathbf{x}_{N}\to\boldsymbol{x}
.
∎

While the convergence of the process itself has been established in Proposition
3.1
, it is much more subtle to prove convergence of additive functionals of the process to the local time of the Brownian motion. This will be needed to prove the main results, and this will be the main focus going forward.

3.1.
Analysis of the annealed difference process

In this subsection we will prove some results for the annealed difference process, that is, the Markov chain on
I
with transition density
\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}
as defined in Assumption
1.2
Item (
6
). By Lemma
2.2
this Markov chain equals the averaged law of the difference of two particles in the random environment, that is, the law of the process
R^{i}-R^{j}
under
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}
with
\beta=0
(the untilted case). These untilted results will be crucial in deriving the generalizations to the tilted case later.

Definition 3.2
.

Recall the Markov kernel
\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,A):=\int_{I^{2}}\mathbf{1}_{\{a-b\in A\}}\boldsymbol{p}^{(2)}\big((x,0),(\mathrm{d}a,\mathrm{d}b)\big)
from Assumption
1.2
Item (
6
). We also define the associated Markov operator

(P_{\mathbf{dif}}f)(x):=\int_{I}f(a)\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a),

for all measurable functions
f:I\to\mathbb{R}
for which the integral is absolutely convergent.

Definition 3.3
.

Throughout this subsection, we will let
(X_{r})_{r\geq 0}
denote the canonical process on the space
I^{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
. We will let
\mathbf{P}_{x}
denote the probability measure on
I^{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
given by the law of the Markov chain associated to
\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}
started from
x\in I.

Thus by Lemma
2.2
, the canonical process
(X_{r})_{r\geq 0}
under
\mathbf{P}_{x}
is distributed the same as the process
R^{i}-R^{j}
under
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}
with
\beta=0
and
x_{i}-x_{j}=x.

Proposition 3.4

(Existence of a Foster-Lyapunov drift function)
.

There exists a function
V:I\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
with compact sublevel sets such that
P_{\mathbf{dif}}V(x)\leq V(x)
for all
x
outside some compact interval.

We remark that in some references such as
[
MT93
]
, any function
V
as above would be said to satisfy the
(V1)
drift condition (as opposed to
(V2)
or
(V3)
which will not be relevant in this paper).

Proof.

We set
V(x):=\sqrt{|x|+1}.
We will show that
P_{\mathbf{dif}}V(x)\leq V(x)
for all positive and sufficiently large
x
, and the proof for negative
x
would be completely symmetric.

For
x>2
and
a\in(x/2,3x/2)
we can write

\displaystyle V(a)-V(x)

\displaystyle=V^{\prime}(x)(a-x)+\frac{1}{2}V^{\prime\prime}(x)(a-x)^{2}+R(a,x)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}(x+1)^{-1/2}(a-x)-\frac{1}{4}(x+1)^{-3/2}(a-x)^{2}+R(a,x),

where by Taylor’s remainder theorem one has uniformly over all
x>2
and
a\in(x/2,3x/2)
the bound

|R(a,x)|\leq\frac{1}{6}|a-x|^{3}\sup_{b\in[x/2,3x/2]}|V^{\prime\prime\prime}(b)|\leq\frac{3}{48}(x/2)^{-5/2}|a-x|^{3}.

Now recall that
\int_{I}(a-x)\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a)=0
for all
x\in I
, which is immediate from the definitions, see e.g. Lemma
2.2
. Consequently we can disregard the first-order term when calculating
P_{\mathbf{dif}}V
and we find that

\displaystyle P_{\mathbf{dif}}V(x)-V(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{I}(V(a)-V(x))\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a)

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{1}{4}(x+1)^{-3/2}\int_{I}(a-x)^{2}\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a)+\frac{3}{48}(x/2)^{-5/2}\int_{I}|a-x|^{3}\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a)

(3.1)

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;+\int_{I}|V(a)-V(x)|\mathbf{1}_{\{|x-a|>x/2\}}\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a).

Let
\alpha
be as in Assumption
1.2
Item (
3
), and let us define constants

\displaystyle\omega_{exp}:

\displaystyle=\sup_{y\in I}\int_{I}e^{\frac{\alpha}{4}|a-y|}\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(y,\mathrm{d}a),

\displaystyle\omega_{2}:

\displaystyle=\sup_{y\in I}\int_{I}(a-y)^{2}\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(y,\mathrm{d}a),

\displaystyle\omega_{3}:

\displaystyle=\sup_{y\in I}\int_{I}|a-y|^{3}\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(y,\mathrm{d}a),

\displaystyle\delta_{2}:

\displaystyle=\inf_{y\in I}\int_{I}(a-y)^{2}\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(y,\mathrm{d}a).

The first three constants are all finite by e.g. Lemma
2.9
, and the last constant is strictly positive as noted in Lemma
2.16
. Notice that
V
is a globally Lipchitz function on
I
with Lipchitz constant 1/2, i.e.,
|V(x)-V(a)|\leq\frac{1}{2}|x-a|,
thus using Cauchy-Schwarz and Markov’s inequality we see that

\displaystyle\int_{I}|V(a)-V(x)|\mathbf{1}_{\{|x-a|>x/2\}}\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a)

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\int_{I}(V(a)-V(x))^{2}\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a)\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg(\int_{I}\mathbf{1}_{\{|x-a|>x/2\}}\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a)\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\frac{1}{4}\int_{I}(a-x)^{2}\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a)\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg(\frac{\int_{I}e^{\frac{\alpha}{4}|x-a|}\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a)}{e^{\frac{\alpha}{4}(x/2)}}\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\omega_{2}^{1/2}\omega_{exp}^{1/2}\cdot e^{-\frac{\alpha x}{16}}.

Plugging this bound back into (
3.1
), we find that for
x>2
one has

P_{\mathbf{dif}}V(x)-V(x)\leq-\frac{\delta_{2}}{4}(x+1)^{-3/2}+\frac{3\omega_{3}}{48}(x/2)^{-5/2}+\frac{1}{2}\omega_{2}^{1/2}\omega_{exp}^{1/2}\cdot e^{-\frac{\alpha x}{16}}.

The right side is clearly negative for sufficiently large
x>2
, thus proving the claim.
∎

Theorem 3.5
.

Let
\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}
be the Markov kernel from Assumption
1.2
Item (
6
), and
P_{\mathbf{dif}}
its associated Markov operator as in Definition
3.2
. Then there exists a unique (up to scalar multiple) invariant measure
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
on
I
, in other words a measure such that
P_{\mathbf{dif}}f\in L^{1}(\pi^{\mathrm{inv}})
whenever
f\in L^{1}(\pi^{\mathrm{inv}})
, and moreover
\int_{I}(P_{\mathbf{dif}}f-f)d\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}=0
for all
f\in L^{1}(\pi^{\mathrm{inv}})
. Furthermore,

•

\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(K)<\infty
for all compact subsets
K\subset I.

•

\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
has full support on
I
.

•

For all
f,g\in L^{1}(\pi^{\mathrm{inv}})
such that
\int_{I}g\;d\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}\neq 0
we have that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\sum_{r=1}^{n}f(X_{k})}{\sum_{r=1}^{n}g(X_{k})}=\frac{\int_{I}f\;\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}}{\int_{I}g\;\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}},\;\;\;\;\mathbf{P}_{x}\;a.s.\;\;\;\;\;for\;all\;x\in I.

Proof.

By the first bullet point in Assumption
1.2
Item (
6
), the Markov kernel
\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}
is
Strong Feller,
that is, the operator
P_{\mathbf{dif}}
sends bounded measurable functions to bounded continuous functions. In particular
\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}
is a “
T
-chain” in the sense of
[
MT93
, Definition 6.0.0 (iii)]
.

Furthermore
\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}
is “open-set irreducible” in the sense of
[
MT93
, Section 6.1.2]
by the second bullet point of Assumption
1.2
Item (
6
). In particular, by
[
MT93
, Proposition 6.1.5]
the chain is “
\psi
-irreducible” where the maximal irreduciblilty measure
\psi
has full support due to the open-set irreducibility and the Strong Feller property.

Furthermore, by Proposition
3.4
and
[
MT93
, Theorem 9.4.1]
, for all
x\in I
, there exists a compact set
K\subset I
such that the Markov chain
(X_{r})_{r\geq 0}
visits
K
infinitely often
\mathbf{P}_{x}
-a.s.. In other words, the Markov kernel
\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}
is “non-evanescent” in the sense of
[
MT93
, Section 9.2.1]
. Now by
[
MT93
, Theorem 9.2.2 (ii)]
any non-evanescent
\psi
-irreducible
T
-chain is automatically
Harris recurrent
.

By
[
MT93
, Theorem 17.3.2]
, Harris recurrent chains can be characterized as exactly those chains for which a unique invariant measure
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
exists (up to scalar multiple) and satisfies the ratio limit theorem as in the third bullet point of the theorem statement. By
[
MT93
, Theorem 10.4.9]
, the measure
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
is equivalent to the maximal irreducibility measure
\psi
, which has already been explained to have full support. By
[
MT93
, Theorem 12.3.3]
, the invariant measure
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
is finite on compact sets as long as the Foster-Lyapunov drift criterion is satisfied as we have already shown in Proposition
3.4
.
∎

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
\alpha
be as in Assumption
1.2
Item (
3
) and let
C>0
. There exists
C^{\prime}>0
such that uniformly over all
f:I\to\mathbb{R}
and
b,x\in I
and
\theta\in[0,\frac{\alpha}{2}]
the following bound holds true.

•

If
|f(x)-|x-b||\leq Ce^{-\theta|x-b|}
, then
|(P_{\mathbf{dif}}f-f)(x)|\leq C^{\prime}\theta^{-1}e^{-\theta|x-b|}
where
C^{\prime}
is a larger constant depending on
C
but not
f,x,b,\theta
.

Proof.

For simplicity of notation we will prove the claim with
b=0
but the proof for general
b
is similar, recentering around
b
rather than
0
everywhere in the argument below.

By the triangle inequality, we can write

\displaystyle|P_{\mathbf{dif}}f(x)-f(x)|

\displaystyle=|\int_{I}(f(a)-f(x))\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a)|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{I}|f(a)-|a||\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a)+\bigg|\int_{I}(|a|-|x|)\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a)\bigg|+||x|-|f(x)||

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{I}e^{-\theta|a|}\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a)+\bigg|\int_{I}(|a|-|x|)\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a)\bigg|+Ce^{-\theta|x|}.

Let us call these terms
E_{1},E_{2},E_{3}
. Note that
E_{3}
already satisfies the required bound. Since
-|a|\leq|x-a|-|x|,
we can bound
E_{1}
by

C\bigg(\sup_{x^{\prime}\in I}\sup_{\theta^{\prime}\in[0,\frac{\alpha}{2}]}\int_{I}e^{\theta|x^{\prime}-a|}\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x^{\prime},\mathrm{d}a)\bigg)\cdot e^{-\theta|x|},

and the supremum is finite by the assumption
\theta<\alpha/2.
This is indeed a bound of the desired form after replacing
C
by a larger constant.

Finally we need to bound
E_{2}
. Without loss of generality we assume
x\geq 0
, because the case
x\leq 0
is symmetric. Then we have
|x|=x=\int_{I}a\;\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a),
since every
\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x^{\prime},\cdot)
is centered at
x^{\prime}
by construction. Thus we have that

\displaystyle E_{2}

\displaystyle=\bigg|\int_{I}(|a|-a)\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a)\bigg|=2\int_{I}|a|\mathbf{1}_{\{a\leq 0\}}\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a)

Note that for
a\leq 0
and
x\geq 0
one has

|a|\mathbf{1}_{\{a\leq 0\}}\leq\theta^{-1}\sqrt{r}e^{-\theta a}=\theta^{-1}e^{-\theta(a-x)}e^{-\theta x}=\theta^{-1}e^{\theta|a-x|}e^{-\theta|x|}.

We can thus bound
E_{2}
by

C\bigg(\sup_{x^{\prime}\in I}\sup_{\theta^{\prime}\in[0,\frac{\alpha}{2}]}\int_{I}e^{\theta|x^{\prime}-a|}\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}^{r^{\prime}}(x^{\prime},\mathrm{d}a)\bigg)\theta^{-1}e^{-\theta|x|},

which is again finite by the previous lemma, as explained before. This proves the lemma.
∎

Proposition 3.7
.

let
x_{N}
be any sequence of values in
I
such that
N^{-1/2}x_{N}\to x\in\mathbb{R}
as
N\to\infty
. Consider the rescaled process
\big(N^{-1/2}X_{Nt}\big)_{t\in(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})\cap[0,T]},
viewed as random variables in
C[0,T]
under the measure
\mathbf{P}_{x_{N}}
. This is a tight sequence of processes in
C[0,T]
and any limit point is given the law of
(W,\gamma(f)L_{0}^{W})
where
W
is a Brownian motion of rate 2 starting at
x
.

Proof.

Take
\beta_{N}=0
in Proposition
3.1
.
∎

Proposition 3.8
.

Let
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
be the invariant measure constructed in Theorem
3.5
. For all decreasing functions
F:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
satisfying
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}F(k)<\infty
, one has the explicit bound

\int_{I}F(|x|)\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(\mathrm{d}x)\leq C\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}F(k),

where
C
is some absolute constant that does not depend on
F.

Proof.

We break the proof into several steps.

Step 1.
Let
u_{b}(y):=|y-b|
, and
v_{b}:=P_{\mathbf{dif}}u_{b}-u_{b}
. In this step we will prove that for every fixed
x,b\in I
, the process
N^{-1/2}\sum_{r=0}^{Nt}v_{b}(X_{r})
converges in law under
\mathbf{P}_{x}
to the process
L_{0}^{W}
, where the latter denotes the local time at zero of a Brownian motion
W
of rate 2 started from
0
.

Fix
T>0
and
q\geq 1
and
b\in I
. We claim that there exists some
C>0
such that for
N\geq 1
and
0\leq s\leq t\leq T
with
s,t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
q\geq 1
one has

\sup_{x\in I}\mathbf{E}_{x}\bigg[\bigg|N^{-1/2}\sum_{r=Ns}^{Nt}v_{b}(X_{r})\bigg|^{q}\bigg]^{1/q}\leq C(b)\sqrt{t-s}.

Indeed, this is immediate from applying Corollary
2.20
with
\beta=0
, since we have exponential decay of
v_{b}
by Lemma
3.6
with
\theta=\alpha/2
.
The tightness of the pair of processes is thus immediate from this bound. Therefore we just need to show how to identify the limit points. To do this we will use martingale problems. We already know from Proposition
3.7
that
(N^{-1/2}X_{Nt})_{t\geq 0}
is converging to a Brownian motion.

Using Corollary
2.10
with
\beta=0
, one sees that the process
Z_{r}:=u_{b}(X_{r})-\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}v_{b}(X_{s})
is a
\mathbf{P}_{x}
-martingale for every
x\in I
. Consider any joint limit point
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V})
of the 3-tuple of processes

\big(N^{-1/2}X_{Nt},N^{-1/2}u_{b}(X_{Nt}),N^{-1/2}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt-1}v_{b}(X_{s}),\big)_{t\geq 0}.

Tightness of the first and third coordinates has already been explained, and the second coordinate is tight by Lemma
3.6
and Corollary
2.20
, since Lemma
3.6
with
\theta=\alpha/2
guarantees that
|v(x)|\leq Ce^{-\frac{2}{\alpha}|x|}
. We have already shown that
\mathcal{X}
must be distributed as
W
for a Brownian motion
W
. For fixed
b\in I
, the functions
x\mapsto N^{-1/2}u_{b}(N^{1/2}x)
are converging uniformly on compacts as
N\to\infty
to absolute value, thus we must have
\mathcal{U}=|\mathcal{X}|=|W|
. Again using the fact that martingality is preserved by limit points, we see that
\mathcal{U}-\mathcal{V}=|W|-\mathcal{V}
must be a martingale for the limit point (with respect to the canonical filtration on the space of 3-tuples of continuous paths), which by Tanaka’s formula forces
\mathcal{V}=L_{0}^{W},
where
L_{0}^{W}
is the local time.

Step 2.
In this step we show that
v_{b}\in L^{1}(\pi^{\mathrm{inv}})
for every
b\in I
, and moreover
\int_{I}v_{b}\;\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
does not depend on
b.

We first show that
v_{0}\in L^{1}(\pi^{\mathrm{inv}})
, i.e., the case
b=0
. Suppose it was not the case, we will now derive a contradiction. By convexity of
u_{0}
and Jensen’s inequality, we know that
v_{0}
is nonnegative, thus
\int_{I}v_{0}\;\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}=+\infty.
On the other hand by the first bullet point of Theorem
3.5
we have that
\mathbf{1}_{[-J,J]}\in L^{1}(\pi^{\mathrm{inv}})
for all
J>0
. Then by the third bullet point of Theorem
3.5
one easily shows that for all
J>0
that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{1}_{[-J,J]}(X_{r})}{\sum_{r=0}^{N}v_{0}(X_{r})}=0

\mathbf{P}_{x}
-a.s. for all
x\in I
. By the result of Step 1, this means that for all
J>0
we have that

N^{-1/2}\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{1}_{[-J,J]}(X_{r})=\frac{\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{1}_{[-J,J]}(X_{r})}{\sum_{r=0}^{N}v_{0}(X_{r})}\cdot N^{-1/2}\sum_{r=0}^{N}v_{0}(X_{r})\;\;\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle N\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}\;\;\;0\cdot L_{0}^{W}(1)=0,

in distribution (hence in probability) under every
\mathbf{P}_{x}
. On the other hand by Corollary
2.20
with
\beta=0
and
q=2
we know that for all (fixed)
J>0

\sup_{N\geq 1}\sup_{x\in I}\mathbf{E}_{x}\bigg[\bigg(N^{-1/2}\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{1}_{[-J,J]}(X_{r})\bigg)^{2}\bigg]<\infty,

thus by uniform integrability and the previous convergence statement, for all
x\in I
one has

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbf{E}_{x}\bigg[N^{-1/2}\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{1}_{[-J,J]}(X_{r})\bigg]=0,\;\;\;for\;all\;\;J>0.

Define
g_{N}(J):=\mathbf{E}_{0}\big[N^{-1/2}\sum_{r=0}^{N}\mathbf{1}_{[-J,J]}(X_{r})\big],
so we have shown that
\lim_{N\to\infty}g_{N}(J)=0
for all
J>0.
For any
\theta>0
note that
\mathbf{1}_{[-J,J]}(x)\leq e^{\theta J/2}e^{-\theta|x|/2},
consequently we find that

\sup_{N\geq 1}g_{N}(J)\leq e^{\theta J/2}\sup_{N\geq 1}\mathbf{E}_{0}\bigg[N^{-1/2}\sum_{r=0}^{N}e^{-\theta|X_{r}|/2}\bigg]=C(\theta)e^{\theta J/2}

where we know that the second supremum is finite by Corollary
2.20
. Thus by Fubini’s theorem and the Dominated Convergence Theorem we see that for all fixed
\theta>0

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbf{E}_{0}\bigg[N^{-1/2}\sum_{r=0}^{N}\bigg(\sum_{J=1}^{\infty}e^{-\theta J}\mathbf{1}_{[-J,J]}(X_{r})\bigg)\bigg]

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\sum_{J=1}^{\infty}e^{-\theta J}g_{N}(J)=\sum_{J=1}^{\infty}e^{-\theta J}\lim_{N\to\infty}g_{N}(J)=0.

On the other hand, we know that
v_{0}
decays exponentially fast at infinity thanks to Lemma
3.6
, which means that there exists some
C,\theta>0
such that
v_{0}\leq C\sum_{J=1}^{\infty}e^{-\theta J}\mathbf{1}_{[-J,J]}
. Thus the last expression implies that
N^{-1/2}\sum_{r=0}^{N}v_{0}(X_{r})
converges to zero in
L^{1}(\mathbf{P}_{0})
, contradicting the result of Step 1 and thus completing the proof that
v_{0}\in L^{1}(\pi^{\mathrm{inv}})
.

It remains to show that
v_{b}\in L^{1}(\pi^{\mathrm{inv}})
and
\int_{I}v_{b}\;\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}=\int_{I}v_{0}\;\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
, for all
b\in I
. First note that
\int_{I}v_{0}\;\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}\neq 0
. Indeed
v_{0}\geq 0
and
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
has full support by Theorem
3.5
. So if the integral vanishes, then
v_{0}=0
which would imply that
|X_{r}|
is a nonnegative martingale, thus convergent, contradicting the result of Proposition
3.7
. From the result of Step 1 we know that for every
b\in I

\frac{\sum_{r=0}^{N}v_{b}(X_{r})}{\sum_{r=0}^{N}v_{0}(X_{r})}\;\;\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle N\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}\;\;\;\frac{L_{0}^{W}(1)}{L_{0}^{W}(1)}=1

in distribution (hence in probability) under every
\mathbf{P}_{x}
. Using the third bullet point of Theorem
3.5
, it is clear that this would be impossible unless
\int_{I}v_{b}\;\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}<\infty
and
\int_{I}v_{b}\;\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}=\int_{I}v_{0}\;\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
, for all
b\in I
.

Step 3.
In this step we will finally prove the claim being made in the proposition statement. Let
F
be as in the theorem statement. The argument will proceed very similarly to the proof of (
2.10
), but we repeat the details in the present context for additional clarity.

We first show that
\inf_{b\in I}P_{\mathbf{dif}}u_{b}(b)>0.
To prove this, note that
\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(b,\cdot)
is never a Dirac mass, simply because it has mean
x
and Assumption
1.2
Item (
6
) implies that there cannot be absorbing states. Consequently
P_{\mathbf{dif}}u_{b}(b)=\int_{I}|b-a|\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(b,\mathrm{d}a)>0
for all
b\in I
. Furthermore
P_{\mathbf{dif}}u_{b}(b)
is a continuous function of
b
, simply by the assumption of weak continuity of all of the kernels in the paper. If we had that
u_{b_{n}}(b_{n})\to 0
for some
|b_{n}|\to\infty
, this would mean that the measures
\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(b_{n},b_{n}+\cdot)
would be converging weakly to a Dirac mass at
0
. But the exponential moments of these measures are uniformly bounded by Lemma
2.9
. Thus the variances of
\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x_{n},\cdot)
would be converging to zero, which is impossible since we have already observed in (
2.8
) that they converge to
2>0
.

We now claim that there exists
\delta>0
so that for all
b\in I
one has that
v_{b}>\delta\mathbf{1}_{(b-\delta,b+\delta)}
. To prove this, note that

\displaystyle|P_{\mathbf{dif}}u_{b}(y)-P_{\mathbf{dif}}u_{y}(y)|

\displaystyle=\bigg|\int_{I}|a-b|\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(y,\mathrm{d}a)-\int_{I}|a-y|\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(y,\mathrm{d}a)\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{I}\big||a-b|-|a-y|\big|\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(y,\mathrm{d}a)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{I}|b-y|\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(y,\mathrm{d}a)=|b-y|.

Recall that
c:=\inf_{y\in I}P_{\mathbf{dif}}u_{y}(y)
has already been shown to be strictly positive. Thus for all
b,y\in I
we have
P_{\mathbf{dif}}u_{y}(y)>c
, and from the above bound it then follows that
P_{\mathbf{dif}}u_{b}(y)>2c/3
if
|b-y|<c/3.
Thus we see that
v_{x}(y)=P_{\mathbf{dif}}u_{b}(y)-|b-y|>c/3
if
|b-y|<c/3
, thus proving the claim with
\delta:=c/3.

If
I=c\mathbb{Z}
we will henceforth assume that
\delta=c
, otherwise it may be a smaller value. Using the fact that
v_{b}>\delta\mathbf{1}_{(b-\delta,b+\delta)}
, we obtain

F\leq\delta^{-1}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}F(\delta|m|)v_{\delta m}.

Integrating both sides with respect to
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
and then using the result of Step 2, we find that

\int_{I}h\;\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}\leq\delta^{-1}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}F(\delta|m|)\int_{I}v_{\delta m}\;\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}=\bigg(\delta^{-1}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}F(\delta|m|)\bigg)\int_{I}v_{0}\;\mathrm{d}\pi^{\mathrm{inv}},

and the sum is bounded above by
2\delta^{-1}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}F(k)
.
∎

3.2.
Analysis and convergence results for nonzero
\beta

With all of the necessary prerequisites established for the untilted
\beta=0
case, we will now move on to the case where the tilting coefficient
\beta
will be nonzero, as will be needed to prove convergence to the KPZ equation. The following lemma will be a crucial step in proving convergence of the additive functional process to Brownian local time under a varying tilt.

Lemma 3.9
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
t>0
and
1\leq i<j\leq k
. Recall the tilted path measures
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}
as given in Definition
2.8
. Consider any sequences
\beta_{N}\in[-\alpha/(2k),\alpha/(2k)]
and
\mathbf{x}_{N}\in I^{k}
such that
|\beta_{N}|\leq CN^{-\frac{1}{4p}}
and
N^{-1/2}\mathbf{x}_{N}\to\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
as
N\to\infty
. Take any continuous function
f:I\to\mathbb{R}
such that
|f(x)|\leq F(|x|)
for some decreasing
F:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
such that
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}F(k)<\infty
. Then we have that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(\beta_{N},k)}\bigg[N^{-1/2}\sum_{r=0}^{Nt-1}f(R_{r}^{i}-R_{r}^{j})\bigg]=\gamma(f)\mathbf{E}^{BM}_{x_{i}-x_{j}}[L_{0}^{W}(t)]

where the latter denotes expectation with respect to
a Brownian motion of rate 2 started from
x_{i}-x_{j}
where
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},...,x_{k})
. Furthermore
L_{0}^{W}
is its local time at zero, and

\gamma(f):=\frac{\int_{I}f(x)\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(dx)}{\int_{I}\big[\int_{I}|a|\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a)-|x|\big]\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(dx)}.

Note by Lemma
2.6
that we are ultimately interested in the case
\beta_{N}=N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}
, but there is no additional difficulty in considering
|\beta_{N}|\leq CN^{-\frac{1}{4p}}
at the moment.

Proof.

Step 1.
We first establish the claim in the very special case that
f=(P_{\mathbf{dif}}-\mathrm{Id})u
where
u(x)=|x|.
In this case it is clear that
\gamma(f)=1
. Letting
\overline{u}(\mathbf{x}):=|x_{i}-x_{j}|
and
\overline{f}_{\beta}(\mathbf{x}):=(\mathscr{Q}^{\beta}_{k}-\mathrm{Id})\overline{u}
, both of which are functions on
I^{k}
, we claim that

(3.2)

|f(x_{i}-x_{j})-\overline{f}_{\beta}(\mathbf{x})|\leq|\beta|^{2p-1}F(\min_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}|x_{i^{\prime}}-x_{j^{\prime}}|)+C|\beta|^{4p}.

for some large enough constant
C>0
and some decreasing function
F:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
satisfying
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}F(k)<\infty
.
To prove (
3.2
), we first prove an intermediate claim that

(3.3)

\bigg|\int_{I^{k}}|y_{i}-y_{j}|\boldsymbol{q}^{(k)}_{\beta}(\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})-\mathrm{sign}(x_{i}-x_{j})\int_{I^{k}}(y_{i}-y_{j})\boldsymbol{q}^{(k)}_{\beta}(\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})\bigg|\leq Ce^{-\frac{\alpha}{8k}|x_{i}-x_{j}|}.

To prove the latter bound, it suffices by symmetry to consider the case
x_{i}-x_{j}\geq 0
. In this case, the integral appearing on the left is bounded above by
\int_{I^{k}}|y_{i}-y_{j}|\mathbf{1}_{\{y_{i}-y_{j}\leq 0\}}\boldsymbol{q}^{(k)}_{\beta}(\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}),
which by Cauchy-Schwartz can be bounded from above by
\big(\int_{I^{k}}(y_{i}-y_{j})^{2}\boldsymbol{q}^{(k)}_{\beta}(\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})\big)^{1/2}\big(\int_{I^{k}}\mathbf{1}_{\{y_{i}-y_{j}\leq 0\}}\boldsymbol{q}^{(k)}_{\beta}(\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})\big)^{1/2}.
Using Lemma
2.9
, the first term can be bounded above by
(C+C(x_{i}-x_{j})^{2})^{1/2},
with
C
uniform over
\beta
and
\mathbf{x}
. For the second term, note that the distance of the point
\mathbf{x}
to the set
\{\mathbf{y}:y_{i}-y_{j}\leq 0\}
is
x_{i}-x_{j}
, thus by the uniform exponential moment bounds of Lemma
2.9
and Markov’s inequality we have an upper bound of
Ce^{-\frac{\alpha}{2k}|x_{i}-x_{j}|}.
Finally note that by making the constant larger, one has
(C+C(x_{i}-x_{j})^{2})^{1/2}e^{-\frac{\alpha}{2k}|x_{i}-x_{j}|}\leq Ce^{-\frac{\alpha}{8k}|x_{i}-x_{j}|},
thus establishing (
3.3
).

Note that (
3.3
) reduces proving (
3.2
) to showing that

\bigg|\int_{I^{k}}(y_{i}-y_{j})\boldsymbol{q}^{(k)}_{\beta}(\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})-\int_{I^{k}}(y_{i}-y_{j})\boldsymbol{p}^{(k)}(\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})\bigg|\leq C|\beta|^{2p-1}\mathrm{F_{decay}}\big(\min_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}|x_{i^{\prime}}-x_{j^{\prime}}|\big)+C|\beta|^{4p}.

But this is clear simply by performing a Taylor expansion of
\boldsymbol{q}^{(k)}_{\beta}
from (
2.4
) of order
4p-1
in the variable
\beta
, then applying Items (
4
) and (
5
) of Assumption
1.2
.

With (
3.2
) established, use the bound
F\big(\min_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}|x_{i^{\prime}}-x_{j^{\prime}}|\big)\leq\sum_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}F(|x_{i^{\prime}}-x_{j^{\prime}}|),
and we find that

\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(\beta_{N},k)}\bigg[N^{-1/2}\sum_{r=0}^{Nt-1}|f(R_{r}^{i}-R_{r}^{j})-\overline{f}_{\beta_{N}}(\mathbf{R}_{r})|\bigg]\leq\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(\beta_{N},k)}\bigg[N^{-1/2}\sum_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}\sum_{r=0}^{Nt-1}\big(|\beta_{N}|^{2p-1}F(|R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}|)+|\beta_{N}|^{4p}\big)\bigg].

Using
|\beta_{N}|\leq CN^{-\frac{1}{4p}}
, we may use the second bound in Corollary
2.20
to immediately conclude that the above expectation tends to zero. Thus (recalling
\gamma(f)=1
) in order to complete this step, it suffices to prove that

(3.4)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(\beta_{N},k)}\bigg[N^{-1/2}\sum_{r=0}^{Nt-1}\overline{f}_{\beta_{N}}(\mathbf{R}_{r})\bigg]=\mathbf{E}^{BM}_{x_{i}-x_{j}}[L_{0}^{W}(t)].

But by Corollary
2.10
we know that
|R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}|-\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\overline{f}_{\beta}(\mathbf{R}_{s})
is a
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}
-martingale, thus the expectation on the left side is precisely

\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(\beta_{N},k)}\bigg[N^{-1/2}\big|R^{i}_{Nt}-R^{j}_{Nt}|\bigg]-N^{-1/2}|x_{N}^{i}-x_{N}^{j}|,

where
\mathbf{x}_{N}:=(x^{1}_{N},...,x^{k}_{N})
in coordinates. By Proposition
3.1
and the uniform
L^{q}
bounds in Corollary
2.20
, we immediately have that the last expectation converges to

\mathbf{E}_{x_{i}-x_{j}}^{BM}[|W_{t}|]-|x_{i}-x_{j}|.

By Tanaka’s formula, this is precisely
\mathbf{E}^{BM}_{x_{i}-x_{j}}[L_{0}^{W}(t)],
thus establishing (
3.4
) and proving the claim for this special case.

Step 2.
Now we consider the case of general
f
. For this we use a Krylov-Bogoliubov type of trick. Fixing
t>0
henceforth, consider the sequence of measures
\gamma_{N}
on
I
given by

\int_{I}f\;\mathrm{d}\gamma_{N}:=N^{-1/2}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt-1}\mathbf{E}^{(\beta_{N},k)}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}[f(R^{i}_{s}-R^{j}_{s})].

We will show that any subsequence
\gamma_{N_{k}}
of this sequence of measures has a further subsequence
\gamma_{N_{k_{j}}}
converging as
N\to\infty
to an invariant measure
\gamma
for the Markov kernel
\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}
. By convergence, we mean that for all continuous
f:I\to\mathbb{R}
of exponential decay, we have
\int_{I}f\;\mathrm{d}\gamma_{N}\to\int_{I}f\;\mathrm{d}\gamma
along this subsequence
\gamma_{N_{k_{j}}}
.

If we can prove this, then the lemma would be proved for all functions
f
of exponential decay, because by the uniqueness in Theorem
3.5
, this would mean that the subsequential limit
\gamma
must be a constant multiple of
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
. But the result of Step 1 uniquely identifies the constant as the one appearing in the lemma statement (the expectation of the Brownian local time). If every subsequence of
\gamma_{N}
has a further subsequence converging to some fixed measure, then the sequence
\gamma_{N}
must itself converge to that fixed measure, thus completing the proof.

Thus consider a subsequence
\gamma_{N_{k}}
. There is a further convergent subsequence
\gamma_{N_{k_{j}}}
simply by the second bound in Corollary
2.20
(with
q=1
) combined with e.g. Banach-Alaoglu (to extract subsequential limits on every compact set, then applying a diagonal argument). Let us call this subsequential limit
\gamma
. We need to show that for all continuous
f:I\to\mathbb{R}
of exponential decay at infinity, one has
\int_{I}P_{\mathbf{dif}}f\;\mathrm{d}\gamma=\int_{I}f\;\mathrm{d}\gamma.
To prove this, consider such
f
, say
|f(x)|\leq e^{-\theta|x|}
where
\theta\leq\alpha/(2k)
, and define
\overline{f}:I^{k}\to\mathbb{R}
by
\overline{f}(\mathbf{x})=f(x_{i}-x_{j})
. To show that
\int_{I}P_{\mathbf{dif}}f\;\mathrm{d}\gamma=\int_{I}f\;\mathrm{d}\gamma,
it suffices to show that

(3.5)

\lim_{N\to\infty}N^{-1/2}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt-1}\mathbf{E}^{(\beta_{N},k)}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}[|(\mathscr{Q}^{0}_{k}-\mathscr{Q}^{\beta_{N}}_{k})\overline{f}(\mathbf{R}_{s})|]=0,

where
\mathscr{Q}^{0}_{k}
denotes the operator
\mathscr{Q}_{k}^{\beta}
with
\beta=0
(these operators were defined in Corollary
2.10
). Indeed, it suffices to show this because
\mathscr{Q}^{0}_{k}\overline{f}(\mathbf{R}_{s})=P_{\mathbf{dif}}f(R^{i}_{s}-R^{j}_{s})
so that
N^{-1/2}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt-1}\mathbf{E}^{(\beta_{N},k)}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}[\mathscr{Q}^{0}_{k}\overline{f}(\mathbf{R}_{s})]
converges to
\int_{I}P_{\mathbf{dif}}f\;\mathrm{d}\gamma
along the subsequence
N_{k_{j}}
, and moreover because
N^{-1/2}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt-1}\mathbf{E}^{(\beta_{N},k)}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}[\mathscr{Q}^{\beta_{N}}_{k}\overline{f}(\mathbf{R}_{s})]
by the Markov property is equal to
\int_{I}f\mathrm{d}\gamma_{N}+N^{-1/2}\big(\mathbf{E}^{(\beta_{N},k)}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}[f(R^{i}_{Nt}-R^{j}_{Nt})]-f(x^{N}_{i}-x^{N}_{j})\big),
which converges to
\int_{I}f\;\mathrm{d}\gamma
along the subsequence
N_{k_{j}}
.

To prove (
3.5
), we claim by the exponential decay assumption on
f
and from the definition (
2.4
) of the measures
\boldsymbol{q}^{(k)}_{\beta}
, one has the bound

(3.6)

|(\mathscr{Q}^{0}_{k}-\mathscr{Q}^{\beta}_{k})\overline{f}(\mathbf{x})|\leq C\min\{|\beta|,e^{-\frac{\theta}{2}|x_{i}-x_{j}|}\}\leq C|\beta|^{1/2}e^{-\frac{\theta}{4}|x_{i}-x_{j}|}.

The second inequality is immediate from the first one using
\min\{u,v\}\leq u^{1/2}v^{1/2}
. To prove the first inequality, the upper bound of
C|\beta|
is clear simply from differentiability of the kernels
\boldsymbol{q}^{(k)}_{\beta}
in the variable
\beta
near
\beta=0
, see their explicit expression (
2.4
). For the upper bound of
Ce^{-\frac{\theta}{2}|x_{i}-x_{j}|}
, we can show that both of the terms
\mathscr{Q}^{\beta}_{k}\overline{f}(\mathbf{x})
and
\mathscr{Q}^{0}_{k})\overline{f}(\mathbf{x})
are bounded above by such a quantity. To show this, write the definition
\mathscr{Q}^{\beta}_{k}\overline{f}(\mathbf{x}):=\int_{I^{k}}f(y_{i}-y_{j})\boldsymbol{q}^{(k)}_{\beta}(\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}),
then split the integral into two parts:
\{\mathbf{y}:|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}|\leq\frac{1}{2}|x_{i}-x_{j}|\}
and
\{\mathbf{y}:|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x}|>\frac{1}{2}|x_{i}-x_{j}|\}
, where
|\mathbf{x}|=\sum_{j=1}^{k}|x_{j}|
. On the first set, use the exponential decay bound on
f
to bound
|f(\mathbf{y})|\leq Ce^{-\frac{\theta}{2}|x_{i}-x_{j}|}
. On the second set, use the uniform moment bounds of Lemma
2.9
and Markov’s inequality (and
|f|\leq 1
) to obtain an upper bound of
Ce^{-\frac{\alpha}{4k}|x_{i}-x_{j}|}\leq Ce^{-\frac{\theta}{2}|x_{i}-x_{j}|}.

With (
3.6
) proved, the claim (
3.5
) follows immediately from the second bound of Corollary
2.20
. While this proves the claim for all
f
of exponential decay, the general claim for all
f
as in the theorem statement can be proved using the uniform bound (
2.21
) (only
q=1
is needed there) and an approximation argument of
f
by some sequence
f_{n}
with
|f_{n}|\leq|f|
with each
|f_{n}|
decaying exponentially.
∎

Proposition 3.10
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
T>0
and
1\leq i<j\leq k
. Recall the tilted path measure
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}
as given in Definition
2.8
. Consider any sequences
\beta_{N}\in[-\alpha/(2k),\alpha/(2k)]
and
\mathbf{x}_{N}\in I^{k}
such that
|\beta_{N}|\leq CN^{-\frac{1}{4p}}
and
N^{-1/2}\mathbf{x}_{N}\to\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
as
N\to\infty
. Take any continuous function
f:I\to\mathbb{R}
such that
|f(x)|\leq F(|x|)
for some decreasing
F:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
such that
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}F(k)<\infty
. Consider the pair of processes

\bigg(N^{-1/2}\big(R^{i}_{Nt}-R^{j}_{Nt}\big),N^{-1/2}\sum_{r=0}^{Nt-1}f(R_{r}^{i}-R_{r}^{j})\bigg)_{t\in(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})\cap[0,T]},

viewed as
C([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{2})
-valued random variables under the measures
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(\beta_{N},k)}.
This sequence converges in law to the pair

(W,\gamma(f)L_{0}^{W}(t))
, where
W
is a Brownian motion of rate 2 starting at
x_{i}-x_{j}
and
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},...,x_{k})
. Furthermore
L_{0}^{W}
is its local time at zero, and

\gamma(f):=\frac{\int_{I}f(x)\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(dx)}{\int_{I}\big[|x|-\int_{I}|a|\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a)\big]\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(dx)}.

Proof.

Convergence of the first coordinate was already established in Proposition
3.1
. Tightness of the second coordinate is immediate from the second bound in Corollary
2.20
. We will thus show how to jointly identify the limit of the second coordinate. Without loss of generality we can assume
f\geq 0
, since we can always write
f=f_{+}-f_{-}
with
f_{+},f_{-}\geq 0
, both of which still satisfy the same exponential bounds, and the result respects the linearity in the function variable.

Consider any joint limit point
\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{lim}}
, which is a probability measure on the canonical space
C([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Let
(W,\mathcal{L})
denote the canonical process on that space, and let
(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{lim}}(t))_{t\geq 0}
be the canonical filtration on that space. On one hand
|W_{t}|-|x_{i}-x_{j}|-L_{0}^{W}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}sgn(W_{s})dW_{s}
is a
\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{lim}}
-martingale, because
W
is a
\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{lim}}
-Brownian motion as we already verified in Proposition
3.1
. In particular this implies that for
t\geq s\geq 0
,

(3.7)

\mathbf{E}_{\mathrm{lim}}[L_{0}^{W}(t)-L_{0}^{W}(s)|\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{lim}}(s)]=g(t-s,W_{s}),

where
g(t,x):=\mathbf{E}_{x}^{BM}[L_{0}^{B}(t)],
and the expectation is with respect to a Brownian motion
B
of rate 2 started from
x\in\mathbb{R}
. So far we have only studied the first part
W
of the joint limit. Now we study the other part
\mathcal{L}
. Immediately from the Markov property of the prelimiting processes and the result of Lemma
3.9
, we find that for
t\geq s\geq 0

(3.8)

\mathbf{E}_{\mathrm{lim}}[\mathcal{L}(t)-\mathcal{L}(s)|\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{lim}}(s)]=\gamma(f)\cdot g(t-s,W_{s}).

Combining (
3.7
) and (
3.8
), we see that
\mathcal{L}-\gamma(f)L_{0}^{W}
is a continuous
\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{lim}}
-martingale starting from 0. But it is also a difference of two increasing processes, thus of bounded variation
\mathbf{P}_{\mathrm{lim}}
-almost surely. We conclude that
\mathcal{L}-\gamma(f)L_{0}^{W}=0
, proving the theorem.
∎

We now come to the result that will be most crucial in proving convergence of the quenched density field to the KPZ equation, namely the invariance principle for the tilted Markov chains
\boldsymbol{q}^{(k)}_{\beta}
.

Theorem 3.11
.

Fix any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and constants
C,T>0
. Take any sequence
\mathbf{x}_{N}\in I^{k}
such that
N^{-1/2}\mathbf{x}_{N}\to\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Recall the drift constant
d_{N}
that was defined in Equation (
1.3
) of the introduction. Fix any continuous function
f:I\to\mathbb{R}
such that
|f(x)|\leq F(|x|)
for some decreasing
F:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
such that
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}F(k)<\infty
. Let
\mathbf{R}=(\mathbf{R}(r))_{r\geq 0}
be the canonical process on
(I^{k})^{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
, and define the rescaled processes

\displaystyle{\mathbf{X}}_{N}(t):=\frac{\mathbf{R}(Nt)-d_{N}t}{\sqrt{N}},\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\mathscr{V}^{ij}_{N}(f;t):=\frac{\sum_{r=1}^{Nt}f(R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r})}{\sqrt{N}},

where these expressions are valid for
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, and understood to be linearly interpolated for
t\notin N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Now recall the measure
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}
as given in Definition
2.8
. Then the processes
(\mathbf{X}_{N},\mathscr{V}_{N})
under
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}},k)}

converge in law with respect to the topology of
C([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{k(k-1)/2})
to
(\mathbf{U},(\gamma(f)\cdot L^{ij})_{1\leq i<j\leq k})
where

•

\mathbf{U}=(U^{1},...,U^{k})
is a standard
k
-dimensional Brownian motion starting from
\boldsymbol{x}
.

•

L^{ij}=L_{0}^{U^{i}-U^{j}}
denotes the pairwise local time of the
i^{th}
and
j^{th}
coordinates.

•

\gamma(f)
is a real number given by

\gamma(f):=\frac{\int_{I}f(x)\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(\mathrm{d}x)}{\int_{I}\big[\int_{I}|a|\;\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\mathrm{d}a)-|x|\big]\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(\mathrm{d}x)}.

Before the proof, we remark that the tilting strength of
N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}
can be replaced by any sequence
\beta_{N}
satisfying
|\beta_{N}|\leq CN^{-\frac{1}{4p}}
but then the constants
d_{N}
need to be replaced by
N\mathpzc F(\beta_{N})
where
\mathpzc F
was defined in Corollary
2.13
. This will be clear from the proof.

Proof.

We break the proof into several steps. Throughout the proof we will set
\beta_{N}:=N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}
.

Step 1.
In this step we find a useful family of martingales, associated to each of the coordinates of the process. Define the
j^{th}
coordinate function
v_{j}:I^{k}\to\mathbb{R}
by
(x_{1},...,x_{k})\mapsto x_{j}.
Note by Corollary
2.10
that for each
1\leq j\leq k
that the process

M^{j,\beta}_{r}:=R^{j}_{r}-\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}(\mathscr{Q}^{\beta}_{k}-\mathrm{Id})v_{j}(\mathbf{R}_{s})

is a
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}
-martingale for all
\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}
and
\beta\in[-\alpha/(2k),\alpha/(2k)]
. Explicitly we have that

(3.9)

(\mathscr{Q}^{\beta}_{k}-\mathrm{Id})v_{j}(\mathbf{x})=\int_{I^{k}}(y_{j}-x_{j})\boldsymbol{q}^{(k)}_{\beta}(\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}),

with
\boldsymbol{q}^{(k)}_{\beta}
as defined in (
2.4
).

Step 2.
Fix
j\in\{1,...,k\}
, and let
R^{j}
as usual denote the
j^{th}
coordinate of the canonical process
\mathbf{R}
on
(I^{k})^{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
. Recall the quantity
\mathcal{S}_{q}(M,P)
from Lemma
2.17
. In this step we will show that for each
q\geq 1
,

(3.10)

\sup_{N\geq 1}\mathcal{S}_{q}(M^{j,\beta_{N}};\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(\beta_{N},k)})<\infty.

First note by (
3.9
) and Lemma
2.9
that

(3.11)

\sup_{\beta\in[-\alpha/(2k),\alpha/(2k)]}\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}}|(\mathscr{Q}^{\beta}_{k}-\mathrm{Id})v_{j}(\mathbf{x})|\leq\sup_{\beta\in[-\alpha/(2k),\alpha/(2k)]}\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}}\int_{I^{k}}|y_{j}-x_{j}|\boldsymbol{q}^{(k)}_{\beta}(\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})<\infty.

Then note by the Markov property and another application of Lemma
2.9
that

(3.12)

\sup_{r\geq 1}\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}}\sup_{\beta\in[-\alpha/(2k),\alpha/(2k)]}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}[|R^{j}_{r}-R^{j}_{r-1}|^{q}]\leq\sup_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in I^{k}}\sup_{\beta\in[-\alpha/(2k),\alpha/(2k)]}\int_{I^{k}}|y_{j}-x_{j}|^{q}\boldsymbol{q}^{(k)}_{\beta}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})<\infty.

Combining (
3.11
) and (
3.12
), we immediately obtain (
3.10
).

Step 3.
In this step we study the the processes given by

\mathcal{B}_{N}(t):=N^{-1/2}d_{N}t-N^{-1/2}\sum_{r=0}^{Nt-1}(\mathscr{Q}^{\beta_{N}}_{k}-\mathrm{Id})v_{j}(\mathbf{R}_{r}).

In particular we will show that if
q\geq 1
then
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(\beta_{N},k)}[|\mathcal{B}_{N}(t)-\mathcal{B}_{N}(s)|^{q}]^{1/q}\leq CN^{-(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{4p})}|t-s|^{1/2},
so that
\mathcal{B}_{N}
converges to the zero process in the topology of
C[0,T]
. To prove this, notice that in the notation of Corollary
2.13
, we have
d_{N}=N\mathpzc F(\beta_{N})
. Consequently by that corollary we have that for
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}

\displaystyle|\mathcal{B}_{N}(t+N^{-1})-\mathcal{B}_{N}(t)|

\displaystyle=N^{-1/2}|\mathpzc F(\beta_{N})-\int_{I^{k}}(y_{j}-R^{j}_{r})\boldsymbol{q}^{(k)}_{\beta}(\mathbf{R}_{r},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})|

\displaystyle\leq N^{-1/2}\big||\beta_{N}|^{2p-1}\mathrm{F_{decay}}\big(\min_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}|R^{i^{\prime}}_{r}-R^{j^{\prime}}_{r}|\big)+|\beta_{N}|^{4p}\big|

\displaystyle=\big|N^{-1+\frac{1}{4p}}\mathrm{F_{decay}}\big(\min_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}|R^{i^{\prime}}_{r}-R^{j^{\prime}}_{r}|\big)+N^{-3/2}|

where
t=Nr
and we used the fact that
\beta_{N}=N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}
in the last equality. Thus using the bound
\mathrm{F_{decay}}(\min_{\ell}u_{\ell})\leq\sum_{\ell}\mathrm{F_{decay}}(u_{\ell})
we find that

\displaystyle|\mathcal{B}_{N}(t)-\mathcal{B}_{N}(s)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{u\in[s,t]\cap N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}|\mathcal{B}_{N}(u+N^{-1})-\mathcal{B}_{N}(u)|

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(N^{-1+\frac{1}{4p}}\sum_{r\in[Ns,Nt]\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}\sum_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}\mathrm{F_{decay}}(|R^{i^{\prime}}_{r}-R^{j^{\prime}}_{r}|)\bigg)\;\;\;\;+N^{-1/2}(t-s).

Thus taking expectation and applying the second estimate of Corollary
2.20
we find that

(3.13)

\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(\beta_{N},k)}[|\mathcal{B}_{N}(t)-\mathcal{B}_{N}(s)|^{q}]^{1/q}\leq CN^{-(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{4p})}|t-s|^{1/2}+N^{-1/2}|t-s|.

Step 4.
Combining the results of (
3.10
) and (
3.13
) and Lemma
2.17
, we immediately obtain tightness of the rescaled process
\mathbf{X}_{N}
in the space
C[0,T]
. It remains to identify the limit point as Brownian motion. In this step, we will begin by showing that any limit point
\mathbf{U}=(U^{1},...,U^{k})
is necessarily a martingale in the joint filtration of all coordinates, and moreover each coordinate
U^{j}
is
individually
distributed as a standard Brownian motion started from
x_{j}
where
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},...,x_{k})
is as in the theorem statement.

Write
\mathbf{X}_{N}=(X_{N}^{1},...,X_{N}^{j})
in coordinates. By (
3.13
), the difference
X_{N}^{j}(t)-N^{-1/2}M^{j,\beta_{N}}_{Nt}
converges in probability to zero in the topology of
C[0,T]
, thus it suffices to show that if
\mathbf{U}
is any limit point of the processes
(N^{-1/2}M^{j,\beta_{N}}_{Nt})_{1\leq j\leq k,t\geq 0}
then
\mathbf{U}
is a martingale and each coordinate is individually a Brownian motion. The martingality of the limit point is clear, since martingality is preserved by limit points as long as one has uniform integrability as guaranteed by the
L^{q}
bounds (
3.10
).

Now we will show that each coordinate of the limit point
\mathbf{U}
is a standard Brownian motion. To do this, we need to study the quadratic variations of the martingales
(N^{-1/2}M^{j,\beta_{N}}_{Nt})_{t\geq 0}
for each fixed
j
. More precisely, note that the process

Y^{j,\beta}_{r}:=(M^{j,\beta}_{r})^{2}-\sum_{s=0}^{r-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}[(M^{j,\beta}_{s+1}-M^{j,\beta}_{s})^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]

is a
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}
-martingale. We claim that
(N^{-1}Y^{j,\beta_{N}}_{Nt})_{t\geq 0}
is a tight family of processes in
C[0,T]
as
N\to\infty
. Indeed, one easily verifies that
\big(N^{-1}(M^{j,\beta_{N}}_{Nt})^{2}\big)_{t\geq 0}
is tight and satisfies the same
L^{q}
estimates as the processes
\big(N^{-1/2}M^{j,\beta_{N}}_{Nt}\big)_{t\geq 0}
on any compact time interval
t\in[0,T]
, simply because it is the square of a process satisfying such bounds as shown in Steps 2 and 3. On the other hand, by Minkowski’s inequality, we also have that

\displaystyle\bigg\|N^{-1}\sum_{r=Ns}^{Nt}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(\beta_{N},k)}[(M^{j,\beta_{N}}_{r+1}-M^{j,\beta_{N}}_{r})^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]\bigg\|_{L^{q}(\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(\beta_{N},k)})}

\displaystyle\leq N^{-1}\sum_{r=Ns}^{Nt}\big\|M^{j,\beta_{N}}_{r+1}-M^{j,\beta_{N}}_{r}\big\|_{L^{2q}(\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(\beta_{N},k)})}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\sup_{N\geq 1}\mathcal{S}_{2q}(M^{j,\beta_{N}};\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(\beta_{N},k)})\bigg)|t-s|,

where the supremum is finite by (
3.10
). Summarizing these bounds, we have that for each
q\geq 1
we have
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(\beta_{N},k)}\big[\big(N^{-1}|Y^{j,\beta_{N}}_{Nt}-Y^{j,\beta_{N}}_{Ns}|\big)^{q}\big]^{1/q}\leq C|t-s|^{1/2}
for all
s,t
in a compact interval, thus completing the proof that
(N^{-1}Y^{j,\beta_{N}}_{Nt})_{t\geq 0}
is a tight family of processes in
C[0,T]
as
N\to\infty
.

We will now study the joint limit as
N\to\infty
under the measures
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(\beta_{N},k)}
of the pair of processes
(N^{-1/2}M^{j,\beta_{N}}_{Nt},N^{-1}Y^{j,\beta_{N}}_{Nt})_{t\geq 0}.
Consider any joint limit point
(U,V).
Then the pair are martingales in their joint filtration, since martingality is preserved by limit points as long as one has the
L^{q}
bounds as shown above. Let us study how
U,V
must be related. Corollary
2.13
easily implies that

\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}}\bigg|\int_{I^{k}}(y_{j}-x_{j})\boldsymbol{q}^{(k)}_{\beta}(\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})-\mathpzc F(\beta)\bigg|\leq C|\beta|

where
C
is independent of
\beta\in[-\alpha/(2k),\alpha/(2k)].
From this inequality and (
3.9
), it is immediate that
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(\beta_{N},k)}\big[\big((\mathscr{Q}^{\beta_{N}}_{k}-\mathrm{Id})v_{j}(\mathbf{R}_{r})-\mathpzc F(\beta_{N})\big)^{2}\big]\leq C|\beta_{N}|^{2},
which implies from the definition of the martingales
M^{j,\beta}
that

(3.14)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(\beta_{N},k)}\bigg[N^{-1}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt-1}\big|\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(\beta_{N},k)}\big[(M_{r+1}^{j,\beta_{N}}-M_{r}^{j,\beta_{N}})^{2}-(R^{j}_{r+1}-R^{j}_{r}-\mathpzc F(\beta_{N}))^{2}\big|\mathcal{F}_{r}\big]\big|\bigg]=0,

see proof of Proposition
3.1
for a very similar calculation in more detail. Letting
\mathpzc G
be as in Corollary
2.13
, we also have from that corollary that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(\beta_{N},k)}\bigg[N^{-1}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt-1}\big|\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(\beta_{N},k)}

\displaystyle\big[(R^{j}_{r+1}-R^{j}_{r}-\mathpzc F(\beta_{N}))^{2}\big|\mathcal{F}_{r}\big]-\big(\mathpzc G(\beta_{N})-\mathpzc F(\beta_{N})^{2}\big)\big|\bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(\beta_{N},k)}\bigg[N^{-1}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt-1}\bigg|\int_{I^{k}}(y_{j}-R^{j}_{r}-\mathpzc F(\beta_{N}))^{2}q^{(k)}_{\beta_{N}}(\mathbf{R}_{r},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})-\big(\mathpzc G(\beta_{N})-\mathpzc F(\beta_{N})^{2}\big)\bigg|\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(\beta_{N},k)}\bigg[N^{-1}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt-1}\bigg(\sum_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq k}\mathrm{F_{decay}}\big(|R^{i^{\prime}}_{r}-R^{j^{\prime}}_{r}|\big)+|\beta_{N}|^{4p-1}\bigg)\bigg]

(3.15)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle N\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}0.

where we applied Corollary
2.20
and
|\beta_{N}|\to 0
in the last line. Combining (
3.14
) and (
3.15
), and noting from Assumption
1.2
that
\mathpzc G(\beta_{N})-\mathpzc F(\beta_{N})^{2}\to m_{2}-m_{1}^{2}=1
as
\beta_{N}\to 0,
we find that

(3.16)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(\beta_{N},k)}\bigg[\bigg|t\;\;\;-\;\;\;N^{-1}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt-1}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(\beta_{N},k)}\big[(M_{r+1}^{j,\beta_{N}}-M_{r}^{j,\beta_{N}})^{2}\big|\mathcal{F}_{r}\big]\bigg|\bigg]=0.

Using (
3.16
), one may immediately conclude that the joint limit point
(U,V)
of the pair of processes
(N^{-1/2}M^{j,\beta_{N}}_{Nt},N^{-1}Y^{j,\beta_{N}}_{Nt})_{t\geq 0}
must satisfy
V_{t}=U_{t}^{2}-t
. Since both are continuous martingales as explained above, it must be true that
U
is a standard Brownian motion.

Step 5.
In this step we will complete the proof of the theorem. The tightness of
\mathbf{X}_{N}
has been proved in previous steps. The tightness of the processes
\mathscr{V}^{ij}_{N}(f;\bullet)
is immediate from Corollary
2.20
and the fact that
f
is assumed to have exponential decay.

Consider any joint limit point
(\mathbf{U},(\mathcal{L}^{ij})_{1\leq i<j\leq k})
in the space
C([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{k(k-1)/2}).
On one hand, the result of Step 5 shows that
\mathbf{U}
is a martingale in the joint filtration of all processes, with
\mathbf{U}_{0}=\boldsymbol{x}
, and each coordinate has quadratic variation
t
, i.e.,
\langle U^{j}\rangle_{t}=t
. On the other hand, the result of Proposition
3.1
shows that
\langle U^{i}-U^{j}\rangle_{t}=2t
whenever
i<j
. This forces
\langle U^{i},U^{j}\rangle_{t}=0
for
i\neq j
. We conclude by Levy’s criterion that
\mathbf{U}
is a standard
k
-dimensional Brownian motion. Now the result of Theorem
3.10
forces that for each
i<j
the marginal law of the pair
(U^{i}-U^{j},\mathcal{L}^{ij})
must satisfy
\mathcal{L}^{ij}=\gamma(f)\cdot L_{0}^{U^{i}-U^{j}}.
This uniquely identifies the joint limit point
(\mathbf{U},(\mathcal{L}^{ij})_{1\leq i<j\leq k})
in the space
C([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{k(k-1)/2}),
completing the proof of the theorem.
∎

As a corollary of the above convergence theorem, we will now prove that moments of the field (
1.4
) converge to the moments of (
1.5
). While the moment convergence is not enough to prove the weak convergence of Theorem
1.4
, it gives a strong indication of it, and more importantly it implies some useful estimates that will be used later.

Corollary 3.12

(Convergence of moments of the rescaled field to those of the KPZ equation)
.

Let
\mathfrak{H}^{N}(t,\cdot)
be as defined in (
1.4
) in the introduction. Then for each
k\in\mathbb{N},t>0,
and
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}),

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[\mathfrak{H}^{N}(t,\phi)^{k}]=\mathbf{E}_{BM^{\otimes k}}\bigg[e^{\gamma(\boldsymbol{\zeta})\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}L_{0}^{U^{i}-U^{j}}(t)}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\phi(U^{i}_{t})\bigg],
