where the expectation on the right side is with respect to a standard
k
-dimensional Brownian motion
(U^{1},...,U^{k})
, where
L_{0}^{X}
denotes local time at zero of the process
X
, where
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
is the function in Definition
2.11
, and
\gamma(f)
is the coefficient defined in Theorem
3.11
.

Proof.

This is immediate from Lemma
2.6
, Proposition
2.12
(with all
\beta_{i}=N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}
and
m=0
), and Theorem
3.11
, noting that Corollary
2.21
guarantees uniform integrability of all relevant quantities up to exponential scales (hence convergence in law implies convergence of the respective expectations).
∎

The following corollary of Theorem
3.11
will be useful in later sections.

Corollary 3.13
.

Assume the same notations and assumptions of Theorem
3.11
.
Suppose
k=2m
is even. For
t>s>0
define

(3.17)

\displaystyle\Delta_{m}[s,t]:=\{(s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{m})\in[s,t]^{m}\mid s\leq s_{1}\leq s_{2}\leq\cdots\leq s_{m}\leq t\}

to be the set of all ordered
m
-tuples of points in
[s,t]
. We define
\mathcal{M}(\Delta_{m}[0,T])
to be the space of finite and non-negative Borel measures on that simplex, equipped with the topology of weak convergence. Fix some measurable function
f:I\to\mathbb{R}
such that
|f(x)|\leq F(|x|)
for some decreasing
F:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
such that
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}F(k)<\infty
. Consider the following sequence of
\mathcal{M}(\Delta_{m}[0,T])
-valued random variables

(3.18)

\displaystyle\nu_{N}^{f,k}:=N^{-m/2}\sum_{u_{1}\leq...\leq u_{m}\in(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})\cap[0,T]}\;\;\;\prod_{j=1}^{m}f\big(R^{2j-1}_{Nu_{j}}-R^{2j}_{Nu_{j}}\big)\delta_{(u_{1},\ldots,u_{m})}

where as usual
(R^{1},...,R^{2m})
is the canonical process on
(I^{k})^{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
. The random variables
\{\nu_{N}^{f,k}\}_{N\geq 1}
are tight in the topology of
\mathcal{M}(\Delta_{m}[0,T])
. Moreover, recalling the notations from Theorem
3.11
, as
N\to\infty
any limit point under
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(\beta_{N},k)}
of the triple of processes
(\mathbf{X}_{N},\;\big(\mathscr{V}^{ij}_{N}(f,\bullet)\big)_{1\leq i<j\leq k},\;\nu_{N}^{f,k})
is of the form

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{U},\;\big(\gamma(f)\cdot L^{U^{i}-U^{j}}\big)_{1\leq i<j\leq k},\;\gamma(f)^{m}\prod_{j=1}^{m}dL^{U^{2j-1}-U^{2j}}(u_{j})\right)

where
\mathbf{U},L^{ij}
are as in Theorem
3.11
, and
dL(t)
denotes the Lebesgue-Stiltjes measure induced by the increasing function
t\mapsto L(t)
.

Proof.

Note that

\displaystyle\nu_{N}^{f,k}(\Delta_{m}[0,T])\leq\prod_{j=1}^{m}\left[N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{r=0}^{NT}f\big(R^{2j-1}_{Nu}-R^{2j}_{Nu}\big)\right].

From the second bound in Corollary
2.20
, we know for all
q\geq 1
that the
q^{th}
moments of each of the terms in the product are uniformly bounded as
N\to\infty
under the measures
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{N}}^{(\beta_{N},k)}
, where
\beta_{N}=N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}
. Thus for all
q\geq 1
, we can deduce using e.g. Hölder inequality that

(3.19)

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}}\sup_{\beta\in[-\alpha/(2k),\alpha/(2k)]}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}[\nu_{N}^{f,k}(\Delta_{m}[0,T])^{q}]<\infty.

Hence the laws of
\{\nu_{N}^{f,k}\}_{N\geq 1}
are tight as measures on
\mathcal{M}(\Delta_{m}[0,T])
, because the total mass of
\nu_{N}^{f,k}
is a tight family of random variables by (
3.19
) and because
\Delta_{m}[0,T]
as defined in (
3.17
) is a compact space (hence so is the unit ball of
\mathcal{M}(\Delta_{m}[0,T])
in the weak topology). Let
\left(\mathbf{U},\big(K^{ij}\big)_{1\leq i<j\leq k},\nu\right)
be any limit point of the sequence
(\mathbf{X}_{N},\;\big(\mathscr{V}^{ij}_{N}(f,\bullet)\big)_{1\leq i<j\leq k},\;\nu_{N}^{f,k})
. Note that the joint cumulative distribution function of the measure
\nu
is necessarily given by a product of the
K^{ij}
, since this is true in the prelimit. Since we know that
K^{ij}=\gamma(f)L_{0}^{U^{i}-U^{j}}
by Theorem
3.11
, this immediately implies that
\nu=\gamma(f)^{m}\prod_{j=1}^{m}dL_{0}^{U^{2j-1}-U^{2j}}(u_{j})
.
∎

This concludes our study of the
k
-point motion
\boldsymbol{p}^{(k)}
and its Girsanov tilts
\boldsymbol{q}^{(k)}_{\beta}
, which was initiated in Section 2. Subsequent sections will use the convergence results of this section to prove Theorem
1.4
.

4.
The discrete stochastic heat equation

In this section, we are going to change topic to studying the rescaled field
\mathfrak{H}^{N}
from (
1.4
). In particular, we will derive a discrete stochastic heat equation for this rescaled field
\mathfrak{H}^{N}
. This discrete equation will be crucial in proving Theorem
1.4
, because it will eventually allow us to show that any limit point satisfies the martingale problem characterization of the equation (
1.5
)
[
KS88
,
BG97
]
.

First we establish some notations that will be used in this section. For a measure
\nu
on
I
we will write
\int_{I}f(x)\mu(\mathrm{d}x+y)
to mean the integral of
f
with respect to the measure
\nu_{y}(A):=\nu(A+y),
where
A+y
is the translate of the Borel set
A
by the real number
y
. We will hope that this translation notation does not cause any confusion.

Let
\vec{d}_{N}
be as in (
1.3
), and define the discrete lattice

\Lambda_{N}:=\{(t,x)\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{R}:x+tN^{-1}d_{N}\in\mathbb{N}\times I\}

. Let
\mu
be the measure from Assumption
1.2
Item (
3
), and for
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
define the measures
\mu^{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}x)=e^{\lambda x-\log M(\lambda)}\mu(\mathrm{d}x)
. Note that
\mu^{\lambda}
makes sense for
|\lambda|<\alpha
where
\alpha
is as in Assumption
1.2
Item (
3
).

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\Lambda_{N}
be as above, and suppose that
f
is a signed measure on
\Lambda_{N}
. We then define the
discrete heat operator

\mathcal{L}_{N}f(t,x)=f(t+1,x-N^{-1}d_{N})-\int_{I}f(t,x-y)\mu^{N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}}(\mathrm{d}y).

Since convolution of measures is well-defined, this expression makes sense even if
f(t,\cdot)
is a finite measure for all
t\in\mathbb{N}
. The resultant object
g=\mathcal{L}_{N}f
makes sense as a signed measure on
\mathbb{N}\times I
, such that
g(t,\cdot)
is a
finite
signed measure for all
t\in\mathbb{N},
which has exponential moments up to some value.

In particular if
\mathfrak{K}(r,\cdot)
is given by (
1.2
), then we can define for
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}

(4.1)

Z_{N}(r,\mathrm{d}x)=D_{N,N^{-1}r,N^{-1/2}x}\mathfrak{K}(r,rN^{-1}d_{N}+\mathrm{d}x).

Then
\mathcal{L}_{N}Z_{N}
makes sense as a signed measure on
\mathbb{N}\times I.
Furthermore
\mathcal{L}_{N}Z_{N}(Nt,\cdot)
is a finite signed measure on
I
for all
t
, and still has exponential moments. Note that the main object of interest in Theorem
1.4
is essentially the diffusively rescaled field
Z_{N}(Nt,N^{1/2}\mathrm{d}x),
and roughly speaking the main goal in the remainder of the section will be to find and study a nice family of martingales for this rescaled family.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
. For
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
we define
M_{t}^{N}(\phi)
for
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
by the formula
M_{0}^{N}(\phi)=0
, and

(4.2)

M^{N}_{t+N^{-1}}(\phi)-M_{t}^{N}(\phi):=\int_{I}\phi(N^{-1/2}x)\;(\mathcal{L}_{N}Z_{N})(Nt,\mathrm{d}x).

Then for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, the process
M^{N}(\phi)
is a martingale indexed by
N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, with respect to the filtration
(\mathcal{F}^{\omega}_{Nt})_{t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
where
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\omega}:=\sigma(K_{1},...,K_{t})
for
t\in\mathbb{N}
(and
\omega=(K_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
are the iid environment kernels as in Assumption
1.2
).

Proof.

First note by the convolution property of the kernels
\mathfrak{K}
of (
1.2
) that if
r=Nt\in\mathbb{N}
then

Z_{N}(r+1,\mathrm{d}x-N^{-1}d_{N})=D_{N,N^{-1}(r+1),N^{-1/2}(x-N^{-1}d_{N})}\int_{I}D_{N,N^{-1}r,N^{-1/2}y}^{-1}K_{r+1}(y,\mathrm{d}x)Z_{N}(r,\mathrm{d}y).

Now notice that

\displaystyle D_{N,N^{-1}(r+1),N^{-1/2}(x-N^{-1}d_{N})}D_{N,N^{-1}r,N^{-1/2}y}^{-1}

\displaystyle=e^{N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}(x-N^{-1}d_{N}-y)+N^{-1-\frac{1}{4p}}d_{N}-\log M(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}})}

\displaystyle=e^{N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}(x-y)-\log M(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}})}.

On the other hand we also have by definition that
\mu^{N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}}(\mathrm{d}x-y)=e^{N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}(x-y)-\log M(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}})}\mu(\mathrm{d}x-y).
Therefore we can write

(4.3)

(\mathcal{L}_{N}Z_{N})(Nt,\mathrm{d}x)=\int_{I}e^{N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}(x-y)-\log M(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}})}\big[K_{Nt+1}(y,\mathrm{d}x)-\mu(\mathrm{d}x-y)\big]Z_{N}(Nt,\mathrm{d}y).

From the last expression the martingality is clear because
Z_{N}(Nt,\mathrm{d}y)
is
\mathcal{F}_{Nt-1}
-measurable, and
K_{Nt+1}(y,\mathrm{d}x)
is independent of
\mathcal{F}_{Nt-1}
with
\mathbb{E}[K_{Nt+1}(y,\mathrm{d}x)]=\mu(\mathrm{d}x-y).

∎

Next we will calculate the predictable quadratic variations of the above martingales. If
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
define

J^{N}\phi(y_{1},y_{2}):=\int_{I^{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{2}e^{N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}(x_{j}-y_{j})-\log M(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}})}\phi(N^{-1/2}x_{j})\bigg[\boldsymbol{p}^{(2)}\big((y_{1},y_{2}),(\mathrm{d}x_{1},\mathrm{d}x_{2})\big)-\mu(\mathrm{d}x_{1}-y_{1})\mu(\mathrm{d}x_{2}-y_{2})\bigg].

By (
4.3
) we find that

\displaystyle\langle M^{N}(\phi)\rangle_{t}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{s=1}^{Nt}\mathbb{E}\big[\big(M^{N}_{sN^{-1}}(\phi)-M^{N}_{(s-1)N^{-1}}(\phi)\big)^{2}\big|\mathcal{F}_{s-1}^{\omega}\big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=1}^{Nt}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(\int_{I}\int_{I}\phi(N^{-1/2}x)e^{N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}(x-y)-\log M(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}})}\big[K_{s+1}(y,\mathrm{d}x)-\mu(\mathrm{d}x-y)\big]Z_{N}(s,\mathrm{d}y)\bigg)^{2}\bigg|\mathcal{F}^{\omega}_{s-1}\bigg]

(4.4)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=1}^{Nt}\int_{I^{2}}(J^{N}\phi)(y_{1},y_{2})Z_{N}(s,\mathrm{d}y_{1})Z_{N}(s,\mathrm{d}y_{2}).

In the last line we again use the fact that
Z_{N}
is
\mathcal{F}^{\omega}_{s-1}
-measurable. Now we let
E^{\omega}_{(2)}
denote a quenched expectation of two independent particles
(R^{1}_{n},R^{2}_{n})_{n\geq 0}
sampled from a fixed realization of the environment kernels
\{K_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}.
Then the last expression may be rewritten as

\sum_{s=1}^{Nt}E^{\omega}_{(2)}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{2}D_{N,N^{-1}s,N^{-1/2}(R^{j}_{Ns}-sN^{-1}d_{N})}\big(J^{N}\phi\big)(R^{1}_{s},R^{2}_{s})\bigg].

Unfortunately
J^{N}\phi
is not yet in a form that is amenable to asymptotic analysis and tightness arguments, therefore we will need to perform Taylor expansions of some expressions therein, which will yield many “error” terms that we will need to show are inconsequential in the limit.

First let us abbreviate

(4.5)

\rho\big((y_{1},y_{2}),(\mathrm{d}x_{1},\mathrm{d}x_{2})\big):=\boldsymbol{p}^{(2)}\big((y_{1},y_{2}),(\mathrm{d}x_{1},\mathrm{d}x_{2})\big)-\mu(\mathrm{d}x_{1}-y_{1})\mu(\mathrm{d}x_{2}-y_{2}).

Define

\mathcal{A}^{1}_{N}(\phi,y_{1},y_{2}):=\int_{I^{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{2}e^{N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}(x_{j}-y_{j})-\log M(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}})}\cdot\big[\prod_{j=1}^{2}\phi(N^{-1/2}x_{j})\;\;-\;\phi(N^{-1/2}y_{1})^{2}\big]\rho\big((y_{1},y_{2}),(\mathrm{d}x_{1},\mathrm{d}x_{2})\big).

We can then write

(4.6)

\displaystyle J^{N}\phi(y_{1},y_{2})=\mathcal{A}^{1}_{N}(\phi,y_{1},y_{2})+\phi(N^{-1/2}y_{1})^{2}\cdot\int_{I^{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{2}e^{N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}(x_{j}-y_{j})-\log M(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}})}\rho\big((y_{1},y_{2}),(\mathrm{d}x_{1},\mathrm{d}x_{2})\big).

Now we are going to expand the exponential in powers of
N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}
, truncating after the
(2p)^{th}
term. To this end, let us define

H_{2p}(x):=e^{x}-\sum_{k=0}^{2p}\frac{x^{k}}{k!},

so that
|H_{2p}(x)|\leq|x|^{2p+1}e^{|x|}.
Now write the integral appearing on the right side of (
4.6
) as

\displaystyle\int_{I^{2}}

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{2}e^{N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}(x_{j}-y_{j})-\log M(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}})}\rho\big((y_{1},y_{2}),(\mathrm{d}x_{1},\mathrm{d}x_{2})\big)

\displaystyle=e^{-2\log M(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}})}\int_{I^{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{2}\bigg[H_{2p}\big(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}(x_{j}-y_{j})\big)+\sum_{k=0}^{2p}\frac{N^{-\frac{k}{4p}}(x_{j}-y_{j})^{k}}{k!}\bigg]\rho\big((y_{1},y_{2}),(\mathrm{d}x_{1},\mathrm{d}x_{2})\big)

From the deterministicity in Assumption
1.2
Item (
4
), note that we necessarily have

(4.7)

\int_{I^{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{2}(x_{j}-y_{j})^{k_{j}}\rho\big((y_{1},y_{2}),(\mathrm{d}x_{1},\mathrm{d}x_{2})\big)=0,\;\;\;\;\;\text{if}\;\;\;\;k_{1}<p\;\;\;or\;\;\;k_{2}<p.

Therefore we may write

\displaystyle\int_{I^{2}}

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{2}\bigg[H_{2p}\big(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}(x_{j}-y_{j})\big)+\sum_{k=0}^{2p}\frac{N^{-\frac{k}{4p}}(x_{j}-y_{j})^{k}}{k!}\bigg]\rho\big((y_{1},y_{2}),(\mathrm{d}x_{1},\mathrm{d}x_{2})\big)

\displaystyle=N^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{\zeta}(y_{1}-y_{2})+\sum_{j=2,3,4}\mathcal{A}^{j}_{N}(y_{1}-y_{2}),

where

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\zeta}(y_{1}-y_{2})

\displaystyle:=(p!)^{-2}\int_{I^{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{2}(x_{j}-y_{j})^{p}\rho\big((y_{1},y_{2}),(\mathrm{d}x_{1},\mathrm{d}x_{2})\big)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{2}_{N}(y_{1}-y_{2})

\displaystyle:=\int_{I^{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{2}H_{2p}\big(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}(x_{j}-y_{j})\big)\rho\big((y_{1},y_{2}),(\mathrm{d}x_{1},\mathrm{d}x_{2})\big)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{3}_{N}(y_{1}-y_{2})

\displaystyle:=2\int_{I^{2}}H_{2p}\big(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}(x_{1}-y_{1})\big)\sum_{k=0}^{2p}\frac{N^{-\frac{k}{4p}}(x_{2}-y_{2})^{k}}{k!}\rho\big((y_{1},y_{2}),(\mathrm{d}x_{1},\mathrm{d}x_{2})\big)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{4}_{N}(y_{1}-y_{2})

\displaystyle:=\int_{I^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\leq k_{1},k_{2}\leq 2p\\
k_{1}+k_{2}>2p\end{subarray}}\frac{N^{-\frac{(k_{1}+k_{2})}{4p}}(x_{1}-y_{1})^{k_{1}}(x_{2}-y_{2})^{k_{2}}}{k_{1}!k_{2}!}\rho\big((y_{1},y_{2}),(\mathrm{d}x_{1},\mathrm{d}x_{2})\big).

In the last expression we are using the fact that if
k_{1}+k_{2}\leq 2p
then either
(k_{1},k_{2})=(p,p)
(whose contribution is already measured by
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
) or at least one of
k_{1},k_{2}
is less than
p
(hence the contribution vanishes by (
4.7
)). Note that the above expressions appear to be functions of
(y_{1},y_{2})
, but we have implicitly used the translation invariance condition in Assumption
1.2
Item (
2
) to say that they are actually only dependent on
y_{1}-y_{2}
.

We will ultimately show that
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
is important in the limit, while the terms
\mathcal{A}^{j}_{N}
have vanishing contribution. Since
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
has a prefactor of
N^{-1/2}
and since
\mathcal{A}^{2},\mathcal{A}^{3},\mathcal{A}^{4}
are of similar form but of order
N^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{4p}}
and smaller, this is already somewhat intuitively clear. We therefore define

(4.8)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{j}_{N}(t,\phi)

\displaystyle:=e^{-2\log M(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}})}\sum_{s=0}^{Nt-1}\int_{I^{2}}\phi(N^{-1/2}y_{1})^{2}\mathcal{A}^{j}_{N}(y_{1}-y_{2})Z_{N}(s,\mathrm{d}y_{1})Z_{N}(s,\mathrm{d}y_{2}),\;\;\;\;\;\;\;j=2,3,4.

We also define

(4.9)

\mathcal{E}^{1}_{N}(t,\phi)=\sum_{s=0}^{Nt-1}\int_{I^{2}}\mathcal{A}^{1}_{N}(\phi,y_{1},y_{2})Z_{N}(s,\mathrm{d}y_{1})Z_{N}(s,\mathrm{d}y_{2}).

We then summarize our calculations as follows.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
M^{N}
be the martingale from (
4.2
). For all
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
we have that

\displaystyle\langle

\displaystyle M^{N}(\phi)\rangle_{t}=e^{-2\log M(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}})}Q^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{N}(t,\phi^{2})+\sum_{j=1}^{4}\mathcal{E}^{j}_{N}(t,\phi)

where
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
is the function from Definition
2.11
and where for any bounded function
f:I\to\mathbb{R}
, the random field
Q^{f}_{N}
is defined as

(4.10)

Q^{f}_{N}(t,\phi):=N^{-1/2}\sum_{s=1}^{Nt}E^{\omega}_{(2)}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{2}D_{N,N^{-1}s,N^{-1/2}(R^{j}_{s}-N^{-1}d_{N}s)}\cdot\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{1}_{s}-N^{-1}d_{N}s)\big)f(R^{1}_{s}-R^{2}_{s})\bigg],

and furthermore
\mathcal{E}^{1}_{N}
is defined in (
4.9
), where
\mathcal{E}^{2}_{N},\mathcal{E}^{3}_{N},\mathcal{E}^{4}_{N}
are defined in (
4.8
), and
E^{\omega}_{(2)}
denotes a quenched expectation of two independent motions
(R^{1}_{r},R^{2}_{r})_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
in a fixed realization of the environment kernels
\omega=(K_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
.

The above expression (
4.10
) for the quadratic variation will be extremely useful when identifying the limit points. In particular, it will help us to obtain the correct noise coefficient because the expression for
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
is precisely the expression in the numerator of this coefficient
\gamma_{\mathrm{ext}}
in Theorem
1.4
, up to a constant.

4.1.
Analysis of the quadratic variation field

This subsection will heavily focus on obtaining crucial formulas and estimates for the quadratic variation field (QVF)
Q_{N}
defined in (
4.10
). Later, these formulas and estimates will allow us to show tightness of (
1.4
) and also uniquely identify its limit points. The main idea of the proofs in this section is to use the probabilistic interpretation (
4.10
) of the quadratic variation field together with the strong convergence results of Section 3 to obtain very precise estimates. We first have two lemmas before stating the key estimate of this section. At this point, one should also recall the path measures
\mathbf{P}_{RW^{(k)}}=\mathbf{P}^{(\beta=0,k)}_{(0,...,0)}
from Section 2, as well as their tilted generalizations from Definition
2.8
.

Lemma 4.4

(Moment formulas)
.

Fix any bounded functions
\psi,\phi
on
\mathbb{R}
and
N\geq 1
. Suppose that
(R^{1},\ldots,R^{2k})
denotes the canonical process on
(I^{2k})^{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
. Recall
\Delta_{k}[s,t]
from (
3.17
), and define
\Delta_{k}^{N}[s,t]:=(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})^{k}\cap\Delta_{k}[s,t]
. We have the following moment formulas.

(a)

For all
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, all
\gamma>0
, and all bounded
f:I\to\mathbb{R}
, we have that

(4.11)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg(Q_{N}^{f}(t,\psi)-\frac{\gamma}{N}\sum_{s\in(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})\cap[0,t]}\mathfrak{H}^{N}(s,\phi)^{2}\bigg)^{k}\right]

\displaystyle=k!N^{-k}\sum_{(s_{1},\ldots,s_{k})\in\Delta_{k}^{N}[0,t]}

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{RW^{(2k)}}\bigg[\prod_{i=1}^{k}D_{N,s_{i},N^{-1/2}(R^{2i-1}_{Ns_{i}}-d_{N}s_{i})}\cdot D_{N,s_{i},N^{-1/2}(R^{2i}_{Ns_{i}}-d_{N}s_{i})}\Upsilon_{N}^{f}({Ns_{i}};R^{2i-1},R^{2i})\bigg],

where for two processes
X=(X_{r})_{R\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
and
Y=(Y_{r})_{R\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
,

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{N}^{f}(r;X,Y)

\displaystyle:=N^{1/2}\psi\big(N^{-1/2}(X_{r}-N^{-1/4}r)\big)f(X_{r}-Y_{r})-\gamma\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(X_{r}-N^{-1/4}r)\big)\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(Y_{r}-N^{-1/4}r)\big).

(b)

For all
0\leq s<t
we have the following moment formula for the temporal increment of the QVF

(4.12)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg(Q_{N}^{f}(t,\psi)-Q_{N}^{f}(s,\psi)\bigg)^{k}\right]

\displaystyle=N^{-k/2}k!\sum_{(s_{1},\ldots,s_{k})\in\Delta_{k}^{N}[s,t]}

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{RW_{\nu}^{(2k)}}\bigg[\prod_{i=1}^{k}D_{N,s_{i},N^{-1/2}(R^{2i-1}_{Ns_{i}}-d_{N}s_{i})}D_{N,s_{i},N^{-1/2}(R^{2i}_{Ns_{i}}-d_{N}s_{i})}

\displaystyle\cdot\psi(N^{-1/2}(R^{2i}_{Ns_{i}}-d_{N}s_{i}))f(R^{2i-1}_{Ns_{i}}-R^{2i}_{Ns_{i}})\bigg].

Proof.

Note by (
1.4
) and (
1.2
) that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{H}^{N}(t,\phi)^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{I^{2}}D_{N,t,N^{-1/2}(x-d_{N}t)}D_{N,t,N^{-1/2}(y-d_{N}t)}

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\cdot\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(x-d_{N}t)\big)\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(y-d_{N}t)\big)\mathfrak{K}(Nt,dx)\mathfrak{K}(Nt,dy)

\displaystyle=E_{(2)}^{\omega}\big[D_{N,t,N^{-1/2}(S_{Nt}-d_{N}t)}D_{N,t,N^{-1/2}(R_{Nt}-d_{N}t)}

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\cdot\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(S_{Nt}-d_{N}t)\big)\phi\big(N^{-1/2}(R_{Nt}-d_{N}t)\big)\big].

Here
E_{(2)}^{\omega}
denotes quenched expectation for two independent motions
(R_{r},S_{r})_{r\geq 0}
in the fixed realization of the iid environment kernels
\omega=(K_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}.
Note that if we apply
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{s\in(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})\cap[0,t]}
to the last expression, then it is of a similar form to the definition (
4.10
) of
Q_{N}
, hence we have that

\displaystyle Q_{N}^{f}

\displaystyle(t,\psi)-\frac{\gamma}{N}\sum_{s\in(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})\cap[0,t]}\mathfrak{H}^{N}(s,\phi)^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{s\in(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})\cap[0,t]}E_{(2)}^{\omega}[D_{N,s,N^{-1/2}(R_{Ns}-d_{N}s)}D_{N,s,N^{-1/2}(S_{Ns}-d_{N}s)}\Upsilon_{N}^{f}(Ns;R,S)].

From here one expands out the
k^{th}
power of both sides of this equation, then uses the independence of the quenched motions, and finally applies the annealed expectation over the quenched expectation to deduce (
4.11
). The proof of (
4.12
) is similar.
∎

Lemma 4.5
.

Fix any
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\{\psi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}
and
\{\phi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{2k}
be bounded continuous functions on
\mathbb{R}
.

Recall
\Delta_{k}[s,t]
from (
3.17
), and define
\Delta_{k}^{N}[s,t]:=(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})^{k}\cap\Delta_{k}[s,t].
Let
A
be a subset of
\{1,2,\ldots,k\}
. Let
B=\{1,2\ldots,k\}\cap A^{c}
. Define

\displaystyle E_{1}(\vec{t}):=\prod_{i\in A}D_{N,t_{i},N^{-1/2}(R^{2i-1}_{Nt_{i}}-d_{N}t_{i})}\phi_{2i-1}\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{2i-1}_{Nt_{i}}-d_{N}t_{i})\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\cdot\prod_{i\in A}D_{N,t_{i},N^{-1/2}(R^{2i}_{Nt_{i}}-d_{N}t_{i})}\phi_{2i}\big(N^{-1/2}(R^{2i}_{Nt_{i}}-d_{N}t_{i})\big)

\displaystyle E_{2}(\vec{t}):=\prod_{i\in B}D_{N,t_{i},N^{-1/2}(R^{2i-1}_{Nt_{i}}-d_{N}t_{i})}D_{N,t_{i},N^{-1/2}(R^{2i}_{Nt_{i}}-d_{N}t_{i})}\psi_{i}(N^{-1/2}(R_{Nt_{i}}^{2i}-d_{N}t_{i}))f(R^{2i-1}_{Nt_{i}}-R^{2i}_{Nt_{i}})

For each
0\leq s<t\leq T<\infty
we have

(4.13)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}N^{-|A|-\tfrac{1}{2}|B|}\cdot\mathbf{E}_{RW^{(2k)}}\bigg[\sum_{(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})\in\Delta_{k}^{N}[s,t]}E_{1}(\vec{t})E_{2}(\vec{t})\bigg]

\displaystyle=\gamma(f)^{|B|}\mathbf{E}_{BM^{\otimes(2k)}}\bigg[\int_{\Delta_{k}[s,t]}e^{\gamma(\boldsymbol{\zeta})\mathcal{L}_{k}(\vec{t})}\prod_{i\in A}\phi_{2i-1}(U_{t_{i}}^{2i-1})\phi_{2i}(U_{t_{i}}^{2i})dt_{i}\cdot\prod_{i\in B}\psi_{i}(U_{t_{i}}^{2i})dL_{0}^{U^{2i-1}-U^{2i}}(t_{i})\bigg],

where the expectation on the right is with respect to a
2k
-dimensional standard Brownian motion
(U^{1},\ldots,U^{2k})
starting at the origin, and

(4.14)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{k}(\vec{t}):=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{2i-1\leq p<q\leq 2k}\left[L_{0}^{U^{p}-U^{q}}(t_{i})-L_{0}^{U^{p}-U^{q}}(t_{i-1})\right].

Here
\int_{0}^{t}f(s)dL_{0}^{U^{i}-U^{j}}(s)
denotes the integration of the continuous function
f:[0,t]\to\mathbb{R}
against the (random) Lebesgue-Stiltjes measure
dL_{0}^{U^{i}-U^{j}}
induced from the increasing function
t\mapsto L_{0}^{U^{i}-U^{j}}(t).

We remark that
A
and
B
are allowed to be empty in the above lemma, in fact both are important cases.

Proof.

We are going to abbreviate
X^{i}_{N}(t):=N^{-1/2}(R^{i}_{Nt}-d_{N}t)
, and we will also abbreviate

C_{N}(t,x,y):=D_{N,t,x}D_{N,t,y}.

Let us illustrate the proof when
k=2
and
A=\{1\},

B=\{2\}
, as this will already illustrate all of the main ideas of the general argument without becoming extremely notationally cumbersome. We will denote
(\mathcal{F}_{r})_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
the canonical filtration on
(I^{2k})^{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
. In this case, for each
(t_{1},t_{2})\in\Delta_{2}[s,t]
we can condition on the smaller time
Nt_{1}
to write
\mathbf{E}_{RW^{(4)}}[E_{1}(t_{1},t_{2})E_{2}(t_{1},t_{2})]
as

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{RW^{(4)}}\big[C_{N}\big(X_{N}^{1}(t_{1}),X_{N}^{2}(t_{1})\big)\prod_{i=1}^{2}\phi_{1}(X_{N}^{i}(t_{1}))C_{N}\big(X_{N}^{3}(t_{2}),X_{N}^{4}(t_{2})\big)\psi_{2}(X_{N}^{4}(t_{2}))f(R^{3}_{Nt_{2}}-R^{4}_{Nt_{2}})\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}_{RW^{(4)}}\bigg[C_{N}\big(X_{N}^{1}(t_{1}),X_{N}^{2}(t_{1})\big)\prod_{i=1}^{2}\phi_{1}(X_{N}^{i}(t_{1}))

(4.15)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\cdot\mathbf{E}_{RW^{(4)}}[C_{N}\big(X_{N}^{3}(t_{2}),X_{N}^{4}(t_{2})\big)\psi_{2}(X_{N}^{4}(t_{2}))f(R^{3}_{Nt_{2}}-R^{4}_{Nt_{2}})\big|\mathcal{F}_{Nt_{1}}\big]\bigg].

For
\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}
and
\beta\in[-\alpha/(2k),\alpha/(2k)]
let
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}
be the “tilted” path measures from Definition
2.8
, then define for
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
x,y\in I
and
\beta\in[-\alpha/(2k),\alpha/(2k)]

\displaystyle\mathscr{G}_{N}(r,x,y):=\mathbf{E}_{(x,y)}^{(\beta,2)}\bigg[\mathcal{H}^{2}\big(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}},(x,y),\mathbf{R},r\big)\cdot\psi_{2}\big((N^{-1/2}(R^{2}_{r}-N^{-1}d_{N}r)\big)f(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})\bigg],

where

\mathcal{H}^{2k}(\beta,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{R},r):=\exp\bigg(\sum_{s=1}^{r}\bigg\{\log\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(0,2k)}\big[e^{\beta\sum_{j=1}^{2k}(R^{j}_{s}-R^{j}_{s-1})}\big|\mathcal{F}_{s-1}\big]\;-2k\log M(\beta)\bigg\}\bigg)

One sees from the Markov property of the
k
-point motion that the inner conditional expectation of (
4.15
) can be rewritten in terms of the tilted measures as

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}

{}_{RW^{(4)}}[C_{N}\big(X_{N}^{3}(t_{2}),X_{N}^{4}(t_{2})\big)\psi_{2}(X_{N}^{4}(t_{2}))f(R^{3}_{Nt_{2}}-R^{4}_{Nt_{2}})\big|\mathcal{F}_{Nt_{1}}\big]

\displaystyle=C_{N}\big(X_{N}^{3}(t_{1}),X_{N}^{4}(t_{1})\big)\cdot\mathscr{G}_{N}(N(t_{2}-t_{1}),R^{3}_{Nt_{1}},R^{4}_{Nt_{1}}),

where the exponential term
\mathcal{H}_{N}
in the expression for
\mathscr{G}_{N}
appears completely analogously to Lemma
2.6
. This has “reduced” all of the constants
D_{N,t,x}
down to time
t_{1}
, now if we apply another tilt we can get rid of those constants as well. More precisely, from the preceding observations and Definition
2.8
we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{RW^{(4)}}[E_{1}(t_{1},t_{2})E_{2}(t_{1},t_{2})]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}_{RW^{(4)}}\bigg[C_{N}\big(X_{N}^{1}(t_{1}),X_{N}^{2}(t_{1})\big)C_{N}\big(X_{N}^{3}(t_{1}),X_{N}^{4}(t_{1})\big)\prod_{i=1}^{2}\phi_{1}(X_{N}^{i}(t_{1}))\mathscr{G}_{N}(N(t_{2}-t_{1}),R^{3}_{Nt_{1}},R^{4}_{Nt_{1}})\bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}_{(0,0,0,0)}^{(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}},4)}\bigg[\mathcal{H}^{4}(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}},(0,0,0,0),\mathbf{R},Nt_{1})\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{2}\phi_{1}(X_{N}^{i}(t_{1}))\mathscr{G}_{N}(N(t_{2}-t_{1}),R^{3}_{Nt_{1}},R^{4}_{Nt_{1}})\bigg]

Now to prove the lemma, we want to apply
N^{-3/2}\sum_{(t_{1},t_{2})\in\Delta_{2}^{N}[s,t]}
to the last expression, then take the limit and show that it approaches the correct quantity. To do this, define for
t_{1}\in[s,t]\cap(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})

\mathscr{U}_{N}(t_{1}):=N^{-1/2}\sum_{t_{2}\in[t_{1},t]\cap(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})}\mathscr{G}_{N}(N(t_{2}-t_{1}),R^{3}_{Nt_{1}},R^{4}_{Nt_{1}}).

Due to the presence of the term
f(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})
in the expression for
\mathscr{G}_{N}
, one sees that the expression for
\mathscr{U}_{N}
can be interpreted as an integral with respect to the measures
\gamma_{N}^{f}
of Corollary
3.13
. Thus using (
3.19
) and Corollary
2.22
the reader may convince herself that
\mathbf{E}_{(0,0)}^{(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}},4)}[|\mathscr{U}_{N}(t_{1})-\mathscr{U}_{N}(t_{1}^{\prime})|^{q}]^{1/q}\leq C|t_{1}-t_{1}^{\prime}|^{1/2}
with
C=C(q)
independent of
N,t_{1},t_{1}^{\prime}
.

By Theorem
3.11
, we know that under the measures
\mathbf{E}_{(0,0,0,0)}^{(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}},4)}
, the 4-tuple
(X_{N}^{1},...,X_{N}^{4})
is converging in law to a standard 4-dimensional Brownian motion
(W^{1},W^{2},W^{3},W^{4})
. By applying Proposition
2.12
(with
\beta_{i}=N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}
and
m=0
), one sees that to leading order, the expression for
\mathcal{H}^{2}(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}},(x,y),\mathbf{R},r)
is given by the exponential of
N^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{\zeta}(R^{1}_{r}-R^{2}_{r})
. Thus using Corollary
3.13
with
k=1
and using the fact that the map from
\mathcal{M}(\Delta_{k}[0,T])\times C(\Delta_{k}[0,T])\to\mathbb{R}
given by
(f,\mu)\mapsto\int_{\Delta_{k}[0,T]}f\;d\mu
is continuous, one may show that under the measures
\mathbf{E}_{(0,0,0,0)}^{(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}},4)}
, the process
\mathscr{U}_{N}
is converging in law jointly with the
X_{N}^{i}
(with respect to the topology of
C[0,T]
) to the process

V(t_{1}):=\gamma(f)\cdot\mathbf{E}_{BM^{\otimes 2}}^{(W^{3}_{t_{1}},W^{4}_{t_{1}})}\big[\int_{t_{1}}^{t}e^{\gamma(\boldsymbol{\zeta})L_{0}^{B}(t_{2}-t_{1})}\psi_{2}(B_{t_{2}})dL_{0}^{B}(t_{2})\big].

Here the expectation is with respect to a Brownian motion
B
of rate 2 that is independent of
(W^{1},...,W^{4})
. Uniform integrability of all quantities involved (hence convergence of the respective expectations) is clear from the moment bounds in Corollary
2.20
, Corollary
2.21
, and Equation (
3.19
).

By again applying Proposition
2.12
(with
\beta_{i}=N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}
and
m=0
), one sees that to leading order the expression for
\mathcal{H}^{4}(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}},(0,0,0,0),\mathbf{R},r)
is given by the exponential of
N^{-1/2}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq 4}\boldsymbol{\zeta}(R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r})
. Summarizing these facts and applying the convergence in law of the 5-tuple
(X_{N}^{1},...,X_{N}^{4},\mathscr{U}_{N})
to
(W^{1},...,W^{4},V)
, we see that

\displaystyle N

{}^{-3/2}\mathbf{E}_{RW^{(4)}}\bigg[\sum_{(t_{1},t_{2})\in\Delta_{2}^{N}[s,t]}E_{1}(t_{1},t_{2})E_{2}(t_{1},t_{2})\bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}_{(0,0,0,0)}^{(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}},4)}\bigg[N^{-1}\sum_{t_{1}\in[s,t]\cap(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})}\mathcal{H}^{4}(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}},(0,0,0,0),\mathbf{R},Nt_{1})\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{2}\phi_{1}(X_{N}^{i}(t_{1}))\cdot\mathscr{U}_{N}(t_{1})\bigg]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle N\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbf{E}_{BM^{\otimes 4}}\bigg[\int_{s}^{t}e^{\gamma(\boldsymbol{\zeta})\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq 4}L_{0}^{W^{i}-W^{j}}(t_{1})}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\phi_{1}(W^{i}(t_{1}))\cdot V(t_{1})dt_{1}\bigg]

where we used the fact that convergence in the topology of
C[0,T]
is strong enough to imply convergence in law of the Riemann sums of the prelimiting process to the integral of the limiting process (and the uniform integrability guaranteed by Corollaries
2.20
and
2.22
guarantees convergence of the respective expectations). It is now an exercise with the definition of the process
V
and the Markov property of Brownian motion to simplify the last expression to the desired form as in the lemma statement.

While we have proved the claim in a special case when
k=2
, the proof of the general case is similar: one applies the Markov property repeatedly and proceeds inductively in the variable
k
, using the convergence results of Sections 2 and 3 at each step (specifically Corollaries
2.20
,
2.21
,
2.22
,
3.13
, and Theorem
3.11
exactly as we have done above).
∎

Proposition 4.6

(Key estimate for the QVF)
.

Let
a\in\mathbb{R}
and let
\xi(x):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-x^{2}}
, and let
\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}(x):=\varepsilon^{-1}\xi(\varepsilon^{-1}(x-a))
. Let
\boldsymbol{\zeta}:I\to\mathbb{R}
be the specific function from Definition
2.11
.
Then for all
t>0
and
a\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
,

(4.16)

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\;\limsup_{N\to\infty}\;\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(Q_{N}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}(t,\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a})-\frac{\gamma(\boldsymbol{\zeta})}{N}\sum_{s\in(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})\cap[0,t]}\mathfrak{H}^{N}(s,\xi_{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}}^{a})^{2}\bigg)^{2}\bigg]=0.

Furthermore, for all
t>0
we have the bound

(4.17)

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varepsilon>0\\
a\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}\end{subarray}}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left[1\wedge\frac{1}{|\log a|^{2}}\right]\!\cdot\!\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(Q_{N}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}(t,\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a})-\frac{\gamma(\boldsymbol{\zeta})}{N}\sum_{s\in(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})\cap[0,t]}\mathfrak{H}^{N}(s,\xi_{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}}^{a})^{2}\bigg)^{2}\bigg]<\infty.

The above proposition is the key estimate that will allow us to identify limit points. We remark that in the proposition statement
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
and
\gamma(\boldsymbol{\zeta})
can respectively be replaced by any
f
and
\gamma(f)
for any function
f:I\to\mathbb{R}
of exponential decay, though we will not need the more general version.

Proof.

Applying Lemma
4.4

(a)
with
\gamma=\gamma(\boldsymbol{\zeta})
, and Proposition
4.5
with
k=2
, then we obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg(Q_{N}(t,\psi)-\frac{\gamma(\boldsymbol{\zeta})}{N}\sum_{s\in(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})\cap[0,t]}\mathfrak{H}^{N}(s,\phi)^{2}\bigg)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=2\gamma(\boldsymbol{\zeta})^{2}\cdot\mathbf{E}_{BM^{\otimes 4}}\left[\int_{\Delta_{2}(0,t)}e^{\gamma(\boldsymbol{\zeta})\mathcal{L}_{2}(s_{1},s_{2})}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\left(\psi(X_{s_{i}}^{i})\tfrac{1}{2}dL_{0}^{X^{i}-Y^{i}}(s_{i})-\phi(X^{i})\phi(Y^{i})ds_{i}\right)\right]

(4.18)

\displaystyle=2\gamma(\boldsymbol{\zeta})^{2}\hskip-2.84544pt\!\cdot\!\mathbf{E}_{BM^{\otimes 4}}\left[\int\limits_{\Delta_{2}(0,t)}\hskip-8.5359pte^{\gamma(\boldsymbol{\zeta})\mathcal{L}_{2}(s_{1},s_{2})}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\hskip-2.84544pt\left(\psi\big(\tfrac{1}{2}(X_{s_{i}}^{i}+Y_{s_{i}}^{i})\big)\tfrac{1}{2}dL_{0}^{X^{i}-Y^{i}}(s_{i})-\phi(X^{i})\phi(Y^{i})ds_{i}\right)\hskip-2.84544pt\right]

for all
\psi,\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
, where
\mathcal{L}_{2}
is defined in (
4.14
), and
(X^{1},X^{2},Y^{1},Y^{2})
is a 4d standard BM under
\mathbf{P}_{B^{\otimes 4}}
.
The second equality in the above equation follows by observing that
X_{u}^{i}=Y_{u}^{i}
for
u
in the support of
L_{0}^{X^{i}-Y^{i}}(du)
.

We shall now write
\mathbf{E}
instead of
\mathbf{E}_{B^{\otimes 4}}
for convenience. Let us now take

\psi(x):=\xi^{a}_{\varepsilon}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi\varepsilon^{2}}}e^{-(x-a)^{2}/\varepsilon^{2}},\quad\phi(x):=\xi^{a}_{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\varepsilon^{2}}}e^{-(x-a)^{2}/2\varepsilon^{2}},

in (
4.18
). Using the identity
\xi^{a}_{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}}(x)\xi^{a}_{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}}(y)=\xi^{a}_{\varepsilon}((x+y)/2)\xi^{0}_{2\varepsilon}(x-y)
, we may now write (
4.18
) as

(4.19)

\displaystyle 2\gamma(\boldsymbol{\zeta})^{2}\hskip-2.84544pt\!\cdot\!\mathbf{E}\left[\int\limits_{\Delta_{2}(0,t)}\hskip-5.69046pte^{\gamma(\boldsymbol{\zeta})\mathcal{L}_{2}(s_{1},s_{2})}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\left(\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}(X_{s_{i}}^{i}+Y_{s_{i}}^{i})\big)\!\cdot\!(\tfrac{1}{2}dL_{0}^{X^{i}-Y^{i}}(s_{i})-\xi_{2\varepsilon}^{0}(X_{s_{i}}^{i}-Y_{s_{i}}^{i})ds_{i})\right)\hskip-2.84544pt\right]\!.\!

Let us write
U^{i,-}:=X^{i}-Y^{i}
and
U^{i,+}:=X^{i}+Y^{i}
. Note that under
\mathbf{P}_{B^{\otimes 4}}
the four processes
U^{1,-},U^{1,+},U^{2,-},U^{2,+}
are independent Brownian motions with diffusion coefficient
2
. This enables us to view (
4.19
) as

\displaystyle 2\gamma(\boldsymbol{\zeta})^{2}\cdot\mathbf{E}\left[\int_{\Delta_{2}(0,t)}e^{\gamma(\boldsymbol{\zeta})\mathcal{L}_{2}(s_{1},s_{2})}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\left(\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{s_{i}}^{i,+})\big)\cdot(\tfrac{1}{2}dL_{0}^{U^{i,-}}(s_{i})-\xi_{2\varepsilon}^{0}(U_{s_{i}}^{i,-})ds_{i})\right)\right]

(4.20)

\displaystyle=:2\gamma(\boldsymbol{\zeta})^{2}[A_{1}(\varepsilon)-A_{2}(\varepsilon)-A_{3}(\varepsilon)+A_{4}(\varepsilon)],

where

\displaystyle A_{1}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{E}\left[\int_{\Delta_{2}(0,t)}e^{\gamma(\boldsymbol{\zeta})\mathcal{L}_{2}(s_{1},s_{2})}\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{s_{1}}^{1,+}\big)\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{s_{2}}^{2,+}\big)\,\tfrac{1}{2}dL_{0}^{U^{1,-}}(s_{1})\,\tfrac{1}{2}dL_{0}^{U^{2,-}}(s_{2})\right]

\displaystyle A_{2}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{E}\left[\int_{\Delta_{2}(0,t)}e^{\gamma(\boldsymbol{\zeta})\mathcal{L}_{2}(s_{1},s_{2})}\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{s_{1}}^{1,+}\big)\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{s_{2}}^{2,+}\big)\xi_{2\varepsilon}^{0}(U_{s_{2}}^{2,-})\,\tfrac{1}{2}dL_{0}^{U^{1,-}}(s_{1})\,ds_{2}\right]

\displaystyle A_{3}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{E}\left[\int_{\Delta_{2}(0,t)}e^{\gamma(\boldsymbol{\zeta})\mathcal{L}_{2}(s_{1},s_{2})}\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{s_{1}}^{1,+}\big)\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{s_{2}}^{2,+}\big)\xi_{2\varepsilon}^{0}(U_{s_{1}}^{1,-})\,\tfrac{1}{2}dL_{0}^{U^{2,-}}(s_{2})\,ds_{1}\right]

\displaystyle A_{4}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{E}\left[\int_{\Delta_{2}(0,t)}e^{\gamma(\boldsymbol{\zeta})\mathcal{L}_{2}(s_{1},s_{2})}\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{s_{1}}^{1,+}\big)\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{s_{2}}^{2,+}\big)\xi_{2\varepsilon}^{0}(U_{s_{1}}^{1,-})\xi_{2\varepsilon}^{0}(U_{s_{2}}^{2,-})\,ds_{1}\,ds_{2}\right].

From here, the goal is to show that (
4.20
) vanishes as
\varepsilon\to 0
as long as
a\neq 0
, as well as establish a bound given by the right side of (
4.17
) for each of the four terms
A_{i}(\varepsilon)
.

Formally,
\tfrac{1}{2}dL_{0}^{U^{i,\pm}}(s_{i})
is the same as
\delta_{0}(U^{i,\pm})ds_{i}
, which suggests that each of the
A_{i}
may be written in terms of Brownian bridge expectations. This is indeed the case, and consequently the proofs of the desired convergence statements and bounds for the terms
A_{i}(\varepsilon)
rely purely on elementary (albeit lengthy) disintegration formulas for Brownian motion at its endpoint, and these proofs can be copied verbatim from
[
DDP23b
, Proof of Proposition 5.3: Steps 2 and 3]
, replacing the coefficient
\sigma
appearing there with our coefficient
\gamma(\boldsymbol{\zeta})
throughout the proof. For brevity, we will not reproduce the details here.
∎

With the “key estimate” proved, next we focus on obtaining bounds that will be useful for proving tightness of the rescaled field (
1.4
).

Proposition 4.7

(Estimates for moments of the increments of QVF)
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
T>0
, and
F:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
decreasing such that
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}F(k)<\infty
. Let
f(x):=F(|x|)
. Then there exists a constant
C=C(k,T,F)>0
such that for all bounded measurable functions
\phi
on
\mathbb{R}
and all
0\leq s<t\leq T
with
s,t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
one has that

(4.21)

\displaystyle\sup_{N\geq 1}\mathbb{E}\big[(Q_{N}^{f}(t,\phi)-Q_{N}^{f}(s,\phi))^{k}\big]\leq C\|\phi\|^{k}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}(t-s)^{k/2}.

Furthermore fix
q>1
and
\varepsilon>0
. Then there exists
C=C(q,\varepsilon,k,T,F)>0
such that for all functions
\phi\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R})
and all
\varepsilon\leq s<t\leq T
one has

(4.22)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\big[(Q_{N}^{f}(t,\phi)-Q_{N}^{f}(s,\phi))^{k}\big]\leq C\|\phi\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R})}^{k}(t-s)^{k}.

Proof.

We are going to use the same notation and the same family of martingales from the proof of Lemma
4.5
. Using (
4.12
) together with the trivial bound
|\phi(N^{-1/2}(R^{j}(Nt_{j})-N^{3/4}t_{j}))|\leq\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}},
we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\big(Q_{N}^{f}(t,\phi)-Q_{N}^{f}(s,\phi)\big)^{k}]

\displaystyle\leq N^{-k/2}k!\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{k}\sum_{(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})\in\Delta_{k}^{N}[s,t]}\mathbf{E}_{RW^{(2k)}}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{k}\bigg\{f(R^{2j-1}_{Nt_{j}}-R^{2j}_{Nt_{j}})\prod_{i=0,1}D_{N,t_{j},N^{-1/2}(R^{2j-i}_{{Nt_{j}}}-d_{N}t_{j})}\bigg\}\bigg].

Now we actually claim, more generally than (
4.21
), that one has

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}\\
N\geq 1\end{subarray}}N^{-k/2}\sum_{(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})\in\Delta_{k}^{N}[s,t]}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(0,2k)}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{k}\bigg\{f(R^{2j-1}_{Nt_{j}}-R^{2j}_{Nt_{j}})\prod_{i=0,1}D_{N,t_{j},N^{-1/2}(R^{2j-i}_{{Nt_{j}}}-d_{N}t_{j})}\bigg\}\bigg]\leq C(t-s)^{k/2}.

The main idea in proving this is to proceed inductively in the variable
k
, applying similar arguments as in the proof of Lemma
4.5
to get rid of the constants by repeatedly applying the Markov property and writing these expressions in terms of the tilted measures
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}
of Definition
2.8
.

For clarity, write some of the details of this inductive argument. The claim is vacuously true when
k=0
, let us assume (
4.21
) has been proved up to
k-1
where
k\in\mathbb{N}.
By conditioning on time
t_{1}
, using the Markov property, and applying the inductive hypothesis for the conditioned expression on the time interval
[Nt_{1},Nt]
one will obtain uniformly over
\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}
and
N\geq 1
that

\displaystyle N

{}^{-k/2}\sum_{(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})\in\Delta_{k}^{N}[s,t]}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(0,2k)}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{k}\bigg\{f(R^{2j-1}_{Nt_{j}}-R^{2j}_{Nt_{j}})\prod_{i=0,1}D_{N,t_{j},N^{-1/2}(R^{2j-i}_{{Nt_{j}}}-d_{N}t_{j})}\bigg\}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq N^{-1/2}\sum_{t_{1}\in[s,t]\cap(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}(t-t_{1})^{(k-1)/2}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(0,2k)}\bigg[f(R^{1}_{Nt_{1}}-R^{2}_{Nt_{1}})\prod_{j=1}^{k}\bigg\{\prod_{i=0,1}D_{N,t_{1},N^{-1/2}(R^{2j-i}_{Nt_{1}}-d_{N}t_{1})}\bigg\}\bigg]

in other words all of the renormalizing constants have been “reduced” to time
t_{1}
and the inductive hypothesis has yielded the factor
(t-t_{1})^{(k-1)/2}
after summing out all other coordinates. Let us define the adapted process

\mathcal{H}_{N}(\mathbf{R},r):=\exp\bigg(\sum_{s=1}^{r}\bigg\{\log\mathbf{E}_{RW^{(2k)}}\big[e^{N^{\frac{1}{4p}}\sum_{j=1}^{2k}(R^{j}_{s}-R^{j}_{s-1})}\big|\mathcal{F}_{s-1}\big]\;-2k\log M(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}})\bigg\}\bigg).

Then the previous expression can be rewritten in terms of the tilted measures
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}
(see e.g. Lemma
2.6
) as

\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}},2k)}\bigg[N^{-1/2}\sum_{t_{1}\in[s,t]\cap(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})}(t-t_{1})^{(k-1)/2}\mathcal{H}_{N}(\mathbf{R},Nt_{1})f(R^{1}_{Nt_{1}}-R^{2}_{Nt_{1}})\bigg]

If we brutally bound
\mathcal{H}_{N}(\mathbf{R},Nt_{1})\leq\sup_{0\leq r\leq Nt}|\mathcal{H}_{N}(\mathbf{R},r)|:=\mathcal{H}_{N}^{\mathrm{sup}}(\mathbf{R},r)
, then an application of Corollary
2.22
will show that
\Gamma(q):=\sup_{N\geq 1}\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}},2k)}[\mathcal{H}_{N}^{\mathrm{sup}}(\mathbf{R},r)^{q}]<\infty
where
q\geq 1
is arbitrary. We can also use the bound
(t-t_{1})^{(k-1)/2}
by
(t-s)^{(k-1)/2},
thus by Cauchy-Schwarz the last expression can be bounded above

(t-s)^{(k-1)/2}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}},2k)}\bigg[\mathcal{H}_{N}^{\mathrm{sup}}(\mathbf{R},Nt)\cdot\nu^{f,1}_{N}([s,t])\bigg]\leq(t-s)^{(k-1)/2}\Gamma(2)^{1/2}\cdot\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}},2k)}\big[\nu^{f,1}_{N}([s,t])^{2}\big]^{1/2}

where we recall that the measure
\nu_{N}^{f,k}
was introduced in (
3.18
). By Corollary
2.20
it is immediate that
\sup_{N\geq 1}\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}},2k)}\big[\nu^{f,1}_{N}([s,t])^{2}\big]^{1/2}\leq C\sqrt{t-s},
which completes the inductive step and thus finishes the proof of (
4.21
).

For (
4.22
), the proof is completely analogous to a similar proof given in
[
DDP23b
, Eq. (5.43)]
but we rewrite some of the details here for completeness. Appealing to the moment formula for the increment of QVF from (
4.12
) and the convergence from (
4.13
) we have

(4.23)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\big(Q_{N}^{f}(t,\phi)-Q_{N}^{f}(s,\phi)\big)^{k}\bigg]=k!\gamma(\boldsymbol{\zeta})^{k}\cdot\mathbf{E}_{B^{\otimes 2k}}\bigg[\int_{\Delta_{k}[s,t]}e^{\gamma(\boldsymbol{\zeta})\mathcal{L}_{k}(\vec{t})}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\phi(U_{t_{j}}^{2j})dL_{0}^{U^{2j-1}-U^{2j}}(t_{j})\bigg],

where
\mathcal{L}_{k}(\vec{t})
is defined in (
4.14
). Now

[
DDP23b
, Lemma A.2]
formalizes the intuition that
dL_{0}^{U^{2j-1}-U^{2j}}(t_{j})=\delta_{0}(U_{t_{j}}^{2j-1}-U^{2j}_{t_{j}})
. Thus by appealing to that lemma, we can write the above expectation in terms of a certain family of concatenated bridge processes whose law we will write as
\mathbf{P}_{c}
. Specifically we have that

(4.24)

\displaystyle\mbox{right hand side~of \eqref{e.idef}}=\int_{\Delta_{k}[s,t]}\mathbf{E}_{c}\bigg[e^{\gamma(\boldsymbol{\zeta})\mathcal{L}_{k}(\vec{t})}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\phi(U^{2j}_{t_{j}})\bigg]\prod_{j=1}^{k}p_{2t_{j}}(0)dt_{1}\cdots dt_{k},

where the expectation is taken over a collection of paths
U^{j}
such that

•

(U^{2i-1}-U^{2i},U^{2i-1}+U^{2i})_{i=1}^{k}
are
2k
many independent processes.

•

U^{2i-1}+U^{2i}
is a Brownian motion of diffusion rate
2
for
i=1,2,\ldots,k
.

•

U^{2i-1}-U^{2i}
is a Brownian bridge (from
0
to
0
) of diffusion rate
2
from
[0,t_{i}]
and an independent Brownian motion of diffusion rate
2
from
[t_{i},\infty)
for
i=1,2,\ldots,k
.

We have used a different notation for the expectation operator
\mathbf{E}_{c}
in (
4.24
) (
c
for concatenation) just to stress that the law is different from the standard Brownian motion. We claim that for all
q>1
we have

(4.25)

\displaystyle\sup_{\vec{t}\in[0,T]}\mathbf{E}_{c}\bigg[e^{\gamma(\boldsymbol{\zeta})\mathcal{L}_{k}(\vec{t})}\prod_{j=1}^{k}|\phi(U^{2j}_{t_{j}})|\bigg]\leq C\cdot\|\phi\|_{L^{q}}^{k}.

where the
C>0
depends on
q,k,\varepsilon,T
. Let us assume (
4.25
) for the moment. By hypothesis,
t_{j}\geq\varepsilon>0
. Thus,
p_{2t_{j}}(0)\leq C
for some constant
C>0
depending on
\varepsilon
. Thus, in view of (
4.25
), to get an upper bound for the right-hand side of (
4.24
), we may take the supremum of the integrand in the right-hand side of (
4.24
) and pull it outside of the integration. As the Lebesgue measure of
\Delta_{k}[s,t]
is
\frac{(t-s)^{k}}{k!}
, we thus have the desired estimate in (
4.22
).

Let us now establish (
4.25
). Fix
q>1
and take
v>1
so that
(q^{\prime})^{-1}+q^{-1}=1
. Use Hölder’s inequality to write

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{c}\bigg[e^{\gamma(\boldsymbol{\zeta})\mathcal{L}_{k}(\vec{t})}\prod_{j=1}^{k}|\phi(U^{2j}_{t_{j}})|\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{E}_{c}\bigg[e^{q^{\prime}\gamma(\boldsymbol{\zeta})\mathcal{L}_{k}(\vec{t})}\bigg]^{1/q^{\prime}}\mathbf{E}_{c}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{k}|\phi(U^{2j}_{t_{j}})|^{q}\bigg]^{1/q}.

For the first expectation above, observe that by
[
DDP23b
, Lemma A.6]
,
\mathbf{E}_{c}\bigg[e^{q^{\prime}\gamma(\boldsymbol{\zeta})\mathcal{L}_{k}(\vec{t})}\bigg]
is uniformly bounded over
\vec{t}\in\Delta_{k}(0,T)
. For the second expectation above, note that under
\mathbf{E}_{c}
, we have

U_{t_{j}}^{2j}=\tfrac{1}{2}(U_{t_{j}}^{2j-1}+U_{t_{j}}^{2j})-\tfrac{1}{2}(U_{t_{j}}^{2j-1}-U_{t_{j}}^{2j})=\tfrac{1}{2}(U_{t_{j}}^{2j-1}+U_{t_{j}}^{2j})-0.

Thus under
\mathbf{E}_{c}
,
U_{t_{j}}^{2j}
are independent Gaussian random variables with variance
t_{j}/2
. Hence,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{c}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{k}|\phi(U^{2j}_{t_{j}})|^{q}\bigg]^{1/q}=\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathbf{E}_{c}\bigg[|\phi(U^{2j}_{t_{j}})|^{q}\bigg]^{1/q}=\prod_{j=1}^{k}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{t_{j}/2}(y)|\phi(y)|^{q}\,dy\bigg)^{1/q}\leq C\cdot\|\phi\|_{L^{q}}^{k}.

where the last inequality follows by again using the fact that
t_{j}\geq\varepsilon>0
, together with the uniform bound
\sup_{y}p_{t}(y)\leq(2\pi t)^{-1/2}
, and noting that the constant
C
is allowed to depend on
\varepsilon
. This establishes (
4.25
) completing the proof of (
4.22
).
∎

Lemma 4.8
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
. For all
T>0
and
q\geq 0
we have that

\sup_{0\leq t_{1}\leq...\leq t_{k}\leq T}\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{r\leq NT}\mathbf{E}_{RW^{(2k)}}\bigg[\bigg|N^{-1/2}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}|R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}|\bigg|^{q}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{k}D_{N,t_{j},N^{-1/2}(R^{j}_{Nt_{j}}-d_{N}t_{j})}\bigg]<\infty.

Proof.

The proof is very similar to the proofs of Lemma
4.5
and Proposition
4.7
, and we do not give the full argument. The sketch is as follows. One inducts on
k
to show more generally that

\sup_{0\leq t_{1}\leq...\leq t_{k}\leq T}\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in I^{k}}\sup_{r\leq NT}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(0,k)}\bigg[\bigg|N^{-1/2}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}|R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}-(x_{i}-x_{j})|\bigg|^{q}\prod_{j=1}^{k}D_{N,t_{j},N^{-1/2}(R^{j}_{Nt_{j}}-x_{j}-d_{N}t_{j})}\bigg]<\infty.

To perform the induction, one applies the Markov property and the inductive hypothesis to bound the expression on the time interval
[Nt_{1},NT]
, then one rewrites the remaining expectation in terms of the tilted measures
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(\beta,k)}
of Definition
2.8
. On the remaining time interval
[0,Nt_{1}]
this will yield an exponential term which we called
\mathcal{H}_{N}(\mathbf{R},r)
in the proof of Proposition
4.7
. This exponential term has uniform moment bounds as explained in that proof. The other term
N^{-1/2}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}|R^{i}_{r}-R^{j}_{r}|
also has uniform moment bounds using e.g. Corollaries
2.20
and
2.21
. Using Hölder’s inequality will then yield finiteness of the supremum to complete the inductive step.
∎

Recall the martingale field
M^{N}
from (
4.2
). The next few estimates will obtain a
L^{p}
bound on its predictable and optional quadratic variation, which will be the main tool in obtaining tightness for the field (
1.4
). First we will need to control the error terms appearing in Lemma
4.3
. For
\phi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
denote by
\|\phi\|_{C^{k}}=\|\phi\|_{C^{k}(\mathbb{R})}:=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\|\phi^{(j)}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})},
where
\phi^{(j)}(x)=\frac{d^{j}}{dx^{j}}\phi(x).

Proposition 4.9

(Controlling the error terms)
.

Let
\mathcal{E}^{j}_{N}

(1\leq j\leq 4)
be as in Lemma
4.3
. Let
q\geq 1
be an even integer, and let
T>0
. Then there exists
C>0
and a function
f:I\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
|f(x)|\leq F(|x|)
where
F:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
is decreasing and
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}F(k)<\infty
, such that uniformly over all
N\geq 1
and
s,t\in[0,T]\cap(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})
we have

\sum_{j=2,3,4}\mathbb{E}[|\mathcal{E}^{j}_{N}(t,\phi)-\mathcal{E}^{j}_{N}(s,\phi)|^{q}]^{1/q}\leq CN^{-\frac{1}{4p}}\mathbb{E}[|Q^{f}_{N}(t,\phi^{2})-Q^{f}_{N}(s,\phi^{2})|^{q}]^{1/q}+N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}|t-s|.

Furthermore, if
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, say
\phi
is supported on
[-J,J]
then

\mathbb{E}[|\mathcal{E}^{1}_{N}(t,\phi)-\mathcal{E}^{1}_{N}(s,\phi)|^{q}]^{1/q}\leq C\|\phi\|_{C^{2}}^{2}N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{E}[|Q^{f}_{N}(t,\mathbf{1}_{[-J,J]})-Q^{f}_{N}(s,\mathbf{1}_{[-J,J]})|^{q}]^{1/q}+C\|\phi\|_{C^{3}}^{2}N^{-\frac{1}{2}}|t-s|.

Proof.

As introduced earlier in this section, we let
H_{k}(x)=e^{x}-\sum_{n=0}^{k}\frac{x^{k}}{k!}
so that
|H_{k}(x)|\leq|x|^{k+1}e^{|x|}
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

First let us deal with
\mathcal{E}_{N}^{2}
. In the expression for
\mathcal{A}^{2}_{N}
preceding (
4.8
), use the fact that
|H_{2p}(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}(x_{j}-y_{j}))|\leq CN^{-\frac{(2p+1)}{4p}}|x_{j}-y_{j}|^{2p+1}e^{N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}|x_{j}-y_{j}|}
and we obtain uniformly over
N\geq 1
and
y_{1},y_{2}\in I
the brutal bound that

|\mathcal{A}_{N}^{2}(y_{1}-y_{2})|\leq N^{-\frac{(4p+2)}{4p}}\int_{I^{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{2}|x_{j}-y_{j}|e^{N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}|x_{j}-y_{j}|}\big|\rho\big((y_{1},y_{2}),(\mathrm{d}x_{1},\mathrm{d}x_{2})\big)\big|\leq CN^{-\frac{(4p+2)}{4p}}

where the integral can be bounded independently of
N,y_{1},y_{2}
using the definition (
4.5
) of
\rho
and e.g. Lemma
2.9
. From the above bound on
\mathcal{A}^{2}_{N}
it is immediate from (
4.8
) that

|\mathcal{E}^{2}_{N}(t,\phi)-\mathcal{E}_{2}^{N}(s,\phi)|\leq CN^{-\frac{(4p+2)}{4p}}e^{-2\log M(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}})}\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\sum_{r=Ns}^{Nt}\int_{I^{2}}Z_{N}(s,\mathrm{d}y_{1})Z_{N}(s,\mathrm{d}y_{2}).

Taking the
L^{2k}
-norm and applying Minkowski’s inequality to the sum over
r
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{E}^{2}_{N}(t,\phi)-\mathcal{E}_{2}^{N}(s,\phi)\big)^{2k}\big]^{1/(2k)}

\displaystyle\leq CN^{-\frac{(4p+2)}{4p}}e^{-2\log M(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}})}\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\sum_{r=Ns}^{Nt}\mathbf{E}_{RW^{(2k)}}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{2k}D_{N,N^{-1}r,N^{-1/2}(R^{j}_{r}-N^{-1}d_{N}r)}\bigg]^{1/(2k)}

where we applied the definition of
Z_{N}
as defined in (
4.1
). By Lemma
4.8
(with
q=0
) the summands are bounded by some absolute constant independently of
r,N\in\mathbb{N}
, consequently since the number of summands is
N(t-s)
the entire expression can be bounded above by
CN^{-\frac{1}{2p}}\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}|t-s|.
This is a bound of the desired form for
\mathcal{E}^{2}_{N}.

Let us now deal with
\mathcal{E}^{4}_{N}
. In light of (
4.8
), it suffices to show that there exists a decreasing function
F:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
with
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}F(k)<\infty
such that
\sup_{N\geq 1}N^{\frac{1}{4p}}|\mathcal{A}^{4}_{N}(y)|\leq F(|y|)
. Indeed, this would then yield a
pathwise
bound of the form

|\mathcal{E}^{j}_{N}(t,\phi)-\mathcal{E}^{j}_{N}(s,\phi)|\leq N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}|Q^{f}_{N}(t,\phi^{2})-Q^{f}_{N}(s,\phi^{2})|

where
f(x)=F(|x|)
, which certainly implies the claim. But the fact that
\sup_{N\geq 1}N^{\frac{1}{4p}}|\mathcal{A}^{4}_{N}(y)|\leq\mathrm{F_{decay}}(|y|)
is immediate from Assumption
1.2
Item (
5
) since the negative power of
N
in each of the summands is
(k_{1}+k_{2})/(4p)>1/2
(and
1/2
is the power in the definition of
Q_{N}^{f}).
This already gives the desired bound for
\mathcal{E}^{4}_{N}.

Next let us deal with
\mathcal{E}^{3}_{N}.
By writing
H_{2p}=(H_{2p}-H_{4p})+H_{4p},
we may rewrite

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{3}_{N}(y_{1}-y_{2})

\displaystyle=2\int_{I^{2}}H_{4p}\big(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}(x_{1}-y_{1})\big)\sum_{k=0}^{2p}\frac{N^{-\frac{k}{4p}}(x_{2}-y_{2})^{k}}{k!}\rho\big((y_{1},y_{2}),(\mathrm{d}x_{1},\mathrm{d}x_{2})\big)

\displaystyle\;\;+2\int_{I^{2}}\bigg(\sum_{k=2p+1}^{4p}\frac{N^{-\frac{k}{4p}}(x_{1}-y_{1})^{k}}{k!}\bigg)\bigg(\sum_{k=0}^{2p}\frac{N^{-\frac{k}{4p}}(x_{2}-y_{2})^{k}}{k!}\bigg)\rho\big((y_{1},y_{2}),(\mathrm{d}x_{1},\mathrm{d}x_{2})\big).

The first term on the right side has
|H_{4p}(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}(x_{j}-y_{j}))|\leq CN^{-\frac{(4p+1)}{4p}}|x_{j}-y_{j}|^{4p+1}e^{N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}|x_{j}-y_{j}|}
. Since the power of
N
is
-1-\frac{1}{4p}
, and since the number of summands is
N(t-s)
we can use brutal absolute value bounds to obtain the desired estimate using arguments very similar to the bound for
\mathcal{E}_{N}^{2}
above. For the second term on the right side, one will obtain a sum of polynomials
(x_{1}-y_{1})^{k_{1}}(x_{2}-y_{2})^{k_{2}}
multiplied by a factor
N^{-\frac{(k_{1}+k_{2})}{4p}}
with
k_{1}+k_{2}>2p
. For those terms where
k_{1}+k_{2}>4p
we can use brutal absolute value bounds to obtain the desired bound using arguments very similar to the bound for
\mathcal{E}_{N}^{2}
above. For the terms where
2p+1\leq k_{1}+k_{2}\leq 4p
note that these are polynomials of a similar form to
\mathcal{E}^{4}_{N}
, with all negative powers of
N
being strictly larger than
1/2.
Thus we can use an argument very similar to the one obtained in bounding
\mathcal{E}^{4}_{N}
, thus completing the argument for bounding
\mathcal{E}^{3}_{N}
by the desired quantity.

Finally let us bound
\mathcal{E}^{1}_{N}
. To do this we will need to work with
\mathcal{A}^{1}_{N}(\phi,y_{1},y_{2})
as defined just before (
4.9
). Let us perform a second-order Taylor expansion of
\phi
centered at
N^{-1/2}y_{1}
. Then the expression for
\mathcal{A}^{1}_{N}(\phi,y_{1},y_{2})
can be written as a sum
\mathcal{A}^{1,a}_{N}(\phi,y_{1},y_{2})+\mathcal{A}^{1,b}_{N}(\phi,y_{1},y_{2})
where

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{1,a}_{N}(\phi,y_{1},y_{2})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq k_{1},k_{2}\leq 2\\
k_{1}+k_{2}>0\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{k_{1}!k_{2}!}N^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})/2}\phi^{(k_{1})}(N^{-1/2}y_{1})\phi^{(k_{2})}(N^{-1/2}y_{1})

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\cdot\int_{I^{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{2}e^{N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}(x_{j}-y_{j})-\log M(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}})}\cdot(x_{1}-y_{1})^{k_{1}}(x_{2}-y_{1})^{k_{2}}\rho\big((y_{1},y_{2}),(\mathrm{d}x_{1},\mathrm{d}x_{2})\big)

and by Taylor’s theorem the other term
\mathcal{A}^{1,b}_{N}
is a “remainder” satisfying

|\mathcal{A}^{1,b}_{N}(\phi,y_{1},y_{2})|\leq N^{-3/2}\|\phi\|_{C^{3}}^{2}\int_{I^{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{2}e^{N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}|x_{j}-y_{j}|-\log M(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}})}\cdot|x_{1}-y_{1}|^{3}|x_{2}-y_{2}|^{3}\big|\rho\big((y_{1},y_{2}),(\mathrm{d}x_{1},\mathrm{d}x_{2})\big)\big|.

As in (
4.9
) we can write
\mathcal{E}^{1}_{N}=\mathcal{E}^{1,a}_{N}+\mathcal{E}^{1,b}_{N}
corresponding respectively to the contributions of
\mathcal{A}^{1,a}_{N}
and
\mathcal{A}^{1,b}_{N}
respectively.

The bound for
\mathcal{E}^{1,b}_{N}
is straightforward: since the power of
N
is
-3/2
, and since the number of summands in the expression for
\mathcal{E}^{1,b}_{N}
is
N(t-s)
, we can use brutal absolute value bounds to obtain the desired estimate using arguments very similar to the bound for
\mathcal{E}_{N}^{2}
above. By Lemma
2.9
, the integral is bounded independently of
y_{1},y_{2},N
.

Let us discuss the bound for
\mathcal{E}^{1,a}_{N}
. In the expression for
\mathcal{A}^{1,a}_{N}
, each of the terms
(x_{2}-y_{1})^{k_{2}}
can be further expanded out into a sum of terms of the form
(x_{2}-y_{2})^{i}(y_{1}-y_{2})^{k_{2}-i}
where
0\leq i\leq k_{2}.
Ultimately
\mathcal{A}^{1,a}_{N}
will be written as a sum of terms of the form

\displaystyle(y_{1}-y_{2})^{k_{2}-i}N^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})/2}

\displaystyle\phi^{(k_{1})}(N^{-1/2}y_{1})\phi^{(k_{2})}(N^{-1/2}y_{1})

\displaystyle\cdot\int_{I^{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{2}e^{N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}(x_{j}-y_{j})-\log M(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}})}\cdot(x_{1}-y_{1})^{k_{1}}(x_{2}-y_{2})^{i}\rho\big((y_{1},y_{2}),(\mathrm{d}x_{1},\mathrm{d}x_{2})\big)

where
0\leq k_{1}\leq 2
and
0\leq i\leq k_{2}\leq 2
.
Assuming that
\phi
is supported on
[-J,J]
we can then take the absolute value to bound the last expression by

\displaystyle|y_{1}-y_{2}|^{k_{2}-i}N^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})/2}

\displaystyle\|\phi\|_{C^{2}}^{2}\mathbf{1}_{[-J,J]}(N^{-1/2}y_{1})

\displaystyle\cdot\bigg|\int_{I^{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{2}e^{N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}(x_{j}-y_{j})-\log M(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}})}\cdot(x_{1}-y_{1})^{k_{1}}(x_{2}-y_{2})^{i}\rho\big((y_{1},y_{2}),(\mathrm{d}x_{1},\mathrm{d}x_{2})\big)\bigg|

All of this has accomplished the following: modulo the extra factor
|y_{1}-y_{2}|^{k_{2}-i}N^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})/2},
we now have an expression that is very similar to the expression for
\sum_{j=2,3,4}\mathcal{A}^{j}_{N}
which we already dealt with when bounding
\mathcal{E}^{2},\mathcal{E}^{3},\mathcal{E}^{4}
. Consequently, the same arguments that were used to obtain the bounds for
\mathcal{E}^{2},\mathcal{E}^{3},\mathcal{E}^{4}
may now be used to bound
\mathcal{A}^{1,a}_{N}
. More precisely, one expands
\prod_{j=1,2}e^{N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}(x_{j}-y_{j})}
as a truncated Taylor expansion, where the degree of the
(x_{1}-y_{1})
does not exceed
4p-k_{1}
and the degree of
(x_{2}-y_{2})
does not exceed
4p-i
. All of the polynomial terms of joint degree less than
2p
vanish thanks to Assumption
1.2
Item (
4
), only leaving the polynomials of joint degree
\geq 2p
, and the remainder which will contribute at worst an extra
N^{-1-\frac{1}{4p}}
. This will yield terms of the same form as those of
\mathcal{E}^{2}_{N}
and
\mathcal{E}^{4}_{N}
,
albeit
with the extra factor of
|y_{1}-y_{2}|^{k_{2}-i}N^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})/2}
in the front.

By considering all of the different cases of
(k_{1},k_{2},i)
, one verifies that the decay conditions on the function
\mathrm{F_{decay}}
in Item (
5
) of Assumption (
1.2
) are strong enough so that one may find
f
as in the proposition statement to bound
\mathcal{E}^{1,a}_{N}
. For instance if
k_{2}=2
, then the term
|y_{1}-y_{2}|^{k_{2}}
seems problematic, but the power of
N
will be less than
-1
in this case (thanks to the Taylor expansion of
\prod_{j=1,2}e^{N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}(x_{j}-y_{j})}
contributing some factors of
N^{-\frac{k}{4p}}
), and since
x\mapsto x^{2}\mathrm{F_{decay}}(x)
is bounded on
[0,\infty)
as can be verified from Item (
5
) of Assumption (
1.2
), a brutal absolute value bound will suffice (similar to dealing with the terms of type
\mathcal{E}^{2}_{N}
above). The worst terms will actually occur when
k_{2}=1
and
i=k_{1}=0
, as the power of
N
is exactly
-1
in this case, so that extracting an extra factor of
N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}
is not possible to obtain brutal absolute value bounds. Instead, the condition that
\int_{0}^{\infty}x\mathrm{F_{decay}}(x)<\infty
ensures that we can take
f(x):=(1+|x|)\mathrm{F_{decay}}(|x|),
and all of the polynomials terms in the Taylor expansion of
\prod_{j=1,2}e^{N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}(x_{j}-y_{j})}
will guarantee that we can obtain a bound of the form
N^{-1/2}|Q^{f}_{N}(t,\mathbf{1}_{[-J,J]})-Q^{f}_{N}(s,\mathbf{1}_{[-J,J]})|
. By Assumption
1.2
Item (
5
), this
f
is eventually decreasing and thus bounded above by some decreasing function
F
that is still integrable, as required in the proposition statement. Meanwhile the remainder terms of the Taylor expansion ca be dealt with using admit brutal absolute value bounds as we saw earlier, because they come with a factor of
N^{-1-\frac{1}{4p}}

in addition
to the factor of
|y_{1}-y_{2}|^{k_{2}-i}N^{-(k_{1}+k_{2})/2}.
Using Lemma
4.8
with
q=0,1
, or
2
(depending on the value of
k_{2}-i)
, one may easily deal with such remainder terms, thus finishing the proof of the required bound for
\mathcal{E}^{1,a}_{N}.

∎

Corollary 4.10

(Predictable quadratic variation bound)
.

Let
q\geq 1
be an even integer, and let
T>0
. Let
M^{N}(\phi)
be the martingale defined in (
4.2
), and let
\langle M^{N}(\phi)\rangle
denote its predictable quadratic variation as in (
4.4
). Then there exists
C>0
and exists
C>0
and a function
f:I\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
|f(x)|\leq F(|x|)
where
F:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
is decreasing and
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}F(k)<\infty
, such that we have the bound uniformly over all
N\geq 1
, all
s,t\in[0,T]\cap(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}),
and all
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
supported on
[-J,J]

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\big|\langle M^{N}(\phi)\rangle_{t}-\langle M^{N}(\phi)\rangle_{s}\big|^{q}\big]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\phi\|_{C^{2}}^{2}\mathbb{E}[|Q^{f}_{N}(t,\mathbf{1}_{[-J,J]})-Q^{f}_{N}(s,\mathbf{1}_{[-J,J]})|^{q}]^{1/q}+C\|\phi\|_{C^{3}}^{2}N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}|t-s|

\displaystyle\leq C\|\phi\|_{C^{3}}^{2}|t-s|^{1/2}.

Proof.

The first bound is immediate from Lemma
4.3
and Proposition
4.9
. The second inequality follows from (
4.21
) and the fact that
|t-s|\leq C|t-s|^{1/2}
for
s,t\in[0,T]
.
∎

Although the predictable quadratic variation defined in (
4.4
) is a useful object, there is another version of the quadratic variation that is better for obtaining tightness estimates via Burkholder-Davis-Gundy. This is the
optional
quadratic variation, defined as

\displaystyle[M^{N}(\phi)]_{t}=\sum_{s=1}^{Nt}\big(M^{N}_{sN^{-1}}(\phi)-M^{N}_{(s-1)N^{-1}}(\phi)\big)^{2}.

Proposition 4.11

(Optional quadratic variation bound)
.

Fix
q\geq 1
and

T>0
. For all
s,t\in(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})\cap[0,T]
and all
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
we have that

\mathbb{E}\big[\big|[M^{N}(\phi)]_{t}-[M^{N}(\phi)]_{s}\big|^{q}\big]^{1/q}\leq C\|\phi\|_{C^{3}}^{2}|t-s|^{1/2}.

Here
C
is independent of
N,\phi,s,t
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the claim when
p=2k
for some positive integer
k
. For any discrete time martingale
M
note that
M^{2}-[M]
and
M^{2}-\langle M\rangle
are both martingales, therefore their difference
[M]-\langle M\rangle
is also a martingale. For
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
we thus define the martingale

G^{N}_{t}(\phi):=[M^{N}(\phi)]_{t}-\langle M^{N}(\phi)\rangle_{t}.

Using Proposition
4.10
, the proposition will be proved if we can show that

\mathbb{E}\big[\big(G^{N}_{t}(\phi)-G^{N}_{s}(\phi)\big)^{2k}\big]\leq C\|\phi\|_{C^{3}}^{4k}(t-s)^{k}.
By Burkholder-Davis-Gundy, it in turn suffices to show that

(4.26)

\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{r=Ns}^{Nt-1}\big(G^{N}_{N^{-1}(r+1)}(\phi)-G^{N}_{N^{-1}r}(\phi)\big)^{2}\bigg)^{k}\bigg]\leq C\|\phi\|_{C^{3}}^{4k}(t-s)^{k}.

Recall from (
4.3
) that

\displaystyle(M^{N}_{t+N^{-1}}(\phi)-M^{N}_{t}(\phi))^{2}=\bigg(\int_{I}\int_{I}\phi(N^{-1/2}x)e^{N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}(x-y)-\log M(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}})}\big[K_{r+1}(y,\mathrm{d}x)-\mu(\mathrm{d}x-y)\big]Z_{N}(r,\mathrm{d}y)\bigg)^{2}

For
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
let us now define the signed (random) measure
H_{r+1}(y,\mathrm{d}x):=K_{r+1}(y,\mathrm{d}x)-\mu(\mathrm{d}x-y).
Note by Assumption
1.2
Item (
3
) that
\int_{I}(y-x)^{j}H_{r+1}(y,\mathrm{d}x)=0
for all
0\leq j\leq p-1
a.s.. Then define the signed (random) measure on
I^{2}

\Phi_{r+1}\big((y_{1},y_{2}),(\mathrm{d}x_{1},\mathrm{d}x_{2})\big):=H_{r+1}(y_{1},\mathrm{d}x_{1})H_{r+1}(y_{2},\mathrm{d}x_{2})\;\;\;-\;\;\;\rho\big((y_{1},y_{2}),(\mathrm{d}x_{1},\mathrm{d}x_{2})\big),

where
\rho
is given by (
4.5
) as always.
If
r=Nt
, then from the above expression for
(M^{N}_{t+N^{-1}}(\phi)-M^{N}_{t}(\phi))^{2}
, it is clear that
G^{N}_{t+N^{-1}}(\phi)-G_{t}^{N}(\phi)
can be rewritten as

(4.27)

\int_{I^{2}}\bigg(\int_{I^{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{2}\phi(N^{-1/2}x_{j})e^{N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}(x_{j}-y_{j})-\log M(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}})}\Phi_{r+1}\big((y_{1},y_{2}),(\mathrm{d}x_{1},\mathrm{d}x_{2})\big)\bigg)Z_{N}(r,\mathrm{d}y_{1})Z_{N}(r,\mathrm{d}y_{2}).

Let us therefore define

(4.28)

\mathpzc A_{N}(r,\phi,y_{1},y_{2}):=\int_{I^{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{2}\phi(N^{-1/2}x_{j})e^{N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}(x_{j}-y_{j})-\log M(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}})}\Phi_{r+1}\big((y_{1},y_{2}),(\mathrm{d}x_{1},\mathrm{d}x_{2})\big).

For
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
j\in\mathbb{N}
let us now define a
deterministic
collection of signed measures on
I^{4(j-1)}
indexed by
\mathbf{y}\in I^{4j}
:

\varrho_{\phi}^{j}((y_{1},...,y_{4j}),(\mathrm{d}x_{1},...,\mathrm{d}x_{4(j-1)})):=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\mathpzc A_{N}(r,\phi,y_{1},y_{2})\mathpzc A_{N}(r,\phi,y_{3},y_{4})\prod_{j=1}^{4(j-1)}\frac{e^{N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}(x_{j}-y_{4+j})}}{M(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}})}K_{r+1}(y_{4+j},\mathrm{d}x_{j})\bigg].

For
j=1
, we remark that
\rho^{j}_{\phi}
is just a function of four variables
(y_{1},...,y_{4})
rather than a measure, and the product should be interpreted as
1
. The reason that this measure does not depend on
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
is because, for each
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, the random variable inside the expectation is a (fixed) measurable function of the kernels
\{K_{r+1}(x,\cdot)\}_{x\in I},
whose distribution does not depend on
r
by Assumption
1.2
Item (
1
).
To prove (
4.26
), it turns out that brutal absolute value bounds of the
\varrho^{j}_{\phi}
will be sufficient without any additional arguments needed.

Fix
j\in\mathbb{N}
. We claim that there exists
C>0
such that uniformly over
y_{1},...,y_{4j}\in I
, and
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})

(4.29)

\displaystyle\|\rho_{\phi}^{j}\big((y_{1},...,y_{4j}),\bullet\big)\|_{TV}\leq C\|\phi\|_{C^{3}}^{4}

\displaystyle\bigg(1+N^{-1/2}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq 4}|y_{i}-y_{j}|\bigg)^{4}N^{-1}.

To prove this, we claim a decomposition of
\mathpzc A_{N}(r,\phi,y_{1},y_{2})
as the sum

\displaystyle\mathpzc A

{}_{N}^{rem}(r,\phi,y_{1},y_{2})

\displaystyle+e^{-2\log M(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}})}\sum_{0\leq\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\leq 2}\bigg\{\phi^{(\ell_{1})}(N^{-1/2}y_{1})\phi^{(\ell_{2})}(N^{-1/2}y_{1})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\leq k_{1},k_{2}\leq 2p\\
k_{3}\leq\ell_{2}\end{subarray}}N^{-\frac{(k_{1}+k_{2})}{4p}-\frac{(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2})}{2}}c_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}\mathpzc A^{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}(r,y_{1},y_{2})\bigg\}

where
c_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}
are deterministic constants not depending on
N
, where

\displaystyle\mathpzc A^{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}(r,y_{1},y_{2}):=(y_{1}-y_{2})^{k_{3}}\int_{I^{2}}(x_{1}-y_{1})^{k_{1}}(x_{2}-y_{2})^{k_{2}}\Phi_{r+1}\big((y_{1},y_{2}),(\mathrm{d}x_{1},\mathrm{d}x_{2})\big).

and where the “remainder” term
\mathpzc A^{rem}_{N}
satisfies for any
q\geq 1

\sup_{r}\mathbb{E}[|\mathpzc A^{rem}_{N}(r,\phi,y_{1},y_{2})|^{q}]^{1/q}\leq CN^{-1-\frac{1}{4p}}\bigg(1+N^{-1/2}|y_{1}-y_{2}|\bigg)^{2}\|\phi\|_{C^{3}}^{2}.

Indeed, this decomposition follows using exactly the same arguments as in deriving the error decomposition in (
4.9
). More precisely, in (
4.28
) one writes
e^{N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}(x_{j}-y_{j})}
as the sum
H_{2p}(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}(x_{j}-y_{j}))+\sum_{k=0}^{2p}\frac{1}{k!}N^{-\frac{k}{4p}}(x_{j}-y_{j})^{k},
then Taylor expanding each copy of
\phi
around
N^{-1/2}y_{1}
exactly as we did in the proof of Proposition
4.9
while bounding
\mathcal{E}^{1}_{N}
, and then noting that the constants
c_{k,\ell,m}
can be obtained from combining all of the relevant Taylor coefficients of a given pair of exponents
k,\ell,m
. If either of
k_{1}
or
k_{2}
is less than
p
the contribution in (
4.28
) would vanish a.s. by Assumption
1.2
Item (
3
), hence why we have the summation over
p\leq k_{1},k_{2}\leq 2p
rather than
0\leq k_{1},k_{2}\leq 2p
in the decomposition above. The remainder terms
\mathpzc A^{rem}_{N}
will come from exactly those terms in the expansion where the negative power of
N
exceeds
1
.

In the decomposition of
\mathpzc A_{N}
above, the exponent of
N
is always less than or equal to
-1/2
, which is why when one takes the product of two separate copies of
\mathpzc A_{N}
as in the definition of
\varrho_{\phi}^{j}
, one always obtains a factor of
N^{-1}
at most. Thus (
4.29
) may be justified by expanding everything out using the decomposition of
\mathpzc A_{N}
, then using all of the conditions of Assumption
1.2
. We furthermore note that
\big(1+N^{-1/2}|y_{1}-y_{2}|\big)\big(1+N^{-1/2}|y_{3}-y_{4}|\big)\leq\big(1+N^{-1/2}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq 4}|y_{i}-y_{j}|\big)^{2}
, thus explaining we we have the fourth power term in (
4.29
) when using Cauchy-Schwarz for the remainder terms. This sketch verifies the main idea of obtaining (
4.29
), and we will not write the full remaining details here.

With (
4.29
) proved, we will now move onto the proof of (
4.26
). The proof proceeds by using a straightforward induction on
k
, using the Markov property and then (
4.29
) repeatedly. We will illustrate the proof for
k=2
for clarity. Use (
4.27
) and (
4.28
) to write

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum_{r=Ns}^{Nt-1}\big(G^{N}_{N^{-1}(r+1)}(\phi)-G^{N}_{N^{-1}r}(\phi)\big)^{2}\bigg)^{2}

\displaystyle=2\sum_{Ns\leq r_{1}\leq r_{2}<Nt}\int_{I^{8}}\prod_{j=1}^{2}\bigg\{\mathpzc A_{N}(r_{j},\phi,y_{4j-1},y_{4j})\mathpzc A_{N}(r_{j},\phi,y_{4j-3},y_{4j-2})\prod_{i=0}^{3}Z_{N}(r_{j},\mathrm{d}y_{4j-i})\bigg\}.

Denote by
(R_{r}^{1},...,R_{r}^{8})_{r\geq 0}
the canonical process on
(I^{8})^{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
and denote by
X_{N}^{j}(t):=N^{-1/2}(R^{j}_{Nt}-d_{N}t)
. Using the definition (
4.1
) of
Z_{N}
and taking the expectation of the last quantity yields

\displaystyle 2\sum_{Ns\leq r_{1}\leq r_{2}<Nt}

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{RW^{(8)}}\bigg[\int_{I^{4}}\mathscr{H}_{N}(r_{2}-r_{1},\mathbf{x})\varrho^{2}_{\phi}\bigg(\big(X_{N}^{1}(N^{-1}r_{1}),...,X_{N}^{8}(N^{-1}r_{1})\big),\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\bigg)\prod_{i=0}^{7}D_{N,N^{-1}r_{1},N^{-1/2}(R_{r_{1}}^{i}-N^{-1}d_{N}r_{1})}\bigg].

where

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}_{N}(r,\mathbf{x}):=\mathbf{E}

{}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(0,4)}\bigg[\varrho^{1}_{\phi}\big(X_{N}^{1}(N^{-1}r),...,X_{N}^{4}(N^{-1}r)\big)\prod_{i=1,2,3,4}D_{N,N^{-1}r,N^{-1/2}(R_{r}^{i}-x_{j}-N^{-1}d_{N}r)}\bigg],

and the expectation is with respect to the
k
-point motion process starting from
\mathbf{x}
(see Definition
2.8
for these path measures).
Using (
4.29
) to bound
\varrho^{1}_{\phi}
, then applying Lemma
4.8
, one sees that

\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in I^{4}}\sup_{r\in[Ns,Nt]\cap(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})}|\mathscr{H}_{N}(r,\mathbf{x})|\leq C\|\phi\|_{C^{3}}^{4}N^{-1}.

Plugging this bound back into the previous expression, one will obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum_{r=Ns}^{Nt-1}\big(G^{N}_{N^{-1}(r+1)}(\phi)-G^{N}_{N^{-1}r}(\phi)\big)^{2}\bigg)^{2}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq C\|\phi\|_{C^{3}}^{4}N^{-1}\sum_{Ns\leq r_{1}\leq r_{2}<Nt}\mathbf{E}_{RW^{(8)}}\bigg[\bigg\|\varrho^{2}_{\phi}\bigg(\big(X_{N}^{1}(N^{-1}r_{1}),...,X_{N}^{8}(N^{-1}r_{1})\big),\bullet\bigg)\bigg\|_{TV}\prod_{i=1}^{8}D_{N,N^{-1}r_{1},N^{-1/2}(R_{r_{1}}^{i}-N^{-1}d_{N}r_{1})}\bigg].

Applying (
4.26
) again, then applying Lemma
4.8
again, one sees that the expectations are bounded by
CN^{-1}\|\phi\|_{C^{3}}^{2}
independently of
r_{1}
. Consequently one obtains a bound of the form
\sum_{Ns\leq r_{1}\leq r_{2}<Nt}CN^{-2}\|\phi\|_{C^{3}}^{8}\leq C\|\phi\|_{C^{3}}^{8}(t-s)^{2}
as desired.

For
k>2
the proof is similar, using the inductive structure where one first bounds
\varrho^{1}_{\phi},
then
\varrho^{2}_{\phi},
then
\varrho^{3}_{\phi},
and so on. At each step, one uses (
4.26
) in conjunction with Lemma
4.8
, and this will pick up a factor of
\|\phi\|_{C^{3}}^{4}(t-s)
for that particular step.
∎

5.
Tightness of the rescaled field and identification of the limit points

In this section we will finally prove Theorem
1.4
using the estimates of the previous section.

5.1.
Weighted Hölder spaces and Schauder estimates

We now introduce various natural topologies for our field
\mathscr{U}_{N}
and its limit points. We then discuss how the heat flow affects these topologies and record Kolmogorov-type lemmas that will be key in showing tightness under these topologies. We begin by recalling many familiar and useful spaces of continuous and differentiable functions that have natural metric structures.
For
d\geq 1
, we denote by
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the space of all compactly supported smooth functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For a smooth function on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we define its
C^{r}
norm as

\displaystyle\|f\|_{C^{r}}:=\sum_{\vec{\alpha}}\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}|D^{\vec{\alpha}}f(\mathbf{x})|

where the sum is over all
\vec{\alpha}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{d}
with
\sum\alpha_{i}\leq r
and
D^{\vec{\alpha}}:=\partial_{x_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots\partial_{x_{d}}^{\alpha_{d}}
denotes the mixed partial derivative.

We now recall the definition of weighted Hölder spaces from
[
HL15
, Definitions 2.2 and 2.3]
. For the remainder of this paper, we shall work with
elliptic and parabolic
weighted Hölder spaces with polynomial weight function

\displaystyle w(x):=(1+x^{2})^{\tau}

for some fixed
\tau>1
. We introduce these weights because weighted spaces will be more convenient to obtain tightness estimates. Since the solution of (
1.5
) started from Dirac initial condition is known to be globally bounded away from
(0,0)
, we expect that it is possible to remove the weights throughout this section, but this would require more precise moment estimates than the ones we derived in previous sections, which take into account spatial decay of the fields.

Definition 5.1

(Elliptic Hölder spaces)
.

For
\alpha\in(0,1)
we define the space
C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})
to be the completion of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
with respect to the norm given by

\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})}:=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\frac{|f(x)|}{w(x)}+\sup_{|x-y|\leq 1}\frac{|f(x)-f(y)|}{w(x)|x-y|^{\alpha}}.

For
\alpha<0
we let
r=-\lfloor\alpha\rfloor
and we define
C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})
to be the completion of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
with respect to the norm

\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})}:=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\sup_{\lambda\in(0,1]}\sup_{\phi\in B_{r}}\frac{(f,S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}}{w(x)\lambda^{\alpha}}

where the scaling operators
S^{\lambda}_{x}
are defined by

(5.1)

\displaystyle S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi(y)=\lambda^{-1}\phi(\lambda^{-1}(x-y)),

and where
B_{r}
is the set of all smooth functions of
C^{r}
norm less than 1 with support contained in the unit ball of
\mathbb{R}
.

One may verify that these spaces embed continuously into
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
.

Definition 5.2

(Function spaces)
.

Let
C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})
be as in Definition
5.1
. We define
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
to be the space of continuous maps
g:[0,T]\to C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}),
equipped with the norm

\|g\|_{C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))}:=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|g(t)\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})}.

Here and henceforth we will define
\Psi_{[a,b]}:=[a,b]\times\mathbb{R}
and we will define
\Psi_{T}:=\Psi_{[0,T]}.

So far we have used
\phi,\psi
for test functions on
\mathbb{R}
. To make the distinction clear between test functions on
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we shall use variant Greek letters such as
\varphi,\vartheta,\varrho
for test functions on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. In many instances below, we will explicitly write
(f,\varphi)_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}
or
(g,\phi)_{\mathbb{R}}
when we want to be clear about the space in which we are applying the
L^{2}
-pairing.

Definition 5.3

(Parabolic Hölder spaces)
.

We define
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Psi_{T})
to be the set of functions on
\Psi_{T}
that are restrictions to
\Psi_{T}
of some function in
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, in particular we do not impose that elements of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Psi_{T})
vanish at the boundaries of
\Psi_{T}.

For
\alpha\in(0,1)
we define the space
C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})
to be the completion of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Psi_{T})
with respect to the norm

\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})}:=\sup_{(t,x)\in\Psi_{T}}\frac{|f(t,x)|}{w(x)}+\sup_{|s-t|^{1/2}+|x-y|\leq 1}\frac{|f(t,x)-f(s,y)|}{w(x)(|t-s|^{1/2}+|x-y|)^{\alpha}}.

For
\alpha<0
we let
r=-\lfloor\alpha\rfloor
and we define
C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})
to be the completion of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Psi_{T})
with respect to the norm

\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})}:=\sup_{(t,x)\in\Psi_{T}}\sup_{\lambda\in(0,1]}\sup_{\varphi\in B_{r}}\frac{(f,S^{\lambda}_{(t,x)}\varphi)_{L^{2}(\Psi_{T})}}{w(x)\lambda^{\alpha}}

where the scaling operators are defined by

(5.2)

\displaystyle S^{\lambda}_{(t,x)}\varphi(s,y)=\lambda^{-3}\varphi(\lambda^{-2}(t-s),\lambda^{-1}(x-y)),

and where
B_{r}
is the set of all smooth functions of
C^{r}
norm less than 1 with support contained in the unit ball of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

An important property of both the parabolic and elliptic spaces is that one has a continuous embedding
C^{\alpha,\tau}\hookrightarrow C^{\beta,\tau}
whenever
\beta<\alpha.
In fact this embedding is compact, though we will not use this. We also have the following embedding of function spaces inside parabolic spaces.

Lemma 5.4
.

For
\alpha<0,\tau>0
one has a continuous embedding
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))\hookrightarrow C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})
given by identifying
v=(v(t))_{t\in[0,T]}
with the tempered space-time distribution given by

(v,\varphi)_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}=\int_{0}^{T}(v(t),\varphi(t,\cdot))_{\mathbb{R}}dt.

The proof is straightforward from the definitions and is omitted.

Remark 5.5

(Derivatives of distributions)
.

Let
\alpha<0
. By definition, any element
f\in C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha,\tau}(\Psi_{T})
admits an
L^{2}
-pairing with any smooth function
\varphi:\Psi_{T}\to\mathbb{R}
of rapid decay (i.e.,
\varphi
is a Schwarz function restricted to
\Psi_{T}
). We will write this pairing as
(f,\varphi)_{\Psi_{T}}.
Consequently there is a natural embedding
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha,\tau}(\Psi_{T})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
which is defined by formally setting
f
to be zero outside of
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
. More rigorously, this means that the
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
-pairing of
f
with any
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is defined to be equal to
(f,\varphi|_{\Psi_{T}})_{\Psi_{T}}
.

The image of this embedding consists of some specific collection of tempered distributions that are necessarily supported on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}.
Thus we can sensibly define
\partial_{t}f
and
\partial_{x}f
as elements of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
whenever
f\in C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha,\tau}(\Psi_{T})
, by the formulas

(\partial_{t}f,\varphi)_{\Psi_{T}}:=-(f,\partial_{t}\varphi)_{\Psi_{T}},\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;(\partial_{x}f,\varphi)_{\Psi_{T}}:=-(f,\partial_{x}\varphi)_{\Psi_{T}}

for all smooth
\varphi:\Psi_{T}\to\mathbb{R}
of rapid decay. This convention on derivatives will be useful for certain computations later. From the definitions, when
\alpha<0
one can check that for such
f
one necessarily has
\partial_{t}f\in C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha-2,\tau}(\Psi_{T})
and
\partial_{x}f\in C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha-1,\tau}(\Psi_{T}).

The latter statement fails for
\alpha>0
. Indeed by our convention of derivatives,
\partial_{t}f
may no longer be a smooth function (or even a function) even if
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Psi_{T})
. This is because such an
f
gets extended to all of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
by setting it to be zero outside
\Psi_{T}
. In particular, if
f
does not vanish on the boundary of
\Psi_{T}
, then it may become a discontinuous function under our convention of extension to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Due to these discontinuities, the distributional derivative
\partial_{t}f
can be a tempered distribution with singular parts along the boundaries. In our later computations, we never take derivatives of functions in
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha,\tau}(\Psi_{T})
with
\alpha>0
. Sometimes we will write
\partial_{s}
to mean the same thing as
\partial_{t}
.

Proposition 5.6

(Smoothing effect of the heat flow on elliptic spaces)
.

For
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
t>0
define

P_{t}f(x):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{t}(x-y)f(y)dy.

Then for all
\alpha\leq\beta<1
(possibly negative), there exists
C=C(\alpha,\beta,T)>0
such that

\|P_{t}f\|_{C^{\beta,\tau}(\mathbb{R})}\leq Ct^{-(\beta-\alpha)/2}\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})}

uniformly over
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}
