and
t\in[0,T]
. In particular,
P_{t}
extends to a globally defined linear operator on
C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})
which maps boundedly into
C^{\beta,\tau}(\mathbb{R}).

A proof may be found in
[
HL15
, Lemma 2.8]
in the case of an exponential weight. The proof for polynomial weights is very similar.

For the parabolic Hölder spaces, the following lemma states that the heat flow improves the regularity by a factor of
2
and provides a Schauder-type estimate.

Proposition 5.7

(Schauder estimate)
.

For
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Psi_{T})
let us define

(5.3)

\displaystyle Kf(t,x):=\int_{\Psi_{T}}p_{t-s}(x-y)f(s,y)dsdy,

where
p_{t}
is the standard heat kernel for
t>0,x\in\mathbb{R}
and
p_{t}(x):=0
for
t<0.
Then for all
\alpha<-1
with
\alpha\notin\mathbb{Z}
there exists
C=C(\alpha)>0
independent of
f
such that

\|Kf\|_{C^{\alpha+2,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})}\leq C\cdot\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})}.

In particular
K
extends to a globally defined linear operator on
C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})
that maps boundedly into
C^{\alpha+2,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T}).
Furthermore, if
f\in C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Lambda_{T})
, then
K(\partial_{t}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2})f=f
.

It is important to note that the last statement is only true because of our convention on distributional derivatives that we have explained in Remark
5.5
. Without that convention, that statement would be false even for a smooth function
f
that does not vanish on the line
\{t=0\}
.

Proof.

See
[
DDP23b
, Proposition 6.6]
for the proof.
∎

Corollary 5.8
.

Define
J:C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})\to C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha,\tau}(\Psi_{T})
by

Jf(t,x):=\big(f,p_{t}(x-\cdot)\big)_{\mathbb{R}},

J
is a bounded linear operator for any
\alpha<1
and any
\tau>0.
Consider the operator
\widehat{P}_{t}:=JP_{t}
with
P_{t}
defined in Proposition
5.6
. By the semigroup property of the heat kernel, we have that

(5.4)

\displaystyle\widehat{P}_{s}f(t,x)=(Jf)(t+s,x).

Furthermore, we have the operator norm bound

(5.5)

\displaystyle\|\widehat{P}_{t}f\|_{C^{\beta,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})}\leq C\cdot t^{-(\beta-\alpha)/2}\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})},

where
C=C(\alpha,\beta,T)>0
does not depend on
t
and
f
.

Proof.

The first part follows by using the Schauder estimate in Proposition
5.7
, noting that
Jf=K(\delta_{0}\otimes f)
where for
f\in C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})
the latter distribution is defined by
(\delta_{0}\otimes f,\varphi)_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}:=(f,\varphi(0,\cdot))_{\mathbb{R}}.
Directly from the definitions one can check that
f\mapsto\delta_{0}\otimes f
is bounded from
C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})\to C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha-2,\tau}(\Psi_{T}).
Finally, (
5.5
) follows from Proposition
5.6
.
∎

We next define a space-time distribution that is supported on a single temporal cross-section:

Definition 5.9
.

Let
\alpha<0
. Given some
f\in C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})
and
b\in[0,T]
we define
\delta_{b}\otimes f\in C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha-2,\tau}(\Psi_{T})
by the formula

(\delta_{b}\otimes f,\varphi)_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}:=(f,\varphi(b,\cdot))_{\mathbb{R}}.

Directly from the definitions of the scaling operators in (
5.1
) and (
5.2
), it is clear that for fixed
b\in[0,T]
, the linear map
f\mapsto\delta_{b}\otimes f
is bounded from
C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})\to C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha-2,\tau}(\Psi_{T})
as long as
\alpha<0.
It is also clear that
\delta_{b}\otimes f
is necessarily supported on the line
\{b\}\times\mathbb{R}
.

We end this subsection by recording a Kolmogorov-type lemma for the three spaces introduced at the beginning of this subsection. It will be crucial in proving tightness in those respective spaces.

Lemma 5.10

(Kolmogorov lemma)
.

Let
L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
be the space of all random variables defined on a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
with finite second moment. We have the following:

(a)

(Function space)
Let
(t,\phi)\mapsto V(t,\phi)
be a map from
[0,T]\times\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
into
L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
which is linear and continuous in
\phi
. Fix a non-negative integer
r
. Assume there exists some
\kappa>0,p>1/\kappa
and
\alpha<-r
and
C=C(\kappa,\alpha,p,{T})>0
such that one has

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}[|V(t,S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi)|^{p}]^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq C\lambda^{\alpha},

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}[|V(t,S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi)-V(s,S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi)|^{p}]^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq C\lambda^{\alpha-\kappa}|t-s|^{\kappa},

uniformly over all smooth functions
\phi
on
\mathbb{R}
supported on the unit ball of
\mathbb{R}
with
\|\phi\|_{C^{r}}\leq 1
, and uniformly over
\lambda\in(0,1]
and
0\leq s,t\leq T
. Then for any
\tau>1
and any
\beta<\alpha-\kappa
there exists a random variable
\big(\mathscr{V}(t)\big)_{t\in[0,T]}
taking values in
C([0,T],C^{\beta,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
such that
(\mathscr{V}(t),\phi)=V(t,\phi)
almost surely for all
\phi
and
t
. Furthermore, one has that

\mathbf{E}[\|\mathscr{V}\|^{p}_{C([0,T],C^{\beta,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))}]\leq C^{\prime},

where
C^{\prime}
depends on the choice of
\alpha,\beta,p,\kappa,
and the constant
C
appearing in the moment bound above but not on
V,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}
.

(b)

(Parabolic Hölder Space) Let
\varphi\mapsto V(\varphi)
be a bounded linear map from
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
to
L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
. Assume
V(\varphi)=0
for all
\varphi
with support contained in the complement of
\Psi_{T}
. Recall
S^{\lambda}_{(t,x)}
from (
5.2
). Fix a non-negative integer
r
. Assume there exists some
p>1
and
\alpha<0
and
C=C(\alpha,p)>0
such that one has

\mathbf{E}[|V(S^{\lambda}_{(t,x)}\varphi)|^{p}]^{1/p}\leq C\lambda^{\alpha},

uniformly over all smooth functions
\varphi
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
supported on the unit ball of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
\|\varphi\|_{C^{r}}\leq 1
, and uniformly over
\lambda\in(0,1]
and
(t,x)\in\Psi_{T}
. Then for any
\tau>1
and any
\beta<\alpha-3/p
there exists a random variable
\mathscr{V}
taking values in
C^{\beta,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})
such that
(\mathscr{V},\varphi)=V(\varphi)
almost surely for all
\varphi.
Furthermore one has that

\mathbf{E}[\|\mathscr{V}\|^{p}_{C^{\beta,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})}]\leq C^{\prime},

where
C^{\prime}
depends on the choice of
\alpha,p,
and the constant
C
appearing in the moment bound above but not on
V,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}
.

A proof of the above results may be adapted from the proof of Lemma 9 in Section 5 of
[
MW17
]
. We remark that we do not actually need uniformity over a large class of test functions as we have written above, just a single well-chosen test function would suffice (e.g. the Littlewood-Paley blocks as used in
[
MW17
]
or the Daubechies wavelets in
[
HL15
]
).

5.2.
Tightness

Throughout this section, we are going to fix a terminal time
T>0
. Let
\mu
and
\alpha
be as in Assumption
1.2
Item (
3
). As in the beginning of Section 4, we denote
\mu^{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}x):=e^{\lambda x-\log M(\lambda)}\mu(\mathrm{d}x).
We claim that for any
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
and
\lambda\in[-\alpha,\alpha]
the spatial convolution
g:=f*\mu^{\lambda}
given by

(5.6)

(g,\phi):=(f,\phi*\mu^{\lambda}),\;\;\;\;\;\;\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}),

is well-defined as another element of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
. To prove this, we must show that
\phi\mapsto\phi*\mu^{\lambda}
is a well-defined and continuous map from
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
into itself. By taking the Fourier transform, it suffices to show that the multiplication operator
T_{\lambda}\psi(\xi)=\widehat{\mu^{\lambda}}(\xi)\psi(\xi)
is a continuous linear operator from
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
. But this is clear from the fact that the Fourier transform
\widehat{\mu^{\lambda}}
(i.e., the “characteristic function” of the measure
\mu^{\lambda}
) is smooth with all derivatives bounded, since the measure
\mu^{\lambda}
has exponentially decaying tails.

Likewise if
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and one would like to define a tempered distribution
g\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that formally one has
g(t,x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t,x-y)\mu^{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}y),
the procedure is completely analogous, defining it via the Fourier transforms
\widehat{g}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}):=\widehat{\mu^{\lambda}}(\xi_{2})\widehat{f}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}).

Definition 5.11
.

Define

\Psi_{N,T}:=\{(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}:(Nt,N^{1/2}x)\in\mathbb{Z}\times I\}.

Definition 5.12
.

For
(s,y)\in\mathbb{Z}\times I
define
p_{N}(s,dy)
to be the transition density at time
s
and position
y
of a random walker on
\Lambda_{N}
with increment distribution
\mu^{N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}}.
Define linear operators
D_{N},L_{N},K_{N}
on
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
by

\displaystyle D_{N}f(t,x)

\displaystyle=N\big[f(t+N^{-1},x)-f(t,x)\big],

\displaystyle L_{N}f(t,x)

\displaystyle=N\bigg[f(t+N^{-1},x-N^{-1}d_{N})-\int_{I}f(t,x-N^{-1/2}y)\mu^{N^{-\frac{1}{4p}}}(\mathrm{d}y)\bigg],

\displaystyle K_{N}f(t,x)

\displaystyle=N^{-1}\sum_{s\in[0,T]\cap(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})}\int_{I}f(t-s,x-N^{-1/2}y)p_{N}(Ns,\mathrm{d}y).

These equalities should be understood by integration against smooth functions
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, and the convolutions need to be understood using the explanation after (
5.6
).

Intuitively it is clear that
L_{N}
is a diffusively rescaled version of the discrete heat operator
\mathcal{L}_{N}
from Section
4
, but which acts on tempered distributions rather than measures on
\mathbb{Z}\times I
. Indeed if
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
then a second-order Taylor expansion shows that
L_{N}\varphi
converges in
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
as
N\to\infty
to
(\partial_{t}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2})f
, i.e.,
L_{N}
approaches the continuum heat operator. Then
K_{N}
is the inverse operator to
L_{N}
, in the following sense.

Lemma 5.13
.

L_{N}K_{N}f=K_{N}L_{N}f=f
whenever
f
is a tempered distribution supported on
[a,b]\times\mathbb{R}
with
b-a<T+1
.

Proof.

Note that for each
N
the operators
K_{N},L_{N}
are continuous on
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, as may be verified using the Fourier transform as explained after (
5.6
). Therefore it suffices to prove the claim for all smooth functions
f
that have compact support contained in
(a,b)\times\mathbb{R}
, since these are dense in the subset of distributions supported on
[a,b]\times\mathbb{R}
, with respect to the topology of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. The smooth analogue is true by direct calculations, since
p_{N}(s,\mathrm{d}y)
is the kernel for the inverse operator to the discrete heat operator
\mathcal{L}_{N}
introduced in Section
4
.
∎

Definition 5.14
.

With the fields
M_{N}
and
Q_{N}
as defined in (
4.2
) and (
4.10
) respectively, we will associate random elements of
C([0,T+1],\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}))
by the formulas
\widehat{M}_{N}(t,\phi)=0
for
t\in N^{-1}
and

\displaystyle\widehat{M}_{N}(t,\phi)

\displaystyle:=M^{N}_{t}(\phi)-M^{N}_{N^{-1}}(\phi),\;\;\;\;\;t\geq N^{-1}.

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}_{N}^{f}(t,\phi)

\displaystyle:=Q_{N}^{f}(t,\phi)

for
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
and
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. We define these fields by linear interpolation for
t\notin N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

For any
T
, note that
C([0,T],\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}))
embeds naturally into the linear subspace of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
consisting of distributions supported on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
, thus we can make sense of
D_{N}f,L_{N}f,K_{N}f
for all
f\in C([0,T],\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})),
and these will be elements of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
in general.

Definition 5.15
.

We will say that two tempered distributions
f,g\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
agree on
[0,T]
if there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
(f,\varphi)=(g,\varphi)
for all
\varphi
supported on
[-\varepsilon,T+\varepsilon]\times\mathbb{R}.

Definition 5.16
.

Sample the environment
\omega
and then define a collection of coefficients
a_{N}(s,\mathrm{d}y)
as
a_{N}(0,\mathrm{d}y)=\delta_{0}
and for
s\geq 1
,

a_{N}(s,A)=\int_{I}p_{N}(s-1,A-y)K_{1}(0,\mathrm{d}y),

where
p_{N}
is the same random walk kernel from Definition
5.12
. Then define the distribution
\mathfrak{p}_{N}\in C([0,T+1],\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}))
for
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
by

\mathfrak{p}_{N}(t,\phi):=\int_{I}\phi(N^{-1/2}y)a_{N}(Nt,\mathrm{d}y)

and linearly interpolated for
t\notin N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

Although its definition is tedious,
\mathfrak{p}_{N}
admits the following simple description: it agrees with the density field
\mathfrak{H}^{N}
from (
1.4
) on the time interval
[0,N^{-1}]
, and it is a linearly interpolated solution of the discrete heat equation
\mathcal{L}_{N}a_{N}=0
at all positive times in
N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, where
\mathcal{L}_{N}
is the discrete heat operator of Section
4
. The purpose of this will be to make certain technical details work later. Since
\mathfrak{p}_{N}
behaves deterministically after time
N^{-1}
, its scaling limit will simply be the continuum heat kernel.

Lemma 5.17
.

Let
\widehat{M}_{N}
be as in Definition
5.14
. Furthermore, let
\mathfrak{H}^{N}
be as defined in (
1.4
). Restrict
\mathfrak{H}^{N}
to
[0,T]
thus viewed as an element of
C([0,T],\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}))
. Then
L_{N}(\mathfrak{H}^{N}-\mathfrak{p}_{N})
agrees with
D_{N}\widehat{M}_{N}
on
[0,T]
. Consequently
\mathfrak{H}^{N}
agrees with
\mathfrak{p}_{N}+K_{N}D_{N}\widehat{M}_{N}
on
[0,T]
.

Proof.

Note that
\mathfrak{H}^{N}-\mathfrak{p}_{N}
and
\widehat{M}_{N}
both vanish on
[0,N^{-1}]
, consequently
D_{N}\widehat{M}_{N}(0,\phi)=L_{N}(\mathfrak{H}^{N}-\mathfrak{p}_{N})(0,\phi)=0
for all
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. On the other hand,
L_{N}\mathfrak{p}_{N}(t,\cdot)=0
for
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
with
t>0
, consequently
L_{N}(\mathfrak{H}^{N}-\mathfrak{p}_{N})(t,\phi)=L_{N}\mathfrak{H}^{N}(t,\phi)
for such
t
.

Let
\widehat{v}_{N}:=D_{N}\widehat{M}_{N}
. Then it is clear from (
4.2
) that

\widehat{v}_{N}(t,\phi)=N\big[M^{N}_{t+N^{-1}}(\phi)-M^{N}_{t}(\phi)\big]=N\int_{I}\phi(N^{-1/2}x)(\mathcal{L}_{N}Z_{N})(Nt,\mathrm{d}x),

for all
0\leq t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
.
Now, with
Z_{N}
defined in (
4.1
), for
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{N}
we have

\mathfrak{H}^{N}(t,\phi)=\int_{I}\phi(N^{-1/2}x)Z_{N}(Nt,\mathrm{d}x).

With
\mathcal{L}_{N}
as defined in Section
4
, we than apply the convolution defining
L_{N}
to both sides and it is then clear that for
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
the expression for
L_{N}\mathfrak{H}^{N}(t,\cdot)
can be written as

\displaystyle N\int_{I}\phi(N^{-1/2}x)(\mathcal{L}_{N}Z_{N})(Nt,\mathrm{d}x).

which is the same as the expression for
\widehat{v}_{N}(t,\phi).

Now we need to show that equality holds even if
t\notin N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Since linear operators respect linear interpolation, and since all of the fields
\mathfrak{H}^{N},\widehat{v}_{N},\widehat{M}_{N}
are defined via linear interpolation, this is actually immediate.

Finally, if we view the restriction of
\widehat{v}_{N}
to
[0,T]
as an element of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
supported on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
, then we can apply
K_{N}
to both sides and we obtain that
(\mathfrak{H}^{N}-\mathfrak{p}_{N})(t,\cdot)=K_{N}\widehat{v}_{N}(t,\cdot)=K_{N}D_{N}\widehat{M}_{N}(t,\cdot)
for all
t\in[0,T]
by Lemma
5.13
.
∎

Lemma 5.18
.

Fix
\alpha<0,\tau>0
. Let
K_{N},D_{N}
be the operators from Definition
5.12
. Recall the function spaces from Definition
5.2
, and let
\mathcal{X}_{N}^{\alpha,\tau}
denote the closed linear subspace of
C([0,T+1],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
consisting of those paths
v=(v(t))_{t\in[0,T+1]}
such that
v(t)=0
for all
t\in[0,N^{-1}]
. Then we have the operator norm bound

\sup_{N\geq 1}\|K_{N}D_{N}\|_{\mathcal{X}_{N}^{\alpha,\tau}\to C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))}<\infty.

Proof.

We will adapt the argument from the continuum version of this lemma proved in
[
DDP23b
, Proof of Theorem 1.4(b)]
.

Note that
v(t)=0
for
t\in[0,N^{-1}]
ensures that
D_{N}v
is actually a continuous path taking values in
C([0,T+1-N^{-1}],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
, consequently so is
K_{N}D_{N}v
. Let us now define four families of linear operators on
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
, indexed by
N
. For
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
and
s\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
let

\displaystyle P_{N}(s)f(x)

\displaystyle:=\int_{I}f(x-N^{-1/2}y)p_{N}(Ns,\mathrm{d}y),

\displaystyle\delta_{N}f(x)

\displaystyle:=N(P^{N}_{N^{-1}}-\mathrm{Id})f(x)=N\int_{I}\big(f(x-N^{-1/2}y)-f(x)\big)p_{N}(Ns,\mathrm{d}y)

\displaystyle\delta_{N}^{*}f(x)

\displaystyle:=N\int_{I}\big(f(x+N^{-1/2}y)-f(x)\big)p_{N}(Ns,\mathrm{d}y),

\displaystyle P^{*}_{N}(s)f(x)

\displaystyle:=\big(\mathrm{Id}+N^{-1}\delta_{N}^{*}\big)^{Ns}f(x)=\int_{I}f(x+N^{-1/2}y)p_{N}(Ns,\mathrm{d}y).

Then for
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
each of the four expressions
P_{N}(s)f,\delta_{N}f,\delta_{N}^{*}f,P_{N}^{*}(s)f
also make sense as elements of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
, see e.g. the discussion following (
5.6
). Note that
\delta_{N}
and
\delta^{*}_{N}
, both of which approximate the spatial Laplacian
\partial_{x}^{2}
, are adjoint to each other on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Therefore
P_{N}
and
P^{*}_{N}
are also adjoint to each other. Now let
v=(v(t))_{t\in[0,T+1]}\in\mathcal{X}_{N}^{\alpha,\tau}
. For
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
t\in[0,T]
, if we apply summation by parts and use the fact that
v
vanishes on
[0,N^{-1}]
we can write

\displaystyle\big(K_{N}D_{N}v(t),\phi\big)

\displaystyle=\bigg(N^{-1}\sum_{s\in[0,t]\cap(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})}NP_{N}(t-s)\big[v(s+N^{-1})-v(s)\big]\;\;,\;\;\phi\bigg)

\displaystyle=\bigg(v(t+N^{-1})-0+\sum_{s\in[0,t]\cap(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})}\big[P_{N}(t-s)-P_{N}(t-s-N^{-1})\big](v(s))\;\;,\;\;\phi\bigg)

\displaystyle=(v(t+N^{-1}),\phi)+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{s\in[0,t]\cap(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})}(\delta_{N}P_{N}(t-s-N^{-1})v(s),\phi)

\displaystyle=(v(t+N^{-1}),\phi)+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{s\in[0,t]\cap(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})}(v(s),P_{N}^{*}(t-s-N^{-1})\delta^{*}_{N}\phi).

Here we are using the
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
-pairing between distributions and smooth functions. By the definition of the function spaces (Definition
5.2
), we must replace
\phi
by
S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi
(where the scaling operators are given in Definition
5.1
) in the last expression and then study the growth as
\lambda
becomes close to 0.

The first term on the right side is completely straightforward to deal with: the growth is at worst
\lambda^{\alpha}(1+x^{2})^{\tau}
uniformly over
\lambda,\phi,x,T
, since we assumed
v\in C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})).
To deal with the second term, we claim that one has

(5.7)

|(v(s),P_{N}^{*}(t-s-N^{-1})\delta_{N}^{*}S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi)|\lesssim\big(\lambda^{\alpha-2}\wedge(t-s)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\big)(1+x^{2})^{\tau}

uniformly over
s<t\in[0,T]
, as well as
\lambda,\phi,x,N
and
v
with
\|v\|_{C([0,T+1],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))}\leq 1
. Indeed the bound of the form
\lambda^{\alpha-2}
follows by noting that when
t-s
is much smaller than
\lambda
,
P_{N}^{*}(t-s)
is essentially the identity operator, so we can effectively disregard the heat kernel and note that
\delta_{N}^{*}S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi
paired with
v(s)
satisfies a bound of order
\lambda^{\alpha-2}
, uniformly over
N
by e.g. second-order Taylor expansion. Likewise the bound of the form
(t-s)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}
is obtained by noting that when
\lambda
is very small compared to
t-s
,
P_{N}^{*}(t-s-N^{-1})\delta^{*}_{N}S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi
behaves like
S_{x}^{\sqrt{t-s}}\phi
, giving a bound of order
(\sqrt{t-s})^{\alpha-2}
after applying
\delta^{*}_{N}
and pairing with
v(s)
. This proves the bound (
5.7
).

Now the fact that
\|K_{N}D_{N}v\|
can be controlled by
\|v\|
follows simply by noting that uniformly over
0<\lambda^{2}\leq t\leq T
one has

\int_{0}^{t}\big(\lambda^{\alpha-2}\wedge(t-s)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\big)ds=\int_{0}^{t-\lambda^{2}}(t-s)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}ds+\int_{t-\lambda^{2}}^{t}\lambda^{\alpha-2}ds\leq C(\alpha)\cdot\lambda^{\alpha}.

∎

Proposition 5.19

(Tightness of all relevant processes)
.

The following are true.

(1)

The fields
\widehat{M}_{N}
from Definition
5.14
may be realized as an element of
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
for any
\alpha<-5
and
\tau>1
. Moreover, they are tight with respect to that topology.

(2)

The fields
\mathfrak{H}^{N}
from (
1.4
) may be realized as an element of as an element of
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
for any
\alpha<-5
and
\tau>1
. Moreover, they are tight with respect to that topology.

(3)

For any
f:I\to\mathbb{R}
of exponential decay, the fields
\widehat{Q}_{N}^{f}
from Definition
5.14
may be realized as an element of
C([0,T],C^{\gamma,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
for any
\gamma<-1
and
\tau>1
. Moreover, they are tight with respect to that topology.

(4)

Let
\alpha,\gamma<0
be as in the previous items. Let
(M^{\infty},Q^{\infty},H^{\infty})
be a joint limit point of
(\widehat{M}_{N},\widehat{Q}_{N}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}},\mathfrak{H}^{N})
in the space
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})\times C^{\gamma,\tau}(\mathbb{R})\times C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})),
where
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
is the specific function from Definition
2.11
. For all
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
the process
(M_{t}^{\infty}(\phi))_{t\in[0,T]}
is a continuous martingale with respect to the canonical filtration on that space, and moreover its quadratic variation is given by

(5.8)

\langle M^{\infty}(\phi)\rangle_{t}=Q_{t}^{\infty}(\phi^{2}).

Proof.

Take any
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
with
\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq 1.
Recall
S_{(t,x)}^{\lambda}
from (
5.2
). Using the first bound in Proposition
4.7
, we have

\mathbb{E}[(Q_{N}(t,S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi)-Q_{N}(s,S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi))^{k}]\leq C|t-s|^{k/2}\lambda^{-k},

uniformly over
x\in\mathbb{R},\lambda\in(0,1],0\leq s,t\leq T
with
s,t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Since
Q_{N}(0,\phi)=0
by definition, the assumptions of Lemma
5.10

(a)
are therefore satisfied for any
\kappa\leq 1/4
, any
p>1/\kappa
, and any
\alpha\leq-1
, and we conclude the desired tightness for
\widehat{Q}_{N}=Q_{N}
. This proves Item
(3)
.

Now we address the tightness of the
\widehat{M}_{N}
. Using the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality and then Proposition
4.11
, we have that

(5.9)

\mathbb{E}[(M^{N}_{t}(\phi)-M^{N}_{s}(\phi))^{8}]^{1/8}\leq C\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[\big([M^{N}(\phi)]_{t}-[M^{N}(\phi)]_{s})\big)^{4}\big]^{1/8}\leq C(t-s)^{1/4}\|\phi\|_{C^{3}(\mathbb{R})},

where
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
C=C(k)>0
is free of
\phi,s,t,N.

This gives

\mathbb{E}\big[\big(M^{N}_{t}(S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi)-M^{N}_{s}(S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi)\big)^{8}\big]^{1/8}\leq C(t-s)^{1/4}\lambda^{-4}

uniformly over
x\in\mathbb{R},\lambda\in(0,1],0\leq s,t\leq T
, and
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
with
\|\phi\|_{C^{3}}\leq 1
with support contained in the unit interval. Moreover
\widehat{M}_{N}(0,\phi)=0
by definition, therefore the assumptions of Lemma
5.10

(a)
are satisfied with
\kappa=1/4
,
p=8>1/\kappa
, and any
\alpha\leq-4
. Hence, we conclude the desired tightness for
\widehat{M}_{N}
. This proves Item
(1)
.

Now tightness for the fields
\mathfrak{H}^{N}
is immediate from Lemma
5.18
, since we know from Lemma
5.17
that
\mathfrak{H}^{N}=\mathfrak{p}_{N}+K_{N}D_{N}\widehat{M}_{N}
where we view
K_{N}D_{N}
as a bounded operator from
\mathcal{X}_{N}^{\alpha,\tau}\to C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
and the convergence of
\mathfrak{p}_{N}
in this topology is straightforward to deal with. This proves Item
(2).

We next show that the limit point
M^{\infty}(\phi)
is a martingale indexed by
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Since
M^{N}_{0}(\phi)=0
, from (
5.9
), we see that
\sup_{N}\mathbb{E}[M^{N}_{t}(\phi)^{2k}]<\infty.
Thus
M^{\infty}(\phi)
is a martingale since martingality is preserved by limit points under the uniform integrability assumption. Continuity is guaranteed by the definition of the spaces in which we proved tightness. In the prelimit we know from Lemma
4.3
that

M^{N}_{t}(\phi)^{2}-e^{-2\log M(N^{-\frac{1}{4p}})}Q^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{N}(t,\phi^{2})-\sum_{j=1}^{4}\mathcal{E}^{j}_{N}(t,\phi)

is a martingale indexed by
t\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, where the error terms
\mathcal{E}^{j}_{N}
are defined in (
4.9
) and (
4.8
), and satisfy the bounds in Proposition (
4.9
). By the latter proposition and the tightness estimates (
4.21
) of
Q_{N}^{f}
, it follows that
\sum_{j=1}^{4}\mathcal{E}_{N}^{j}(t,\phi)
vanishes in probability (in the topology of
C[0,T]
for each
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
), so we conclude (again by uniform
L^{p}
boundedness guaranteed by Proposition
4.7
) that
M_{t}^{\infty}(\phi)^{2}-Q_{t}^{\infty}(\phi^{2})

is a martingale. This verifies (
5.8
) completing the proof of Item
(4)
.
∎

5.3.
Identification of limit points

Lemma 5.20

(Controlling the difference between the discrete and continuum time-derivatives)
.

Fix
\alpha<0,\tau>1
. The derivative operator
\partial_{s}:C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})\to C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha-2,\tau}(\Psi_{T})
which was defined in Remark
5.5
, is a bounded linear map. Furthermore, let
D_{N}
be as in Definition
5.12
. Then we have the operator norm bounds

\displaystyle\sup_{N\geq 1}\|D_{N}\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})\to C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha-2,\tau}(\Psi_{T})}

\displaystyle<\infty.

\displaystyle\|D_{N}-\partial_{s}\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})\to C^{\alpha-4,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}N^{-1}.

Proof.

The proof is fairly immediate from the definitions, see
[
DDP23a
, Lemma 6.21]
for the complete argument. ∎

Lemma 5.21

(Controlling the difference between the discrete and continuum heat operators)
.

Fix
\alpha<0,\tau>1
. Let
K_{N}
be as in Definition
5.12
, and let
K
be as in (
5.3
). Then we have the operator norm bound

\|K_{N}-K\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})\to C^{\alpha-1,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})}\leq CN^{-1/4}.

Here
C
is independent of
N
.

The above bound is crude, we do not claim optimality of the Hölder exponents here.

Proof.

Define
f_{\varphi}^{\lambda}(t,x):=(f,S^{\lambda}_{(t,x)}\varphi)_{L^{2}(\Psi_{T})}
. It suffices to show that

\sup_{\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})}\leq 1}\sup_{(t,x)\in\Psi_{T}}\sup_{\lambda\in[(0,1]}\sup_{\varphi\in B_{r}}\frac{|(K_{N}-K)f_{\varphi}^{\lambda}(t,x)|}{(1+x^{2})^{\tau}\lambda^{\alpha-1}}\leq CN^{-1/4},

where the scaling operators are defined by

S^{\lambda}_{(t,x)}\varphi(s,y)=\lambda^{-3}\varphi(\lambda^{-2}(t-s),\lambda^{-1}(x-y)),

and where
B_{r}
is the set of all smooth functions of
C^{r}
norm less than 1 with support contained in the unit ball of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
We shall use a probabilistic interpretation of the kernels to prove this. Note that

(K_{N}-K)f_{\varphi}^{\lambda}(t,x)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{s\in[0,t]\cap(N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})}\mathbf{E}[f_{\varphi}^{\lambda}(t-s,x-W_{N}(s))]\;\;\;-\;\;\;\;\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{E}[f_{\varphi}^{\lambda}(t-s,x-W(s))]ds,

where
W_{N}(s)=N^{-1/2}R_{N}(Ns)
for the discrete-time random walk
(R_{N}(r))_{r\geq 0}
with increment distribution described in Definition
5.12
, and
W
is a standard Brownian motion. Define
W_{N}(s)
by linear interpolation for
s\notin N^{-1}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

By the KMT coupling
[
KMT76
]
, we may assume that
W_{N}
and
W
are all coupled onto the same probability space so that
\sup_{s\in[0,T]}|W_{N}(s)-W(s)|\leq G_{N}N^{-1/4}
where
G_{N}
are random variable on that same probability space such that
\sup_{N}\mathbf{E}|G_{N}|^{p}<\infty
for all
p
(note that
-1/4
is the optimal exponent here because, despite KMT giving a better exponent in principle, the increments of the original random walk
R_{N}
are not centered but rather they have a non-zero mean of order
N^{-3/4}
). Consequently, by the definition of the parabolic spaces, we have uniformly over
s,t\in[0,T]
the bound

\displaystyle|f_{\varphi}^{\lambda}(t-s,x-W_{N}(s))-f_{\varphi}^{\lambda}(t-s,x-W(s))|

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\sup_{u\in\big[-|x|-|W(s)|-|W_{N}(s)|,\;|x|+|W(s)|+|W_{N}(s)|\big]}|\partial_{x}f_{\varphi}^{\lambda}(t-s,u)|\bigg)\cdot|W_{N}(s)-W(s)|

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})}\lambda^{\alpha-1}\big(1+4x^{2}+4W(s)^{2}+4W_{N}(s)^{2}\big)^{\tau}\bigg)\cdot G_{N}N^{-1/4}.

Here the factor
\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})}\lambda^{\alpha-1}
can be deduced using e.g. Remark
5.5
which says that
\partial_{x}
boundedly reduces the parabolic regularity by 1 exponent. We also used the fact that
(|x|+|y|+|z|)^{2}\leq 4x^{2}+4y^{2}+4z^{2}.

We claim that

\mathbf{E}\big|G_{N}\cdot\big(1+4x^{2}+4W(s)^{2}+4W_{N}(s)^{2}\big)^{\tau}\big|<C(1+x^{2})^{\tau}

for a constant
C=C(\alpha,T,\tau)
independent of
N,x,s.
To prove this, one uses
(a+b+c+d)^{\tau}\leq 4^{\tau}(a^{\tau}+b^{\tau}+c^{\tau}+d^{\tau})
, then one notes that
\sup_{N}\mathbf{E}|G_{N}\cdot\sup_{s\leq T}(|W(s)|^{2\tau}+|W_{N}(s)|^{2\tau})|<\infty
by e.g. Cauchy-Schwartz.

Consequently the above expression for
(K_{N}-K)f_{\varphi}^{\lambda}(t,x)
can be bounded in absolute value by a universal constant times
\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})}\lambda^{\alpha-1}N^{-1/4}
. ∎

Since we expect to obtain a Dirac initial data in the limiting SPDE, there is an additional singularity at the origin that we have not yet taken into account. To fix this issue, we now formulate a tightness result taking into account this singularity, by starting the field (
1.4
) from some positive time
\varepsilon
(which should be thought of as being close to
0
).

Proposition 5.22

(Regularity of limit points)
.

Fix
\varepsilon>0,
and consider the fields
\mathfrak{H}^{N,\varepsilon}(t,\cdot):=\mathfrak{H}^{N}(t+\varepsilon,\cdot),
for
t\geq 0
. Set
\mathfrak{H}^{N,\varepsilon}(t,\cdot):=0
for
t<0
. The fields
\mathfrak{H}^{N,\varepsilon}
may be realized as random variables taking values in
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
for any
\tau>1
and
\alpha<-5
. Furthermore, they are tight with respect to that topology, and any limit point is necessarily supported on
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-\kappa+1/2,\tau}(\Psi_{T})
for any
\kappa>0
.

In particular, since
-\kappa+1/2>0
, the last statement implies that the limit point must be supported on a space of continuous functions.

Proof.

We already know from Item (2) of Proposition
5.19
that the
\mathfrak{H}^{N}
are tight in
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
. We also know from Item (1) of Proposition
5.19
that
\widehat{M}_{N}
are tight in
C([0,T+1],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
, which is embeds continuously into
C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})
by Lemma
5.4
. Thus using the first bound in Lemma
5.20
, we have that
D_{N}\widehat{M}_{N}
are tight in
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha-2}(\Psi_{T})
.

Consider any joint limit point
(H^{\infty},\mathscr{M}^{\infty})
as
N\to\infty
of the pair
(\mathfrak{H}^{N},D_{N}\widehat{M}_{N})
in the product space
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))\times C^{\alpha-2,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T})
. By Lemmas
5.17
and
5.21
we may conclude that

(5.10)

H^{\infty}=p+K\mathscr{M}^{\infty},

where
p(t,x)=(2\pi t)^{-1/2}e^{-x^{2}/2t}\mathbf{1}_{\{t\geq 0\}}

is the (deterministic) standard heat kernel, and
K
is the operator defined in (
5.3
).

Fix
\varepsilon>0
. Now we consider the
\varepsilon
-translates of these statements. It is automatic from Item (2) of Proposition
5.19
that
\mathfrak{H}^{N,\varepsilon}
are tight in
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
. Likewise it is automatic from Item (1) that the family
\widehat{M}_{N,\varepsilon}:=\widehat{M}_{N}(\varepsilon+\cdot)
is tight in
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
so that from Lemma
5.20
we see that
D_{N}\widehat{M}_{N,\varepsilon}
are tight in
C^{\alpha-2,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Psi_{T}).
Let us now take a joint limit point
(H^{\infty},\mathscr{M}^{\infty},H^{\infty,\varepsilon},\mathscr{M}^{\infty,\varepsilon})
of the family
(\mathfrak{H}^{N},D_{N}\widehat{M}_{N},\mathfrak{H}^{N,\varepsilon},D_{N}\widehat{M}_{N,\varepsilon}).
On one hand we necessarily have
H^{\infty,\varepsilon}(t)=H^{\infty}(t+\varepsilon)
and on the other hand we necessarily have that
H^{\infty}=p+K\mathscr{M}^{\infty}.
From the last statement in Proposition
5.7
, this then forces the relation

H^{\infty,\varepsilon}=K(\delta_{0}\otimes H^{\infty}(\varepsilon,\cdot))+K\mathscr{M}^{\infty,\varepsilon},

where the tensor product was defined in Definition
5.9
. Now we notice
K(\delta_{0}\otimes H^{\infty}(\varepsilon,\cdot))=J\big(H^{\infty}(\varepsilon,\cdot)\big)
, where
J
is defined in Remark
5.8
. We thus have the Duhamel equation

(5.11)

H^{\infty,\varepsilon}=J\big(H^{\infty}(\varepsilon,\cdot)\big)+K\mathscr{M}^{\infty,\varepsilon}.

For technical reasons that will be made clear below, we now replace
\varepsilon
by
\varepsilon/2
and
T
by
T+1
.

We now claim that for
q>1

(5.12)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\mathscr{M}^{\infty,\varepsilon/2}\|_{C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-\kappa-3/2,\tau}(\Psi_{T+1})}^{q}]<\infty.

Let us now complete the proof of regularity assuming (
5.12
).

Using (
5.12
) and Proposition
5.7
, it follows that

\mathbb{E}\bigg[\big\|K\mathscr{M}^{\infty,\varepsilon/2}\big\|_{C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-\kappa+1/2,\tau}(\Psi_{T+1})}^{q}\bigg]<\infty.

This implies that the restriction of
K\mathscr{M}^{\infty,\varepsilon/2}
to
[\varepsilon/2,T+\varepsilon/2]\times\mathbb{R}
lies in
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-\kappa+1/2,\tau}(\Psi_{[\varepsilon/2,T+\varepsilon/2]})
.

•

Let
h:=\mathfrak{H}^{N}(\varepsilon/2,\cdot)
. By Proposition
5.19
Item (2) we have
h\in C^{-5-\kappa,\tau}
almost surely. So from (
5.5
) with
\alpha:=-5-\kappa
and
\beta:=\frac{1}{2}-\kappa,
it follows that
\widehat{P}_{\varepsilon/2}h\in C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-\kappa+1/2,\tau}(\Psi_{T+1})
almost surely.
Since from (
5.4
), we know
Jh(t+\varepsilon/2,x)=\widehat{P}_{\varepsilon/2}h(t,x)
, we thus have that the restriction of
Jh
to
[\varepsilon/2,T+\varepsilon/2]\times\mathbb{R}
lies in
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-\kappa+1/2,\tau}(\Psi_{[\varepsilon/2,T+\varepsilon/2]})
.

Thanks to the above two bullet points and the relation (
5.11
), we have showed that the restriction of
H^{\infty,\varepsilon/2}
to
[\varepsilon/2,T+\varepsilon/2]\times\mathbb{R}
lies in
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-\kappa+1/2,\tau}(\Psi_{[\varepsilon/2,T+\varepsilon/2]})
. This is equivalent to the fact that
H^{\infty,\varepsilon}
lies in
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-\kappa+1/2,\tau}(\Psi_{T})
. This completes the proof modulo (
5.12
).

Proof of (
5.12
)
. We shall show (
5.12
) with
\varepsilon/2
and
T+1
replaced by
\varepsilon
and
T
respectively. For
\alpha<0
, the derivative operator
\overline{\partial}_{s}:C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))\to C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha-2,\tau}(\Psi_{T})
which is defined by sending
(v_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
to the distribution

(5.13)

(\partial_{s}v,\varphi)_{L^{2}(\Psi_{T})}:=v_{T}(\varphi(T,\cdot))-\int_{0}^{T}v_{t}(\partial_{t}\varphi(t,\cdot))dt-v_{0}(\varphi(0,\cdot)),

whenever
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Psi_{T}),
is a bounded linear map. Indeed this operator is just the composition of the embedding map of Lemma
5.4
with the operator
\partial_{s}
that was proved to be bounded in Lemma
5.20
.

Let
\widehat{M}_{N,\varepsilon}:=\widehat{M}_{N}(\varepsilon+\cdot)-\widehat{M}_{N}(\varepsilon)
and
\widehat{Q}_{N,\varepsilon}:=\widehat{Q}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{N}(\varepsilon+\cdot)-\widehat{Q}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{N}(\varepsilon)
. Let us consider any joint limit point
(Q^{\infty,\varepsilon},M^{\infty,\varepsilon},\mathscr{M}^{\infty,\varepsilon})
of
(\widehat{Q}_{N,\varepsilon},\widehat{M}_{N,\varepsilon},D_{N}\widehat{M}_{N,\varepsilon}
) in the space
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))\times C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))\times C^{\alpha-2,\tau}(\Psi_{T}),
where
\gamma<-1
. Then one may verify from the second bound in Lemma
5.20
that one necessarily has
\mathscr{M}^{\infty,\varepsilon}=\overline{\partial}_{s}M^{\infty,\varepsilon}
. Furthermore by Proposition
5.19
one has martingality of
M_{t}^{\infty,\varepsilon}(\phi)
, with
\langle M^{\infty,\varepsilon}(\phi)\rangle=Q^{\infty,\varepsilon}(\phi^{2})
.

Let us consider any smooth function
\varphi
supported on the unit ball of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
\|\varphi\|_{C^{1}}\leq 1.
Recall
S_{(t,x)}^{\lambda}
from (
5.2
), and notice that
\big(\partial_{t}S_{(t,x)}^{\lambda}\varphi\big)^{2}\leq\lambda^{-10}\mathbf{1}_{[t-\lambda^{2},t+\lambda^{2}]\times[x-\lambda,x+\lambda]}
. We will now estimate the
q^{th}
moments of
\mathscr{M}^{\infty,\varepsilon}(S_{(t,x)}^{\lambda}\varphi)=\overline{\partial}_{s}M^{\infty,\varepsilon}(S_{(t,x)}^{\lambda}\varphi)
. By making
\varepsilon
smaller and
T
larger we may simply ignore the boundary terms in (
5.13
). Note that for fixed
s,t,x,\lambda
the martingale
u\mapsto M_{u}^{\infty,\varepsilon}(\partial_{t}S_{(t,x)}^{\lambda}\varphi(s,\cdot))
is constant outside of the interval
[t-\lambda^{2},t+\lambda^{2}]
. Thus we may apply the Minkowski’s inequality and then Burkholder-Davis-Gundy in (
5.13
) to obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|\mathscr{M}^{\infty,\varepsilon}(S_{(t,x)}^{\lambda}\varphi)|^{q}]^{1/q}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[|\overline{\partial}_{s}M^{\infty,\varepsilon}(S_{(t,x)}^{\lambda}\varphi)|^{q}]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{t-\lambda^{2}}^{t+\lambda^{2}}\mathbb{E}\big[\big|M_{s}^{\infty,\varepsilon}(\partial_{t}S_{(t,x)}^{\lambda}\varphi(s,\cdot))\big|^{q}\big]^{1/q}ds

\displaystyle\leq C_{q}\int_{t-\lambda^{2}}^{t+\lambda^{2}}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(Q_{t+\lambda^{2}}^{\infty,\varepsilon}\big((\partial_{t}S_{(t,x)}^{\lambda}\varphi)^{2}(s,\cdot)\big)-Q_{t-\lambda^{2}}^{\infty,\varepsilon}\big((\partial_{t}S_{(t,x)}^{\lambda}\varphi)^{2}(s,\cdot)\big)\bigg)^{q/2}\bigg]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq 2C_{q}\lambda^{-3}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(Q_{t+\lambda^{2}}^{\infty,\varepsilon}\big(\mathbf{1}_{[x-\lambda,x+\lambda]}\big)-Q_{t-\lambda^{2}}^{\infty,\varepsilon}\big(\mathbf{1}_{[x-\lambda,x+\lambda]}\big)\bigg)^{q/2}\bigg]^{1/q}

where
C=C(q)>0
.
For any
\delta>0
, by the second bound in Proposition
4.7
we find that the last expression may be bounded above by

2C\lambda^{-3}(2\lambda^{2})^{1/2}\|\mathbf{1}_{[x-\lambda,x+\lambda]}\|_{L^{1+\delta}(\mathbb{R})}^{1/2}=C\lambda^{-\frac{3}{2}-\big[\frac{\delta}{2(1+\delta)}\big]}.

Given any
\kappa>0
, take
\delta=\delta(\kappa)
close to
0
and
q=q(\kappa)
large enough, then we may then apply Lemma
5.10
(b)
to conclude (
5.12
).
∎

Now we may finally identify the limit points as the solution of the stochastic heat equation (
1.5
) and thereby prove our weak convergence theorem: Theorem
1.4
.

Lemma 5.23
.

Let
\boldsymbol{w}
be a random variable in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
such that for some smooth even (deterministic)
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
and some
\delta>0
one has

\sup_{a,\varepsilon}(1\wedge a^{1-\delta})\mathbb{E}[(\boldsymbol{w},\phi^{a}_{\varepsilon})^{2}]<\infty

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}[(\boldsymbol{w},\phi_{\varepsilon}^{a})^{2}]=0,\text{ for all }a\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\{0\},

where
\phi_{\varepsilon}^{a}(x):=\varepsilon^{-1}\phi(\varepsilon^{-1}(x-a)).
Then
(\boldsymbol{w},\psi)=0
almost surely for all
\psi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.

Define
\boldsymbol{w}^{\varepsilon}(x):=\boldsymbol{w}*\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)
so that
(\boldsymbol{w},\phi^{a}_{\varepsilon})=\boldsymbol{w}^{\varepsilon}(a).
Given some smooth
\psi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
of compact support note that
(\boldsymbol{w}^{\varepsilon},\psi)\to(\boldsymbol{w},\psi)
a.s. as
\varepsilon\to 0
. This is a purely deterministic statement. Thus it suffices to show that
(\boldsymbol{w}^{\varepsilon},\psi)\to 0
in probability. To prove that, suppose that the support of
\psi
is contained in
[-S,S]
and note by Cauchy-Schwarz that

|(\boldsymbol{w}^{\varepsilon},\psi)|=\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\boldsymbol{w}^{\varepsilon}(a)\psi(a)da\bigg|\leq\bigg[\int_{[-S,S]}\boldsymbol{w}^{\varepsilon}(a)^{2}da\bigg]^{1/2}\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},

so that by taking expectation and applying Jensen we find

\mathbb{E}[|(\boldsymbol{w}^{\varepsilon},\psi)|]\leq\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}}\bigg[\int_{[-S,S]}\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{w}^{\varepsilon}(a)^{2}]da\bigg]^{1/2}.

By assumption,
\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{w}^{\varepsilon}(a)^{2}]\leq Ca^{\delta-1}
and
\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{w}^{\varepsilon}(a)^{2}]\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, therefore the dominated convergence theorem now gives the result by letting
\varepsilon\to 0
on the right side. This proves the claim for
\psi
of compact support. For general
\psi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
we may find a sequence
\psi_{n}\to\psi
in the topology of
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
, with each
\psi_{n}
compactly supported. Then
0=(\boldsymbol{w},\psi_{n})\to(\boldsymbol{w},\psi)
a.s. as
n\to\infty
.
∎

Theorem 5.24

(Solving the martingale problem)
.

Consider the triple of processes
(\mathfrak{H}^{N},\widehat{M}_{N},Q_{N}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}})_{t\geq 0}
, where
\mathfrak{H}^{N}
,
\widehat{M}_{N}
,
Q_{N}^{f},
and
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
are defined in (
1.4
), Definition
5.14
, (
4.10
), and Definition
2.11
respectively. Fix
\alpha<-5,\gamma<-1,
and
\tau>1.
These triples are jointly tight in the space

C\big([0,T]\;,\;C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})\times C^{\gamma,\tau}(\mathbb{R})\times C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})\;\big).

Consider any joint limit point
(H^{\infty},M^{\infty},Q^{\infty})
. Then for any
s>0
, the process
(t,x)\mapsto H_{s+t}^{\infty}(x)
is necessarily supported on the space
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-\kappa+1/2,\tau}(\Psi_{T})
. Furthermore,
(M_{t}^{\infty}(\phi))_{t\geq 0}
is a continuous martingale for all
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, and moreover for all
0<s\leq t<T
one has the almost sure identities

(5.14)

\displaystyle M_{t}^{\infty}(\phi)-M_{s}^{\infty}(\phi)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big(H_{t}^{\infty}(x)-H_{s}^{\infty}(x)\big)\phi(x)dx-\frac{1}{2}\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}H_{u}^{\infty}(x)\phi^{\prime\prime}(x)dxdu

(5.15)

\displaystyle\langle M^{\infty}(\phi)\rangle_{t}

\displaystyle=Q_{t}^{\infty}(\phi^{2})

(5.16)

\displaystyle Q_{t}^{\infty}(\phi)-Q_{s}^{\infty}(\phi)

\displaystyle=\gamma(\boldsymbol{\zeta})\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{s}^{t}H_{u}^{\infty}(x)^{2}\phi(x)\,du\,dx.

Before going into the proof, we remark that with some inspection it may be verified that all quantities make sense given the spaces they lie in, as long as we choose
\kappa<1/2
to ensure that
H^{\infty}
is a continuous function in space-time away from
t=0
.

Proof.

Most parts of the following theorem are already established in previous propositions and lemmas. The tightness of the triple was shown in Proposition
5.19
. Consider any limit point
(H^{\infty},M^{\infty},Q^{\infty}).
The fact that for any
s>0
, the process
(t,x)\mapsto H^{\infty}_{s+t}(x)
is necessarily supported on the space
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-\kappa+1/2,\tau}(\Psi_{T})
was proved in Proposition
5.22
. From Proposition
5.19
we have that for all
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
the process
(M_{t}^{\infty}(\phi))_{t\geq 0}
is a martingale. (
5.15
) is already proven in Proposition
5.19
as (
5.8
). Now we just need to show (
5.14
) and (
5.16
).

Proof of (
5.14
).

In (
5.10
) we obtained that
H^{\infty}=p+K\mathscr{M}^{\infty}
where (just as we observed after (
5.13
)) one necessarily has
\mathscr{M}^{\infty}=\overline{\partial}_{s}M^{\infty}
. By disregarding the boundary terms implies that

(\overline{\partial}_{s}M^{\infty},\varphi)=((\partial_{t}-\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2})H^{\infty},\varphi)

for all
\varphi
of compact support contained in
[\varepsilon,T-\varepsilon]\times\mathbb{R}
for some
\varepsilon>0
. Since both
M^{\infty}
and
H^{\infty}
lie in spaces with strong enough topologies (see Proposition
5.22
), taking
\varphi(u,x)
to approach the function
(u,x)\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{[s,t]}(u)\phi(x)
in the above equation leads to (
5.14
).

Proof of (
5.16
).
Fix any
0\leq t\leq T
and let
\boldsymbol{w}
be a
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
-valued random variable defined as

(5.17)

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{w},\phi):=Q_{t}^{\infty}(\phi)-\gamma(\boldsymbol{\zeta})\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{0}^{t}H_{s}^{\infty}(x)^{2}\phi(x)\,ds\,dx.

We claim that
(\boldsymbol{w},\phi)=0
a.s. for all
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}).
This will validate (
5.16
). To verify this, let us define
\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}(x):=\varepsilon^{-1}\xi(\varepsilon^{-1}(x-a))
with
\xi(x):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-x^{2}}
. Using the “key estimate” (
4.16
) of Proposition
4.6
, one verifies that the assumptions of Lemma
5.23
hold true for
\boldsymbol{w}
with this family of mollifiers.

To see why Lemma
5.23
is applicable, first note that for fixed
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, the the difference between the sum appearing in (
4.16
) and integral over
[0,t]
of the same quantity tends to zero in probability with respect to the topology of
C[0,T]
, since we proved tightness of
\mathfrak{H}^{N}
in a topology given by the norm of
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
. Therefore that sum in (
4.16
) converges in law, jointly with
\mathfrak{H}^{N}
, to the integral appearing in (
5.17
). Then an application of (
4.16
) and (
4.17
) shows that
(\boldsymbol{w},\phi)
as defined by (
5.17
) satisfies the conditions of Lemma
5.23
for any
\delta\in(0,1)
, since (
4.17
) gives a polylogarithmic bound which is less than
Ca^{-\delta}
for arbitrary choice of
\delta>0
. This is enough to complete the proof.
∎

We now complete the proof of our main theorem, Theorem
1.4
.

Proof of Theorem
1.4
.

We continue with the notation and setup of Theorem
5.24
. We have already established the tightness of
\mathfrak{H}^{N}
in Proposition
5.19
. Consider any limit point
H^{\infty}
of
\mathfrak{H}^{N}
. From the previous theorem, we already know that
(t,x)\mapsto H_{t+\varepsilon}^{\infty}(x)
is a continuous function in space and time. From the three equations (
5.14
), (
5.15
), and (
5.16
) in Theorem
5.24
it follows that the martingale problem for (
1.5
) is satisfied by any limit point
H^{\infty}
. We refer the reader to
[
BG97
, Proposition 4.11]
for the characterization of the law of (
1.5
) as the solution to this martingale problem.

The result there is only stated for continuous initial conditions, so what this really shows is that for any
\varepsilon>0
the law of the continuous field
(t,x)\mapsto H^{\infty}_{t+\varepsilon}(x)
is that of the solution of (
1.5
) with initial condition
H^{\infty}_{\varepsilon}(\cdot).
Thus we still need to pin down the initial data as
\delta_{0}
, by showing that we can let
\varepsilon\to 0
and see that the limit of
H^{\infty}_{\varepsilon}(\cdot)
is equal to
\delta_{0}
in some sense.

In
[
Par18
, Section 6]
there is a general approach to doing this. Specifically, it suffices to show as in Lemma 6.6 of that paper the two bounds

(5.18)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|H_{t}^{\infty}(x)|^{q}]^{2/q}\leq C\cdot t^{-1/2}p_{t}(x),

(5.19)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|H_{t}^{\infty}(x)-p_{t}(x)|^{q}]^{2/q}\leq C\cdot p_{t}(x),

where
q>1
is arbitrary,
p_{t}(x)=(2\pi t)^{-1/2}e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2t}}
is the standard heat kernel, and
C
is independent of
t>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Clearly, it suffices to show this when
r
is an even integer. The solution of (
1.5
) with
\delta_{0}
initial condition certainly satisfies this bound, and by the moment convergence result in Section 3, we know any limit point
H^{\infty}
must satisfy
\mathbb{E}[(\int_{\mathbb{R}}H_{t}^{\infty}(x)\phi(x)dx)^{k}]=\mathbf{E}[(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{U}_{t}(x)\phi(x)dx)^{k}]
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, where
(t,x)\mapsto\mathcal{U}_{t}(x)
solves (
1.5
) with
\delta_{0}
initial data. From here we can conclude by letting
\phi\to\delta_{x}
that
\mathbb{E}[H_{t}^{\infty}(x)^{k}]=\mathbf{E}[\mathcal{U}_{t}(x)^{k}]
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Then we may immediately deduce (
5.18
) and (
5.19
) by the corresponding bounds for
\mathcal{U}_{t}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.25

(Extension to other initial data)
.

Throughout this paper we have taken the convention of Dirac initial data, where one starts
\mathfrak{H}^{N}
from a Dirac mass at zero, corresponding to all particles starting from 0 in the quenched random walks. In fact, this may be generalized, where one assumes instead that
\omega_{N}
is a sequence of probability measures on
I
such that the measures
e^{xN^{\frac{2p-1}{4p}}}\omega_{N}(N^{1/2}\mathrm{d}x)
converges in the topology of
C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})
to some limiting
bounded and continuous
function
\mathcal{U}_{0}.
Then define
\mathfrak{K}(n,\cdot):=\omega_{N}K_{1}\cdots K_{n}
and let
\mathfrak{H}^{N}
be as in (
1.4
) with
\mathfrak{K}
defined in this more general fashion. The above methods can be generalized to show that
\mathfrak{H}^{N}
as defined this way converge in law (in the same sense as Theorem
1.4
) to the solution of (
1.5
) started from
\mathcal{U}_{0}
. Indeed, this is because all of the bounds and convergence theorems we derived in Sections 2 and 3 worked with varying initial starting point, and this generality can be propagated into the remainder of the paper. Some aspects of the paper need an appropriate modification, for instance Proposition
4.6
will no longer require the polylogarithmic factor at the origin.

Of course, this leads to the question of
e^{xN^{\frac{2p-1}{4p}}}\omega_{N}(N^{1/2}\mathrm{d}x)
converging to a more singular limit in the topology of
C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})
, as opposed to a
continuous function
(e.g. a measure or tempered distribution). For a reasonable limiting measure, we still expect to see (
1.5
) in the limit started from this singular initial data, but it is beyond the scope of the present paper to determine how to generalize Proposition
4.6
and
[
BG97
, Proposition 4.11]
to this setting.

6.
Examples of models satisfying the hypotheses

In this section, we will introduce a number of different models, prove that Assumption
1.2
is satisfied for all these models, and, if possible, calculate the coefficient
\gamma_{\mathrm{ext}}
from Theorem
1.4
for these models. As explained in Remark
1.7
of the introduction, if all other conditions of Assumption
1.2
hold true but
m_{2}-m_{1}^{2}\neq 1
, then the condition
m_{2}-m_{1}^{2}=1
can always be forced to be true by replacing
I
by
\alpha I
for some
\alpha>0
, and modifying the kernels
K_{i}
to reflect this rescaling. Therefore we will not verify this condition for the models below, and it should be implicitly understood that this would affect the diffusion coefficient in (
1.5
).

6.1.
A generalized model of random walk in random environment on
\mathbb{Z}

Here we consider a generalized non-nearest neighbor model of random walks in random environments whose KPZ fluctuations were recently conjectured in the physics paper
[
HDCC24
]
.

Define the set
\Omega:=\{v\in[0,1]^{\mathbb{Z}}:\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}v(i)=1\}
, and let
P
be any probability measure on
\Omega
(defined on the
\sigma
algebra generated by the coordinate maps
v\mapsto v(i)
with
i\in\mathbb{Z}
). Assume the following four conditions on the probability measure
P
:

•

With positive
P
-probability, the additive subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}
generated by
\{i\in\mathbb{Z}:v(i)>0\}
is all of
\mathbb{Z}.

•

E[v(i)]\leq Ce^{-c|i|}
for some constants
C,c>0.

•

\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}E[v(i)^{2}]<1,
i.e.,
v
is not
P
-a.s. concentrated at a single point of
\mathbb{Z}
.

•

The first
p-1
moments of
v
sampled from
P
are deterministic but the
p^{th}
one is not.

It is clear that such
P
exists for every choice of
p\in\mathbb{N}
, for instance choose a probability distribution on
\{-1,...,p-1\}
so that the first
p-1
moments are deterministic but the
p^{th}
one may take e.g. either one of two possible distinct values with equal probability. Using linear algebra one may show that this is possible.

With
P
as above, let us now sample
\{v_{r,x}\}_{x\in\mathbb{Z},r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
according to the measure
P^{\otimes(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{Z})}
on the product space
\Omega^{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{Z}}.
In other words all of the
v_{r,x}(\bullet)
are iid
\Omega
-valued random variables sampled from
P
for every lattice site
(r,x)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{Z}.
Now sample the random kernels

K_{r}(x,y):=v_{r,x}(y-x).

Proposition 6.1
.

K_{r}
as defined above satisfies all of the conditions of Assumption
1.2
.

Proof.

With the exception of Item (
5
), it is immediate from the above four bullet points that all of the items of Assumption
1.2
are satisfied. To show Item (
5
), one may actually show that the stronger condition (
1.1
) is satisfied. But this is fairly obvious since the total variation distance is zero as long as no two
x_{i}
are the same, simply by the assumption of independence (i.e., sampling the kernels from the product measure
P^{\otimes(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{Z})}
).∎

Theorem
1.4
thus gives convergence of the rescaled and recentered quenched density field (
1.4
) to the solution of (
1.5
) for such a model of random walks in a random environment with such iid kernels at every lattice site. This generalizes our result from
[
DDP23a
, Theorem 1.1]
and confirms a conjecture from the physics work of
[
HDCC24
]
.

One may show that the invariant measure
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
of the annealed difference process is just counting measure on
\mathbb{Z}
except at the origin where it carries extra mass. The annealed difference process is actually reversible in this case, i.e., the detailed balance equations can be solved, and the amount of extra mass
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
has at the origin can be found fairly explicitly in terms of the parameters of the model. This was done in the physics paper
[
HDCC24
]
which led to several interesting alternate forms that the coefficient
\gamma_{\mathrm{ext}}
could be written. In the present work we do not explore questions such as massaging the coefficient into other forms, determining when the measure is reversible, etc.

6.2.
A “random landscape model” with strongly mixing weight fields

Here we will study a particular type of stochastic flow model introduced to us by I. Corwin. We will see that KPZ fluctuations arise in systems where there may be infinite range of particle interaction or non-iid weights satisfying strong enough mixing conditions. This seems to be somewhat novel among KPZ-type convergence results.

Let
(\omega_{r,x})_{r\in\mathbb{N},x\in\mathbb{Z}}
be a collection of random variables, and for each
r\in\mathbb{N}
denote
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{r}:=(\omega_{r,x})_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}.
Let
b:\mathbb{Z}\to[0,1]
be any deterministic function, nonzero on at least two sites, such that
\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}b(x)=1
, and assume that the additive subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}
generated by
\{x:b(x)>0\}
is all of
\mathbb{Z}.

Define the kernels

(6.1)

K_{r}(x,y):=\frac{b(y-x)e^{\omega_{r,y}}}{\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}b(y^{\prime}-x)e^{\omega_{r,y^{\prime}}}}.

In order to prove KPZ fluctuations for this type of model, we will need to verify Assumption
1.2
, for which we will need to assume that either one of the following two conditions holds:

(A)

b
is finitely supported, furthermore the
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{3},...
are independent families with each
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{r}
having the same finite-dimensional marginals as
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1},
and moreover
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}
is strictly stationary and
\alpha
-mixing with
\alpha(n)\leq Ce^{-\theta n}
for some
C,\theta>0
.

(B)

|b(x)|\leq Ce^{-\theta|x|}
for some
C,\theta>0
, and furthermore
(\omega_{r,x})_{r\in\mathbb{N},x\in\mathbb{Z}}
are iid with
\mathbb{E}[e^{16|\omega_{1,0}|}]<\infty
.

Except for Items (
3
) and (
5
), the remaining items of Assumption
1.2
are trivially satisfied from the assumptions on the weights and the probabilities (taking
p=1
in Item (
4
)). Note that for
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in I^{k}
we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}^{(k)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\prod_{j=1}^{k}K_{1}(x_{j},y_{j})]=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{k}\frac{b(y_{j}-x_{j})e^{\omega_{1,y_{j}}}}{\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}b(y^{\prime}-x_{j})e^{\omega_{1,y^{\prime}}}}\bigg]

\displaystyle\mu^{\otimes k}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathbb{E}[K_{1}(x_{j},y_{j})]=\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{b(y_{j}-x_{j})e^{\omega_{1,y_{j}}}}{\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}b(y^{\prime}-x_{j})e^{\omega_{1,y^{\prime}}}}\bigg].

Using these expressions, let us focus on verifying Items (
3
) and (
5
) in the conditions of Assumption
1.2
. This will be done separately for each of the two bulleted assumptions above.

Proposition 6.2
.

Under either Condition
(A)
or Condition
(B)
, Assumption
1.2
is satisfied by the kernels
K_{r}
from (
6.1
), with
p=1
.

Proof.

Verification of Assumption
1.2
under Condition (A).
Since
b
is finitely supported, Item (
3
) is trivially satisfied, so we just need to verify Item (
5
). To do this, we will verify the stronger assumption (
1.1
).

Let us first recall the definition of exponential
\alpha
-mixing for a strictly stationary sequence of random variables. If
\boldsymbol{\omega}:=(\omega_{x})_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a such a sequence, then we have a definition

\alpha(n):=\alpha(\mathcal{F}_{-\infty,0},\mathcal{F}_{n,\infty})

where
\mathcal{F}_{m,n}:=\sigma(\omega_{x}:m\leq x\leq n)
for
m\leq n
and
\alpha(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}):=\sup\{|\mathbb{P}(A\cap B)-\mathbb{P}(A)\mathbb{P}(B)|:A\in\mathcal{F},B\in\mathcal{G}\}
for two sub
\sigma
-algebras
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}
. The sequence
\boldsymbol{\omega}
is called
\alpha
-mixing if
\alpha(n)\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, and it is called
exponentially

\alpha
-mixing if
\alpha(n)\to 0
exponentially fast (e.g. iid sequences are
\alpha
-mixing with
\alpha(n)=\mathbf{1}_{\{n=0\}}.)

Suppose
\boldsymbol{\omega}
is any
\alpha
-mixing sequence with
\alpha(n)\leq Ce^{-\theta n}
where
C,\theta>0
. Let
[m_{i},n_{i}]

(1\leq i\leq k)
be disjoint intervals of
\mathbb{Z}
such that
n_{i}+J\leq m_{i+1}
where
J>0
(large). By iteratively applying the
\alpha
-mixing property and using the triangle inequality, one may show that if
f_{i}
are
\mathcal{F}_{m_{i},n_{i}}
-measurable with
|f_{i}|\leq 1
, then

(6.2)

\bigg|\mathbb{E}\big[\prod_{j=1}^{k}f_{i}\big]-\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathbb{E}[f_{i}]\bigg|\leq Ck\cdot e^{-\theta J}.

Let us apply this bound in our context. Suppose that our weight function
b
is supported on
[-B,B]
where
B>0.
Now if
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},...,x_{k})\in I^{k}
, letting
2J:=\min_{1\leq i<j\leq k}|x_{i}-x_{j}|
we see from (
6.2
) and the above expressions for
\boldsymbol{p}^{(k)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
and
\mu^{\otimes k}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x})
that

\big|\boldsymbol{p}^{(k)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\mu^{\otimes k}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x})\big|\leq Cke^{-\theta(J-B)}=C^{\prime}e^{-\frac{\theta}{2}\min_{1\leq i<j\leq k}|x_{i}-x_{j}|},

where
C^{\prime}=Cke^{\theta B}.
Since
b
is finitely supported on
[-B,B]
, it follows that the measures
\boldsymbol{p}^{(k)}(\mathbf{x},\bullet)
and
\mu^{\otimes k}
have support contained on at most
(2B)^{k}
sites. Thus the last expression is already enough to imply the total variation bound (
1.1
), thus completing the proof.

Verification of Assumption
1.2
under Conditition (B).
Using Jensen’s inequality on the denominator, we have

K_{r}(x,y)\leq\frac{b(y-x)e^{\omega_{r,y}}}{e^{\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}b(y^{\prime}-x)\omega_{r,y^{\prime}}}}=b(y-x)e^{\omega_{r,y}-\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}b(y^{\prime}-x)\omega_{r,y^{\prime}}}.

Taking expectation, we see that

\mathbb{E}[K_{r}(x,y)]\leq b(y-x)\mathbb{E}[e^{(1-b(y-x))\omega_{r,y}}]\prod_{y^{\prime}\neq y}\mathbb{E}[e^{-b(y^{\prime}-x)\omega_{r,y^{\prime}}}]=C\cdot b(x-y),

where the (possibly infinite) product is convergent since we know that the weights are iid and
\sum_{y^{\prime}}b(y^{\prime}-x)<\infty
. Thus Item (
3
) of Assumption
1.2
is satisfied. More generally, the same arguments together with Hölder’s inequality can be used to show that as long as
e^{|\omega_{1,0}|}
has at least
m
finite moments, we have for all
k\leq m/4
that

(6.3)

\mathbb{E}[\prod_{j=1}^{k}K_{r}(x_{j},y_{j})]\leq C\prod_{j=1}^{k}b(x_{j}-y_{j}),

Let us verify Item (
5
) by verifying the stronger assumption (
1.1
).
Notice that if
X,Y,Z\geq 0
are three random variables defined on any probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},P)
then we have that

\displaystyle E\bigg|\frac{X}{Y+Z}-\frac{X}{Y}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq E[X^{2}]^{1/2}E\bigg[\bigg(\frac{1}{Y+Z}-\frac{1}{Y}\bigg)^{2}\bigg]^{1/2}=E[X^{2}]^{1/2}E\bigg[\bigg(\frac{Z}{Y(Y+Z)}\bigg)^{2}\bigg]^{1/2}

(6.4)

\displaystyle\leq E[X^{2}]^{1/2}E\bigg[\frac{Z^{2}}{(Y+Z)^{2}}\bigg]^{1/2}\leq E[X^{2}]^{1/2}E[Z^{4}]^{1/4}E[(Y+Z)^{-4}]^{1/4}.

Fix
J>0
(large) and
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
where
1\leq k\leq 4
. We will use this inequality with

X:=\prod_{j=1}^{k}e^{\omega_{1,y_{j}}},\;\;\;\;\;\;\;Y=\prod_{j=1}^{k}\bigg(\sum_{y^{\prime}:|y^{\prime}-x_{j}|\leq J}b(y^{\prime}-x_{j})e^{\omega_{1,y^{\prime}}}\bigg),\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;Z:=\sum_{\mathbf{y}^{\prime}:\max|y_{j}^{\prime}-x_{j}|>J}\prod_{j=1}^{k}b(y_{j}^{\prime}-x_{j})e^{\omega_{1,y_{j}^{\prime}}},

where the last sum is over vectors
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}=(y_{1}^{\prime},...,y_{k}^{\prime}).
By Minkowski’s inequality we have

\mathbb{E}[Z^{4}]^{1/4}\leq\sum_{\mathbf{y}^{\prime}:\max|y_{j}^{\prime}-x_{j}|>J}\prod_{j=1}^{k}b(y_{j}^{\prime}-x_{j})\mathbb{E}[e^{4\omega_{1,y_{j}^{\prime}}}]^{1/4}\leq Ce^{-\theta J},

where we are using the exponential decay of the kernel
b
and the fact that the weights are iid. Now by Jensen we also have

(Y+Z)^{-1}=\prod_{j=1}^{k}\big(\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}b(y^{\prime}-x_{j})e^{\omega_{1,y^{\prime}}}\big)^{-1}\leq\prod_{j=1}^{k}e^{-\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}b(y^{\prime}-x_{j})\omega_{1,y^{\prime}}},

therefore by applying Hölder’s inequality we have

\mathbb{E}[(Y+Z)^{-4}]\leq\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathbb{E}[e^{-4k\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}b(y^{\prime}-x_{j})\omega_{1,y^{\prime}}}]^{1/k}=\prod_{j=1}^{k}\prod_{y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{E}[e^{-4kb(y^{\prime}-x_{j})\omega_{1,y^{\prime}}}]^{1/k},

and the latter is finite and bounded by an absolute constant since
b(y^{\prime}-x)
is summable over
y^{\prime}
and the weights are iid satisfying
\mathbb{E}[e^{4k|\omega_{1,0}|}]<\infty
. Summarizing the last three expressions and applying (
6.4
), we find uniformly over
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in I^{k}
that

(6.5)

\bigg|\boldsymbol{p}^{(k)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\mathbb{E}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{k}\frac{b(y_{j}-x_{j})e^{\omega_{1,y_{j}}}}{\sum_{y^{\prime}:|y^{\prime}-x_{j}|\leq J}b(y^{\prime}-x_{j})e^{\omega_{1,y^{\prime}}}}\bigg]\bigg|\leq Ce^{-\theta J}.

The same argument with
k=1
also yields that

\bigg|\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{b(y_{j}-x_{j})e^{\omega_{1,y_{j}}}}{\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}b(y^{\prime}-x_{j})e^{\omega_{1,y^{\prime}}}}\bigg]-\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{b(y_{j}-x_{j})e^{\omega_{1,y_{j}}}}{\sum_{y^{\prime}:|y^{\prime}-x_{j}|\leq J}b(y^{\prime}-x_{j})e^{\omega_{1,y^{\prime}}}}\bigg]\bigg|\leq Ce^{-\theta J},\;\;\;\;\;\;\;1\leq j\leq k.

By taking products over
j\leq k
this will yield

(6.6)

\bigg|\mu^{\otimes k}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x})-\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{b(y_{j}-x_{j})e^{\omega_{1,y_{j}}}}{\sum_{y^{\prime}:|y^{\prime}-x_{j}|\leq J}b(y^{\prime}-x_{j})e^{\omega_{1,y^{\prime}}}}\bigg]\bigg|\leq Ce^{-\theta J},

where the constant
C
may have grown. Using the independence of the weights we see that if
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},...,x_{k})\in I^{k}
such that
\min_{1\leq i<j\leq k}|x_{i}-x_{j}|=:2J
then the expectation of the product in (
6.5
) is equal to the product of expectations in (
6.6
), thus
\sup_{\mathbf{y}\in I^{k}}\big|\boldsymbol{p}^{(k)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\mu^{\otimes k}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x})\big|\leq Ce^{-\theta J}.
In other words, we have shown that

\sup_{\mathbf{y}\in I^{k}}\big|\boldsymbol{p}^{(k)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\mu^{\otimes k}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x})\big|\leq Ce^{-\frac{\theta}{2}\min_{1\leq i<j\leq k}|x_{i}-x_{j}|}.

Using (
6.3
) we also have

\big|\boldsymbol{p}^{(k)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\mu^{\otimes k}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x})\big|\leq\big|\boldsymbol{p}^{(k)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})|+|\mu^{\otimes k}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x})\big|\leq C\prod_{j=1}^{k}b(x_{j}-y_{j}).

Combining the last two expressions and using
\min\{u,v\}\leq u^{1/2}v^{1/2}
we thus find that

\big|\boldsymbol{p}^{(k)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\mu^{\otimes k}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x})\big|\leq Ce^{-\frac{\theta}{4}\min_{1\leq i<j\leq k}|x_{i}-x_{j}|}\prod_{j=1}^{k}b(x_{j}-y_{j})^{1/2}\leq Ce^{-\frac{\theta}{4}\min_{1\leq i<j\leq k}|x_{i}-x_{j}|}e^{-\frac{\theta}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{k}|x_{j}-y_{j}|},

where we applied the assumption of exponential decay of
b
. Consequently we find that

\displaystyle\big\|\mu^{\otimes k}(\bullet-\mathbf{x})\;\;-\;\;\boldsymbol{p}^{(k)}\big(\mathbf{x},\bullet\big)\big\|_{TV}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{y}\in I^{k}}\big|\boldsymbol{p}^{(k)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\mu^{\otimes k}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x})\big|

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{-\frac{\theta}{4}\min_{1\leq i<j\leq k}|x_{i}-x_{j}|}\sum_{\mathbf{y}\in I^{k}}e^{-\frac{\theta}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{k}|x_{j}-y_{j}|}

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{-\frac{\theta}{4}\min_{1\leq i<j\leq k}|x_{i}-x_{j}|},

which proves (
1.1
) as desired. The conditions needed to make sure all expectations above are finite is that
\mathbb{E}[e^{4k|\omega_{1,0}|}]<\infty
. Since
p=1
in Assumption
1.2
Item (
4
), we need (
1.1
) to hold only up to
k=4
, and it follows that
16
moments is all that is necessary.
∎

Remark 6.3
.

Note that Condition
(A)
does not require any moment conditions on the weights
\omega_{r,x}
. We also remark that the mixing rate could be weakened to
\alpha(n)\leq Cn^{-2-\epsilon}
, and thanks to the assumptions on
\mathrm{F_{decay}}
in Item (
5
) of Assumption
1.2
, this rate would still suffice, despite the exponential bound (
1.1
) not holding.

Additionally, while we formulated the model on discrete space
\mathbb{Z}
, there is also a continuous-space analogue of the above
\alpha
-mixing landscape model. Let
(\omega(x))_{x\in\mathbb{R}}
be any strictly stationary
\alpha
-mixing field with continuous sample paths. Let
b:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a bounded nonnegative measurable function of compact support that is not Lebesgue-a.e. zero. Then we may define the kernel

K_{1}(x,dy):=\frac{b(y-x)e^{\omega(y)}}{\int_{\mathbb{R}}b(y^{\prime}-x)e^{\omega(y^{\prime})}dy^{\prime}}.

Essentially the same arguments as those given above will verify the conditions of Assumption
1.2
for iid samplings
K_{1},K_{2},K_{3},...
of this kernel. An example of such a field
(\omega(x))_{x\in\mathbb{R}}
is an Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process. In fact, it is known that any strictly stationary Markov process that is geometrically ergodic is exponentially
\beta
-mixing and thus exponentially
\alpha
-mixing, see
[
Bra05
, Theorem 3.7]
. Consequently we can take
\omega
to be the stationary solution to the SDE
d\omega(t)=-V^{\prime}(\omega(t))dt+dW(t)
where e.g.
\lim_{|x|\to\infty}\frac{V(x)}{|x|}=\infty.

Finally we remark that for the “landscape-type” models considered above, we do not know if the invariant measure
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
can be explicitly described in terms of the parameters of the model, as was the case for the Dirichlet-type models of the previous subsection. We are not sure if the annealed difference process
\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}
is reversible with respect to
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
, though we see no reason for this to be the case given the complex correlation structure between the particles.

6.3.
Transport-type SPDE model: diffusion in random environment

While Theorem
1.4
perhaps gives the impression that one can only consider discrete-time systems, this is not at all the case. We now discuss a continuous-time stochastic PDE model that motivated much of the discussion of KPZ fluctuations arising in stochastic flow models
[
BLD20
,
LDT17
,
GH04
,
BW22
]
.

Let
\xi
be a standard Gaussian space-time white noise on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R},
and consider any smooth even compactly supported function
\phi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}.
Consider the spatially mollified noise
\eta:=\xi*\phi
which is smooth in space and white in time. Assume that
\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}<1
, i.e.,
\phi*\phi(0)<1
which implies that
\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\phi*\phi(x)<1
.

Formally the covariance kernel of
\eta
is given by
\mathbb{E}[\eta(t,x)\eta(s,y)]=(\phi*\phi)(x-y)\cdot\delta(t-s)
. As explained in
[
BW22
]
, one may sensibly construct the Itô solution to the stochastic PDE

(6.7)

\partial_{t}u(t,x)=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2}u(t,x)+\partial_{x}\big(u(t,x)\eta(t,x)\big),\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;u(0,x)=\delta_{0}(x),

where
t\geq 0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Note that, unlike (
SHE
), there is a derivative around the multiplicative term and consequently the qualitative properties of this equation are substantially different. A pathwise solution
u
that is continuous in both variables (away from the origin) and adapted to the filtration of the noise
\eta
is only possible due to the spatial smoothness of
\eta
, and it turns out that the solution preserves mass of the initial data, i.e.,
\int_{\mathbb{R}}u(t,x)dx=1
for all
t>0
a.s., see
[
Kun94
,
Kun97
,
LJR02
]
.

For
t>s\geq 0
and
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
we define the “propagators”
u_{s,t}(x,y)
associated to the equation (
6.7
) as follows: let
(t,y)\mapsto u_{s,t}(x,y)
be the solution of (
6.7
) started from
\delta_{x}(y)
at time
s
. All of these solutions are coupled to the same realization of the driving noise
\eta
. Then we have the convolution property

(6.8)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}u_{s,t}(x,y)u_{t,r}(y,z)dy=u_{s,r}(x,z),\;\;\;\;\;\forall s<t<r\;\;\;\;\;a.s.,

and furthermore if
\{(s_{i},t_{i}]\}_{i=1}^{m}
are disjoint intervals then
\{u_{s_{i},t_{i}}\}_{i=1}^{m}
are independent (because
u_{s,t}
is always measurable with respect to the restriction of
\eta
to
[s,t]\times\mathbb{R}
)
[
Kun94
,
Kun97
,
LJR02
]
.

We may thus define the kernels

K_{r}(x,y):=u_{r,r+1}(x,y),

and the preservation of mass implies that
\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{r}(x,y)dy=1
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

Proposition 6.4
.

With
K_{r}
as defined above, all of the items of Assumption
1.2
are satisfied for these kernels.

Proof.

Since the driving noise
\eta
of (
6.7
) is spatially and temporally stationary, it follows that
K_{1}
is translation invariant and that the kernels
K_{i}
all have the same distribution. The fact that the kernels
K_{1},K_{2},...
are independent was already explained above. Let us explicitly calculate the annealed one-step law
\mu
as in Item (
3
) of Assumption
1.2
. Letting
\theta:=1-(\phi*\phi)(0)
,
[
DG22
, Proposition 2.1]
proves that (
6.7
) is the Kolmogorov forward equation associated to the SDE formally given by

(6.9)

dX(t)=\eta(t,X(t))dt+\theta^{1/2}dW(t),

where
W
is a Brownian motion independent of the noise
\eta
.
1
1

1

This SDE representation looks misleading because the Itô-Stratonovich correction in (
6.7
) is not a constant multiple of
u
, but rather it is given by
\frac{1}{2}(1-\theta)\partial_{x}^{2}u(t,x),
which explains why the viscosity in the Itô equation is
\frac{1}{2}
instead of
\frac{1}{2}\theta
.
It turns out that that this formal SDE admits a solution theory by construction of a stochastic integral
\int_{0}^{t}\eta(s,X(s))ds,
see e.g.
[
BC95
]
. Moreover, for each realization of
\eta
, one may prove that
\int_{B}u_{s,t}(x,y)dy=P^{\eta}(X(t)\in B|X(s)=x)
where
P^{\eta}
is a quenched expectation given
\eta

[
Kun94
,
Kun97
,
LJR02
]
. In the annealed sense the stochastic integral
\int_{0}^{t}\eta(s,X(s))ds,
is just a Brownian motion of rate
(\phi*\phi)(0)=\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2},
consequently the annealed path
X
is itself just a Brownian motion of rate
\theta+(\phi*\phi)(0)=1.

This discussion shows that the annealed one-step law
\mu
is a normal distribution of mean
0
and variance
1,
which is enough to verify Item (
3
) of Assumption
1.2
. Let us now move onto the proof of Item (
6
). If
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}:[0,t]\to\mathbb{R}
are two deterministic paths then we have

\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(\int_{0}^{t}\eta(s,\gamma_{1}(s))ds-\int_{0}^{t}\eta(s,\gamma_{2}(s))ds\bigg)^{2}\bigg]=\int_{0}^{t}\psi(\gamma_{1}(s)-\gamma_{2}(s))ds,\;\;\;\text{ where }\;\;\;\psi(x):=2\big(\phi*\phi(0)-\phi*\phi(x)\big).

Using this fact and the SDE interpretation (
6.9
) of (
6.7
), consider two independent particles
X^{1},X^{2}
satisfying that SDE, with the same realization of
\eta
but independent driving Brownian motions
W^{1},W^{2}
. One may verify that the annealed difference
Y(t):=X^{1}(t)-X^{2}(t)
of two particles is itself an Itô diffusion (with deterministic coefficients) satisfying

(6.10)

dY(t)=\big(2-2(\phi*\phi)(Y(t)\big)^{1/2}dB(t),

where
B
is a standard Brownian motion on
\mathbb{R}
. In other words,
\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\bullet)
is just the transition density at time 1 of the Itô diffusion (
6.10
) started from
Y(0)=x.
This is an elliptic diffusion with generator
Lf(a)=\frac{1}{2}(2-2(\phi*\phi)(a))f^{\prime\prime}(a).
Since the diffusion matrix is bounded above and below by positive constants, both bullets in Item (
6
) of Assumption
1.2
will be satisfied by standard existence theory of smooth positive densities for uniformly elliptic diffusions
[
Str88
]
. Note in this case that we have reversibility of
\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}
with respect to
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
, i.e., the generator
L
is self-adjoint on
L^{2}(\pi^{\mathrm{inv}})
as is easily shown using integration by parts.

It remains to verify Item (
5
) of Assumption
1.2
. To do this, we will again verify the stronger assumption (
1.1
). As noted in
[
BW22
, Eq. (1.10)]
or
[
War15
, Eq. (4)]
, the annealed law of
k
independent particles
X^{i}(t)

(1\leq i\leq k)
each satisfying (
6.9
) (with independent driving noises
W^{i}
but the same realization of
\eta
) is an Itô diffusion on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
with generator

L^{(k)}f(\mathbf{a})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{k}\bigg(\theta\mathbf{1}_{\{i=j\}}+(\phi*\phi)(a_{i}-a_{j})\bigg)\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial a_{i}\partial a_{j}}f(\mathbf{a}),

where we recall that
\theta:=1-(\phi*\phi)(0).
Let
\Sigma(\mathbf{a})
be a positive square root of that diffusion matrix, so that the eigenvalues of
\Sigma(\mathbf{a})
are bounded above and below independently of
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
. If
\phi*\phi
is supported on
[-B,B]
then note that
\Sigma(\mathbf{a})=\sigma\cdot\mathrm{Id}
outside of the set
G:=\{\mathbf{a}:\min_{1\leq i<j\leq k}|a_{i}-a_{j}|>B\}.
Then the diffusion with generator
L
has law given by the solution of
d\vec{X}(t)=\Sigma(\vec{X}(t))d\vec{W}(t)
for a standard
k
-dimensional Brownian motion
\vec{W}
. Thus
\boldsymbol{p}^{(k)}(\mathbf{x},\bullet)
is just the transition density at time 1 of this elliptic diffusion started from
\vec{X}(0)=\mathbf{x}
. Since
\Sigma(\mathbf{a})=\sigma\cdot\mathrm{Id}
outside of the set
G
, the diffusion
\vec{X}
simply evolves as standard
k
-dimensional Brownian motion outside
G
. Consequently the total variation bound (
1.1
) can be proved by a simple and explicit coupling construction. A simple union bound using the Gaussian tail decay shows that, starting from
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},...,x_{k})
, the probability of the Brownian sample path
(\mathbf{x}+\sigma\vec{W}(t))_{t\in[0,1]}
hitting
G
is bounded above by
C\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}e^{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}(x_{i}-x_{j})^{2}},
where
C
is a large constant depending on the the support value
B
. If
\vec{X}(0)=\mathbf{x}
, then the diffusion sample path
(\vec{X}(t))_{t\in[0,1]}
agrees with
(\mathbf{x}+\sigma\vec{W}(t))_{t\in[0,1]}
on the event that
(\mathbf{x}+\sigma\vec{W}(t))_{t\in[0,1]}
does
not
hit
G
, which provides the explicit coupling of
\vec{X}(1)
with the
k
-dimensional normal distribution
\mathbf{x}+\sigma\vec{W}(1)
. This shows that the total variation distance in (
1.1
) is bounded above by
\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}e^{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}(x_{i}-x_{j})^{2}}
. Using the brutal bound
\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq k}e^{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}(x_{i}-x_{j})^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{2}k(k-1)e^{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\min_{1\leq i<j\leq k}(x_{i}-x_{j})^{2}}
and noting that
(x_{i}-x_{j})^{2}>-1+|x_{i}-x_{j}|
then implies the desired bound (
1.1
), thus completing the verification of Assumption
1.2
. We remark that this argument would not work if
\phi
is not compactly supported even if it decays rapidly; one would need to be more clever to control the total variation distance in (
1.1
).
∎

Thus Assumption
1.2
has been verified (with
p=1
in Item (
4
)), which means that Theorem
1.4
will hold for this family of stochastic kernels
(K_{r})_{r\geq 0}
. Using (
6.10
), the invariant measure is given explicitly as

\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}(\mathrm{d}a)=\frac{1}{1-\phi*\phi(a)}\mathrm{d}a.

Thanks to this explicit representation of
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
, one may ask if the coefficient
\gamma_{\mathrm{ext}}
in Theorem
1.4
could also be calculated explicitly. The authors of
[
BW22
]
observe (modulo a small typo) that this is indeed possible, and in fact one has

\gamma_{\mathrm{ext}}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{(\phi*\phi)(a)}{1-(\phi*\phi)(a)}\mathrm{d}a.

Notice by the convolution property (
6.8
) that
K_{1}\cdots K_{r}(0,\cdot)
simply agrees with
u(r,\cdot)
where
u
solves (
6.7
). Consequently in this context, the statement of Theorem
1.4
is saying that the field

\mathfrak{H}^{N}(t,\phi)=N^{1/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}D_{N,t,x}\cdot u(Nt,N^{3/4}t+N^{1/2}x)\cdot\phi(x)dx

is converging to the solution of (
1.5
). Of course this leads to a number of other questions, such as what happens if we do not integrate against a test function
\phi
but rather just consider the “un-coarsened” or “pointwise” field
\mathfrak{L}^{N}(t,x):=N^{1/2}u(Nt,N^{3/4}t+N^{1/2}x).
This question remains open, and we expect a nontrivial answer, specifically we expect that there are
additional
nontrivial pointwise fluctuations in the limit that vanish under the test function formulation as in
\mathfrak{H}^{N}
. It is not difficult to show impossibility of convergence in a topology of continuous functions, and we do this for the model considered in
[
DDP23a
]
. But specifically gauging the exact nature of these additional pointwise fluctuations is more difficult and would require new ideas. This type of “local limit” question was explored in
[
CG17
]
in a different regime; perhaps a similar idea could be used to gauge these pointwise fluctuations.

A related question is that if we define the rescaled noise field
\eta^{N}(t,x):=N^{3/4}\eta(Nt,N^{3/4}t+N^{1/2}x)
, and we take a
joint
limit point of
(\eta^{N},\mathfrak{H}^{N})
, say
(\xi,\mathcal{U})
, then is it true that the space-time white noise
\xi
is simply the driving noise of
\mathcal{U}
? This question was answered recently in
[
DDP23a
, Theorem 1.3]
for a related model, and the answer is that
\xi
will
not
be the driving noise of
\mathcal{U}
. Rather the driving noise of
\mathcal{U}
will consist of
\xi

plus another independent space-time white noise
. This is because, as pointed out in
[
BW22
]
, the rescaled field
v_{N}(t,x):=N^{1/2}D_{N,t,x}u(Nt,N^{3/4}t+N^{1/2}x)
satisfies the Itô SPDE given by

\partial_{t}v(t,x)=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2}v(t,x)+v(t,x)\eta^{N}(t,x)+N^{-1/4}\partial_{x}\big(v(t,x)\eta^{N}(t,x)\big),\;\;\;\;\;\;\;v(0,x)=\delta_{0}(x).

Interestingly the latter SPDE is no longer “locally subcritical” in the sense of regularity structures
[
Hai14
]
, but it is instead critical. Nonetheless Theorem
1.4
clearly indicates that there is an explicit SPDE limit for
v_{N}
, and the final term causes the creation of additional multiplicative noise in the SPDE. A recent result of Hairer
[
Hai24
]
on variance blowup of the tree diagrams may be relevant in describing more explicitly where the extra noise comes from.

This phenomenon of “creating extra noise in the limit” is not explored in the generalized context of the present work, as it is unclear to us how to define the prelimiting analogue of the driving noise
\eta^{N}
for the generalized model considered in Assumption
1.2
.

6.4.
