Sticky Brownian motion

Here we discuss a continuum model of random walks in a random environment that has nice exact solvability properties that were explored in
[
BW21
,
BR20
,
BLD20
]
. We already prove KPZ convergence for this model in
[
DDP23a
]
, but we now discuss how to recover that result in the present context.

In order to define the random
kernels for this model, we first need to introduce the concept of
continuum stochastic flows of kernels
. For
s\leq t
, a random probability kernel, denoted
L_{s,t}(x,A)
, is a measurable function defined on some underlying probability space
\Omega
, such that it defines a probability measure on
\mathbb{R}
for each
(x,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}\times\Omega
.
K_{s,t}(x,A)
is interpreted as the
random probability to arrive in
A
at time
t
starting at
x
at time
s
.

A family of random probability kernels
(L_{s,t})_{s\leq t}
on
\mathbb{R}
is called a (continuum) stochastic flow of kernels if

(a)

For any
s\leq t\leq u
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
, almost surely
K_{s,s}(x,A)=\delta_{x}(A)
, and

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}L_{t,u}(y,A)L_{s,t}(x,dy)=L_{s,u}(x,A)

for all
A
in the Borel
\sigma
-algebra of
\mathbb{R}
.

(b)

For any
t_{1}\leq t_{2}\leq\cdots\leq t_{k}
, the
(L_{t_{i},t_{i+1}})_{i=1}^{k-1}
are independent.

(c)

For any
s\leq u
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
L_{s,u}
and
L_{s+t,u+t}
have the same finite dimensional distributions.

For the continuum stochastic flow that we consider in this section, one can ensure that the random set of probability
1
on which
(a)
holds is independent of
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
s\leq t\leq u
. This allows us to interpret
(L_{s,t})_{s\leq t}
as bona fide transition
kernels of a random motion in a continuum random environment (using e.g. Kolmogorov extension theorem). The annealed law of such a motion is called the
1
-point motion associated to
(L_{s,t})_{s\leq t}
. More generally, the
k
-point motion of a continuum stochastic flow of kernels is defined as the
\mathbb{R}^{k}
valued stochastic process
\mathbf{X}=(X^{1},\ldots,X^{k})
with transition probabilities given by

\displaystyle P_{t}(\vec{x},d\vec{y})=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}L_{0,t}(x_{i},dy_{i})\right].

We will be interested in a particular random motion in a continuum random environment originating from the
Howitt-Warren
flow of kernels. Its corresponding
k
-point motion solves a well-posed martingale problem that was first studied by Howitt and Warren in
[
HW09
]
. Below, we introduce the
k
-point motion by stating the martingale problem as formulated in
[
SSS14
]
.

Let
\nu
be a finite and nonnegative measure on
[0,1]
. We say an
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-valued process
\mathbf{X}_{t}=(X_{t}^{1},\ldots,X_{t}^{k})
solves the Howitt-Warren martingale problem with characteristic measure
\nu
if
\mathbf{X}
is a continuous, square-integrable martingale with the covariance process between
X^{i}
and
X^{j}
given by

\displaystyle\langle X^{i},X^{j}\rangle_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{s}^{i}=X_{s}^{j}\}}ds,

and furthermore it satisfies the following condition: consider any nonempty
\Delta\subset\{1,2,\ldots,k\}
. For
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, let

\displaystyle f_{\Delta}(\mathbf{x}):=\max\{x_{i}:i\in\Delta\},\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad g_{\Delta}(\mathbf{x}):=\big|\{i\in\Delta\mid x_{i}=f_{\Delta}(\mathbf{x})\}\big|.

Then the process

f_{\Delta}(\mathbf{X}_{t})-\int_{0}^{t}\beta_{+}\big(g_{\Delta}(\mathbf{X}_{s})\big)ds
is a martingale with respect to the filtration generated by
\mathbf{X}
, where
\beta_{+}(1):=0
and

\displaystyle\beta_{+}(m):={\frac{1}{2}}\int\sum_{k=0}^{m-2}(1-y)^{k}\nu(dy),\quad m\geq 2.

Howitt and Warren
[
HW09
]
proved existence and uniqueness in law for this martingale problem. From the covariation formula above, we see that each
X^{i}
is marginally a Brownian motion. The
k
particles
X^{i}
can thus be interpreted as Brownian motions evolving independently of each other when apart, but when they meet there is some “stickiness” or tendency to move as a single particle, and the intersection set will have positive measure (albeit being nowhere dense). Consequently the process
\mathbf{X}
is referred to as
k
-point sticky Brownian motion with characteristic measure
\nu
in the literature.

By a remarkable result of Le Jan and Raimond
[
LJR04a
, Theorem 2.1]
, any consistent family of Feller processes can be viewed as a
k
-point motion of some stochastic flow of kernels, unique in finite-dimensional distributions. Thus, in particular, the solution of the Howitt-Warren martingale problem can be viewed as the
k
-point motion of some stochastic flow of kernels. We call this stochastic flow of kernels the Howitt-Warren flow. In other words, these kernels are not explicitly specified in terms of some simpler object, but rather we are using the abstract result of
[
LJR04a
]
to say that there
is
a unique family of stochastic kernels whose
k
-point motion is precisely
k
-point sticky Brownian motion for every
k\in\mathbb{N}.

We refer to
[
SSS09
,
SSS14
,
SSS17
]
for a more concrete description in terms of the Brownian web and net.

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
L_{s,t}(x,\mathrm{d}y)
denote the Howitt-Warren flow, with any choice of finite and nonzero characteristic measure
\nu
, and let us define our kernels

K_{r}(x,\mathrm{d}y):=L_{r,r+1}(x,\mathrm{d}y).

Then Assumption
1.2
holds true for this family of kernels.

Proof.

We note that the independence and stationarity in space-time is clear from the definitions. The fact that
\mu
has exponential moments is clear from the fact that the
1
-point motion is just Brownian motion, hence
\mu
is Normal
(0,1)
. The verification of Item (
5
) can be done by noting that the
k
-point motion behaves as Brownian motion away from the boundary of the Weyl chamber
\bigcup_{1\leq i<j\leq k}\{a_{i}=a_{j}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}
, consequently the total variation distance in (
1.1
) can be controlled using a similar explicit coupling construction that was used for the transport SPDE models of the previous subsection.

It remains to verify Item (
6
) of Assumption
1.2
. To do this, we note that the annealed difference process behaves as
Brownian motion sticky at zero.
This is a process with pregenerator
Lf(a)=\frac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(a)
but with domain given by only those functions, smooth away from
0
, satisfying the relation
f^{\prime}(0+)-f^{\prime}(0-)=\frac{1}{2\nu[0,1]}f^{\prime\prime}(0\pm)
, see
[
EP14
]
. Alternatively it may be described as the unique-in-law process
(Y(t))_{t\geq 0}
such that all three of the processes

Y(t),\;\;\;\;Y(t)^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\{Y_{s}\neq 0\}}ds,\;\;\;\;|Y(t)|-\frac{1}{2\nu[0,1]}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\{Y_{s}=0\}}ds

are continuous martingales. Since the characteristic measure
\nu
is assumed to be nonzero, the result of
[
BW21
, Theorem 1.2]
implies that for every starting point
x\in I
, this Markov process has an absolutely continuous (and everywhere positive) part to its time
t=1
transition kernel
\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}(x,\bullet)
, thus the irreducibility of Item (
6
) in Assumption
1.2
is satisfied. The total variation continuity can also be deduced from the same result in
[
BW21
]
, noting that both the singular part at the origin and the absolutely continuous part appearing in the formula are continuous as a function of the starting value, with the absolutely continuous part decaying like a Gaussian. ∎

Thus Assumption
1.2
has been verified (with
p=1
in Item (
4
)), which means that Theorem
1.4
will hold for this family of stochastic kernels
(K_{r})_{r\geq 0}
. We remark that the invariant measure
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
is explicit in this case, given by Lebesgue measure plus a Dirac mass of weight
\frac{1}{2\nu[0,1]}
at the origin (and
\boldsymbol{p}_{\mathbf{dif}}
is reversible with respect to
\pi^{\mathrm{inv}}
). Thus, as we proved in
[
DDP23b
]
, the coefficient
\gamma_{\mathrm{ext}}
is explicitly computable in this case and satisfies
\gamma_{\mathrm{ext}}^{2}=\frac{1}{2\nu[0,1]}.
